The installed **Pstack Text Trait Unslop** skill uses an indentation-based pseudo-programming language. Its purpose is to detect AI writing patterns and rewrite prose without changing the original meaning.

Below are actual excerpts from the skill, preserving its syntax and structure.

## 1. Language definition

## 2. Named lists and rules

The skill uses named collections of rules. Each item can have a stable numeric identifier, a pattern to detect, and an instruction for correcting it.

## 3. Procedural instructions

This is how the skill defines the order of operations.

## 4. Conditional logic

The language supports conditions, iteration, and named-list evaluation.

## 5. Completion criteria

## Language characteristics

The language has a small vocabulary:

| Construct | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `DIRECTIVE` | Mandatory instruction |
| `WHEN` | Trigger or procedural stage |
| `IF` / `ELSE` | Conditional execution |
| `FOR EACH` | Iteration |
| `ASSESS` | Evaluate a named checklist |
| `REPEAT UNTIL` | Conditional repetition |
| `STOP` | End a procedure or branch |
| `REMARK` | Non-executable metadata |
| `name (sentiment):` | Named collection of rules |

The interesting design choice is that **the procedural structure is explicit, but the operations are expressed in natural language**.

For example, `ASSESS preservation checks` refers to a named collection rather than duplicating four validation instructions throughout the skill.

It also separates the rule catalog from the execution procedure, making it possible to add new rules without rewriting the entire control flow.

These examples represent the actual installed skill, not a proposed redesign.

ChatGPTFri, Oct 23, 11:39 PM
Reasoning / progress:
I’ll use the five description styles from the PowerShell helper as a concrete test case, then compare a few DSL designs. I’ll focus on keeping prompts and output text easy to write while making choices, conditions, and reusable templates obvious at a glance.

I think the most promising direction is a **guided text-template DSL**, designed specifically for turning structured answers into finished text.

It would combine three ideas from what we've explored:

1. The PowerShell project's interactive wizard, with questions, hints, examples, and selectable description styles.
2. The Pstack skill's readable, uppercase keywords and indentation-based structure.
3. A text-template engine that supports variables, conditions, reusable components, and different output formats.

The central idea is that **writing the template should feel almost like writing the finished text**, with a small amount of additional syntax to describe how the user supplies information.

I would avoid creating a general-purpose programming language. Instead, I would design a small language with three primary responsibilities: defining the questions, controlling the conversation, and constructing the output.

The syntax should be simple enough that someone unfamiliar with the language can look at a template and understand what it does.

## 1. Proposed syntax: English-like, indentation-based

Here is what I would want an actual template file to look like.

This is close to how the original PowerShell script works, but it removes the questions and text-generation logic from the application code.

Notice a few properties.

**There are no strings requiring quotation marks.** Everything after `TITLE`, `QUESTION`, `HINT`, and similar keywords is ordinary text.

**Indentation determines structure.** You do not need curly braces, semicolons, or explicit closing statements.

**Output text is treated as literal text.** Only `{{variable}}` expressions receive special treatment inside `OUTPUT`.

**Each input has a stable identifier.** The identifiers `type`, `action`, and `benefit` connect questions to the finished text. The visible question can change without affecting the template's logic.

The `EXAMPLE` at template level demonstrates the final result. The `EXAMPLE` inside each `ASK` demonstrates what a valid answer might look like.

This distinction matters because your PowerShell experience demonstrated that examples are useful at both levels.

## 2. Template selection as a first-class feature

Rather than building a separate wizard for the five description styles, I would introduce a `CATALOG` construct.

The runtime would discover each template's title, description, and example, then construct the selection interface automatically.

An illustrative terminal interface:

<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="lg" gap={2}>
<row align="center" justify="between">
<text color="default" weight="medium">PROJECT DESCRIPTION</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Choose a template</text>
</row>
{@body const [selected,setSelected] = DIL.useState("3")}
<box gap={2}>
{#each [
{id:"1",name:"Purpose + function",desc:"What it is, what it does, and why it is useful.",example:"A browser-based tool that formats and validates JSON to make structured data easier to inspect."},
{id:"2",name:"Problem + solution",desc:"Who needs it, the problem, and how it is solved.",example:"Helps developers inspect messy JSON by validating its syntax and formatting the result."},
{id:"3",name:"Input + processing + output",desc:"What goes in, what happens, and what comes out.",example:"Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version."},
{id:"4",name:"Features + technology",desc:"The implementation stack and notable capabilities.",example:"A browser-based JSON utility built with vanilla JavaScript, featuring syntax validation and readable formatting."},
{id:"5",name:"Structured project profile",desc:"Audience, purpose, workflow, and distinguishing details.",example:"A browser-based utility for inspecting JSON data, providing syntax validation and readable formatting through a lightweight interface."}
] as item}
<pressable key={item.id} background={selected===item.id?"surface-tertiary":undefined} border={{size:1,color:selected===item.id?"strong":"subtle"}} radius="md" padding={2} gap={1} onClick={()=>setSelected(item.id)}>
<row align="center" gap={2}>
<icon name={selected===item.id?"circle-dot":"circle"} color={selected===item.id?"default":"tertiary"}/>
<text weight="medium" size="sm">{item.id}. {item.name}</text>
</row>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">{item.desc}</text>
{#if selected===item.id}
<text size="sm" color="default">Example: {item.example}</text>
{/if}
</pressable>
{/each}
</box>
<button block onClick={()=>GenUI.issueNewTurn("Let's explore the DSL details for the "+({1:"Purpose + function",2:"Problem + solution",3:"Input + processing + output",4:"Features + technology",5:"Structured project profile"}[selected])+" description template, especially its guided questions and output.")}>Explore selected template</button>
<caption>Interactive concept for the future template runner.</caption>
</box>

Importantly, the DSL would describe the catalog, while the CLI or web application decides how it is displayed.

The catalog itself would not need to know anything about terminal colors, web forms, or buttons.

## 3. Another example: Input + processing + output

Here is the third description style from our PowerShell script.

The runtime would display one question at a time, collect its answers, and then render the `OUTPUT` block.

The same file could run in PowerShell, a JavaScript terminal application, or a browser UI without changing its contents.

## 4. Make simple templates extremely short

I would also support a much simpler form.

This should be a valid, complete template.

The engine can automatically recognize `name`, `topic`, and `date` as required inputs and ask for their values.

Later, if you want more guidance, you can add explicit `ASK` declarations without changing the output.

This is an important ergonomic principle: **simple templates should require almost no configuration, while complex templates should have enough structure to guide the user.**

## 5. Expressing conditions and decisions

Now we can borrow more from the Pstack skill language.

Imagine a template for replying to a software bug report.

Here, `CHOOSE` introduces a choice field, and `WHEN` controls which output is generated.

I like this approach because it avoids embedding programming expressions inside the text itself. All the logic is visible as English-like statements.

We could eventually introduce `IF`, `ELSE`, `FOR EACH`, `USE`, and `DEFAULT`, but I would be selective.

One useful distinction is between conditions that determine *whether a question is asked* and conditions that determine *what text is produced*. The same `WHEN` construct can support both.

These conditions are easy to understand without knowing a separate expression language.

For validation, we could add declarations such as `REQUIRED`, `OPTIONAL`, `TYPE DATE`, `MULTILINE`, and `DEFAULT`. That should cover many practical templates before we need complex expressions.

## 6. The most important language design rule

I would establish two clearly separated parsing modes.

**Instruction mode** recognizes keywords such as `TEMPLATE`, `ASK`, `CHOOSE`, `WHEN`, and `OUTPUT`. Indentation determines their scope.

**Text mode** begins inside a text-producing block, such as `OUTPUT`. Everything there is literal text except `{{variable}}` interpolation.

For example:

The `IF` here is ordinary English, not an instruction. Colons, quotes, HTML, Markdown, and other punctuation can be written naturally.

This avoids a major source of frustration in template languages: requiring users to escape ordinary content because it accidentally resembles programming syntax.

For more advanced conditional output, I would initially keep conditions outside literal `OUTPUT` blocks, rather than introducing another embedded control syntax. A single documented escape mechanism for literal `{{` would handle the rare interpolation conflict.

## 7. How I would build the runtime

Given your JavaScript background and your desire to build a lexer and parser, TypeScript would be a suitable implementation language.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Template source (.tpl)**
<text size="xs" color="secondary">Human-readable DSL</text>
</box>

<text size="xs" color="secondary">Collect answers, evaluate rules, render text</text>

<grid columns={3} gap={2}>

<grid-item>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} gap={1} align="center">
<icon name={item.icon} color="secondary"/>
<text weight="medium" size="sm">{item.a}</text>
<text color="secondary" textAlign="center" size="2xs">{item.b}</text>
</box>
</grid-item>
{/each}
</grid>
</box>

The lexer could be line-oriented, producing indentation tokens, keywords, identifiers, and raw text blocks. The parser would build an AST, and a validation pass would catch undefined fields, duplicate identifiers, and invalid template references before execution.

The template engine should not know how to manipulate PowerShell files, inject text into a browser, or write to Git. Those belong in adapters built around the core engine.

This also makes the language portable.

## 8. About Espanso and GitHub templates

Espanso is relevant because it already supports text triggers, variables, forms, and choice dialogs. <Cite refs={["turn742387search0","turn742387search2"]}/> Rather than immediately rebuilding all of its global text-expansion functionality, your application could initially work alongside it.

I would develop this in stages. First, the DSL engine and interactive terminal runner. Next, a web application for editing templates, testing them, and copying the generated text. Then GitHub-based template libraries, followed by browser-extension or desktop text insertion.

Downloaded templates should initially be declarative and sandboxed, without arbitrary shell commands or JavaScript execution. That makes it practical to share templates without trusting their authors with local execution privileges.

## 9. One alternative worth considering: Output-first syntax

There is one other design that I think deserves serious consideration.

Instead of requiring questions to be declared before output, we could make the output itself the starting point.

Here `GUIDE` adds guidance to a variable already defined by its use in the output. The engine would discover the variables automatically, even if they had no `GUIDE` blocks.

I find this approach particularly attractive because the main artifact, the generated text, appears first. The guidance is secondary and can be added incrementally.

It also feels more like authoring a template than programming a wizard.

My current preference would be to combine the two approaches: use `TEMPLATE`, `OUTPUT`, and interpolation as the minimal language; allow optional `ASK` or `GUIDE` definitions; then introduce `CHOOSE`, `WHEN`, and `CATALOG` for more advanced workflows.

The biggest open design question is whether this language should be **primarily output-first**, where prompts are inferred and enriched with guidance, or **primarily conversation-first**, where the author deliberately defines a sequence of questions. Both can coexist, but choosing one as the default will strongly influence how natural the language feels.

YouFri, Oct 23, 11:43 PM

Let's explore the DSL details for the Input + processing + output description template, especially its guided questions and output.

ChatGPTFri, Oct 23, 11:43 PM

For the **Input + processing + output** template, I would design the DSL around three guided questions and one output definition. The important part is that each question should explain not only *what information to provide*, but also *how to phrase the answer* so that it fits naturally into the generated sentence.

## 1. Proposed template

This is relatively small, yet it describes the entire interactive workflow. There is no application-specific code, JSON, YAML, or external configuration.

## 2. How the guided questions would work

I would make the runtime present one question at a time, with a visible example and an explanation of the expected grammatical form.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
{@body const steps = [{id:"input",q:"What does the user provide?",hint:"Describe the original data, file, or information. Use a short noun phrase, not a full sentence.",example:"pasted JSON",value:"pasted JSON"},{id:"processing",q:"What happens to the input?",hint:"Describe the main operation in present tense. Start with a verb that fits after the input.",example:"checks it for syntax errors",value:"checks it for syntax errors"},{id:"result",q:"What does the application produce?",hint:"Describe the final output or observable result. Use a noun phrase, not a full sentence.",example:"a readable formatted version",value:"a readable formatted version"}]}
{@body const [values,setValues] = DIL.useState(steps.map(x=>x.value))}
{@body const [step,setStep] = DIL.useState(0)}
{@body const current = steps[step]}
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text weight="medium">Guided description wizard</text>
<text size="xs" color="secondary">Question {step+1} of 3</text>
</row>
<row gap={1}>
{#each steps as s,i}
<box key={s.id} background={i<=step?"default":"surface-tertiary"} height="4px" flex="1" radius="full"/>
{/each}
</row>
<box gap={2}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs" weight="medium">{current.id.toUpperCase()}</text>
<title size="lg">{current.q}</title>
<text color="secondary" size="sm">{current.hint}</text>
<row align="center" gap={2}>
<icon name="lightbulb" color="secondary"/>
<text color="secondary" size="sm">Example: {current.example}</text>
</row>
<textarea rows={2} value={values[step]} onChange={v=>setValues(prev=>prev.map((x,i)=>i===step?v:x))}/>
</box>
<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md" gap={1}>
<text weight="medium" size="xs" color="secondary">LIVE OUTPUT PREVIEW</text>
<text>{`Takes ${values[0]||"..."}, ${values[1]||"..."}, and produces ${values[2]||"..."}.`}</text>
</box>
<row justify="between">
<button color="secondary" variant="outline" disabled={step===0} onClick={()=>setStep(x=>Math.max(0,x-1))}>Back</button>
<button onClick={()=>step<2?setStep(x=>x+1):GenUI.copy(`Takes ${values[0]}, ${values[1]}, and produces ${values[2]}.`)}>{step===2?"Copy result":"Next question"} <icon name={step===2?"copy":"arrow-right"} size="sm"/></button>
</row>
<caption>Interactive illustration of the proposed runtime, not an implemented DSL interpreter.</caption>
</box>

The live preview is important. It lets you notice awkward wording immediately rather than discovering it after answering every question.

## 3. Output definitions should be reusable

I would allow a template to define multiple output formats using the same answers.

For example:

The runtime would ask the questions once, then allow the user to select a rendering format. All three outputs would use the same collected values.

This means the same template can generate a short description for `index.html`, a section for `README.md`, or a structured note without asking the questions again.

I would make `OUTPUT` without a name equivalent to `OUTPUT default`.

## 4. Consider grammatical guidance as a language feature

One of the problems with text-template engines is that they concatenate correct values into grammatically incorrect sentences.

For example:

The output would become:

> Takes a JSON document, the application validates its syntax, and produces the result is formatted JSON.

The individual responses are understandable, but the sentence is not.

We could address this with a small `FORM` declaration.

Initially, `FORM` would be guidance metadata, not a promise of automatic grammatical validation. The runtime could display it as a hint and optionally use it for future validation features.

That avoids introducing an NLP dependency into a simple template engine.

## 5. Optional answers and conditional text

Some projects might produce several outputs. Others have no obvious input.

For such cases, the language should support optional questions and conditional output.

For instance:

This introduces a design decision: should `WHEN` be valid within `OUTPUT` blocks?

There are two reasonable approaches.

<box gap={2}>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} gap={2}>
**Approach A: Separate logic and text**

```text
WHEN export IS PROVIDED
OUTPUT export-note
It also supports exporting {{export}}.
```

Logic remains visually distinct, and text blocks stay completely literal.
</box>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} gap={2}>
**Approach B: Embedded conditional text**

```text
OUTPUT
Takes {{input}} and produces {{result}}.

WHEN export IS PROVIDED
It also supports exporting {{export}}.
```

More convenient for complex documents, but indentation now has two meanings inside text blocks.
</box>
</box>

I prefer **Approach A for the initial language** because its parsing rules are simpler and it protects the principle that content inside `OUTPUT` is ordinary text. We could later introduce explicit conditional fragments without making every `WHEN` in the generated text ambiguous.

## 6. A useful shorthand for simple templates

We should not force the author to declare `ASK` blocks when examples and guidance are unnecessary.

This should be valid too:

The interpreter can discover the three variables and create basic prompts automatically.

You could start with this minimal template and later add `ASK` declarations to improve the guided experience.

## My proposed core language

At this point, I would keep the initial language vocabulary small: `TEMPLATE`, `TITLE`, `ABOUT`, `EXAMPLE`, `ASK`, `QUESTION`, `HINT`, `FORM`, `REQUIRED`, `OPTIONAL`, `OUTPUT`, and `REVIEW`.

Then add `CHOOSE`, `WHEN`, and `USE` when we start working with branching and template composition.

One design principle I would establish now is that **the author's template describes the interaction and the output, but not the UI**. The CLI, web editor, and eventual text-expansion application can each present the same template differently.

The next interesting question is whether `ASK` should support *several examples with explanations*, rather than just one. That might be particularly useful for someone who knows what a project does but is unsure how to phrase it.

Reasoning / progress:
I’ll consolidate the two earlier designs into one specification, with `$` variables, colon-delimited statements, and `ASK REQUIRED` / `ASK OPTIONAL`. I’ll also define explicit rules for block boundaries, indentation, and trailing newlines so the syntax is predictable for both authors and the parser.

# Guided Template Language (GTL)
<text color="secondary" weight="medium" size="sm">Language specification | Draft 0.1 | Design and brainstorming</text>

GTL is a domain-specific scripting language for creating guided text templates. It combines ordinary text, interactive questions, examples, conditional logic, reusable text fragments, and output generation.

The goal is to let people define templates without writing JavaScript, PowerShell, JSON, or YAML. A template should be readable even by someone who has never seen the language before.

This specification consolidates our earlier designs and incorporates the new syntax decisions: `$`-prefixed variables, `{{...}}` interpolation, uppercase structural keywords, colons for declarations and blocks, and indentation-based scoping.

Everything below is a proposed language contract, not an implemented interpreter.

---

### A00. Language philosophy

GTL should prioritize five characteristics.

1. **Readable at first sight.** `ASK`, `WHEN`, `ELSE`, and `FOR EACH` should communicate their purpose without documentation.
2. **Minimal punctuation.** Colons separate declarations from values or introduce blocks. Indentation determines scope. Standalone actions do not require punctuation.
3. **Literal text by default.** Writing HTML, Markdown, email messages, source code, and other text should require almost no escaping.
4. **Progressive complexity.** A three-line template should be valid. Authors can add guided questions, choices, and conditions only when necessary.
5. **Independent of the interface.** The same template should work in a terminal wizard, a browser, a desktop application, or a text-expansion tool.

A template is primarily a description of an interaction and its resulting text. It is not a general-purpose program.

### B00. Core syntax

Here is the complete proposed Input + processing + output template with the new syntax.

I recommend adopting this as the reference example against which we test future syntax changes.

#### B00.1. Statement conventions

| Syntax | Interpretation |
|---|---|
| `TEMPLATE: name` | Declare a template |
| `TITLE: text` | Assign a human-readable title |
| `ABOUT:` | Start a descriptive text block |
| `ASK REQUIRED $name QUESTION: text` | Declare a required guided input |
| `ASK OPTIONAL $name QUESTION: text` | Declare an optional guided input |
| `HINT: text` | Provide guidance |
| `EXAMPLE: text` | Provide an example |
| `OUTPUT:` | Define the default output |
| `OUTPUT $summary:` | Define a named output |
| `REVIEW:` | Declare review behavior |
| `ALLOW EDIT` | Enable an action within a block |
| `WHEN $status IS fixed:` | Introduce a conditional block |
| `ELSE:` | Introduce the alternative branch |
| `FOR EACH $item IN $items:` | Introduce iteration |

The guiding convention is that **a colon introduces a value or block, while a standalone action has no colon**.

For instance, `ALLOW EDIT` does not need a colon because it does not introduce further content.

`ASK REQUIRED $input QUESTION:` is a slightly specialized declaration. It introduces an input and its human-readable question in one line. Additional properties belong to its indented block.

I prefer this to:

The longer form provides no meaningful benefit for this domain.

#### B00.2. Capitalization

Structural keywords are uppercase and case-sensitive.

`WHEN`, `IS`, and `ELSE` are language keywords. `fixed` is an ordinary value.

Template names, variable names, and option identifiers are case-sensitive. Human-readable text retains its original capitalization.

Importantly, uppercase words inside literal text blocks are not interpreted as instructions.

### C00. Variables and interpolation

Every variable reference begins with `$`.

I recommend allowing letters, digits, underscores, and hyphens, with the first character after `$` required to be a letter or underscore.

This supports readable identifiers such as `$project-name` without quotation marks.

#### C00.1. Variable interpolation

Within generated text, variables use double braces and retain the dollar sign.

The resulting text could be:

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} gap={1}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs" weight="medium">RENDERED OUTPUT</text>
Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.
</box>

The braces distinguish variable interpolation from ordinary text.

This is useful for shell commands, currencies, Markdown, and JavaScript examples because an ordinary `$` character remains literal.

No escaping is required for `$25`.

#### C00.2. Dynamic values without declared types

I agree with avoiding a statically typed variable system.

The language would support strings, numbers, booleans, lists, objects, and missing values at runtime without requiring declarations such as `string`, `number`, or `boolean`.

The parser recognizes variable references. The runtime determines their actual values.

However, operations still have requirements. For example, `FOR EACH` needs an iterable value. That is a runtime operation check, not static type checking.

UI hints such as `FORM: noun-phrase` or `INPUT: date` would not change the variable's underlying type or enforce a type system.

#### C00.3. Literal interpolation characters

There is one unavoidable escaping case: generating the actual characters `{{` followed by text resembling an interpolation expression.

I propose a single escape sequence:

Rendered result:

Outside this case, backslashes are ordinary characters. We should not introduce a general string-escaping system.

### D00. Indentation, margins, and newlines

This needs precise rules because whitespace is both part of the syntax and potentially part of the generated text.

My recommendation is to distinguish **structural whitespace** from **literal content whitespace**.

#### D00.1. Indentation and block boundaries

Use two spaces per indentation level in examples and formatters. Accept consistent deeper space indentation, but reject tabs used for structural indentation in version 1.

A block begins after its declaration and ends when the parser encounters the next nonblank line at the same or a lower indentation level.

For example:

The first `OUTPUT` ends before `OUTPUT $detailed:`.

No `END`, `ENDIF`, closing brace, or terminator is necessary.

Blank lines never close a block. Only a nonblank line's indentation can do so.

#### D00.2. Default margin stripping

Consider:

The parser removes the common four-space margin from all nonblank lines.

Rendered text:

This preserves intentional relative indentation.

The algorithm is straightforward: find the minimum leading whitespace across nonblank content lines, then remove that amount from every line in the block.

#### D00.3. Default newline behavior

I recommend the following rule:

**Preserve blank lines inside content, but remove blank lines surrounding the content. Do not add a trailing newline automatically.**

Example:

```text
OUTPUT:
First paragraph.

Second paragraph.

OUTPUT $other:
Another result.
```

The first output contains exactly:

There are two newline characters between the paragraphs and zero after the last character.

The two empty lines before `OUTPUT $other:` are treated as visual separation in the source file, not output content.

This addresses the ambiguity you identified. Extra source-level blank lines at the end of a block should not silently change generated text.

#### D00.4. Explicit trailing newlines

When the author actually needs trailing newlines, I recommend a header modifier:

This produces:

Here the quoted representation is illustrative; the output contains actual newline characters.

`TRAILING NEWLINES 2` means exactly two trailing newline characters, regardless of how many blank lines appear before the next statement.

The default is equivalent to `TRAILING NEWLINES 0`.

This is more reliable than `KEEP TRAILING` because the intended number is explicit.

An optional symmetrical `LEADING NEWLINES 2` modifier could be added later.

#### D00.5. Keeping the margin

For preformatted text, introduce `KEEP MARGIN`.

Unlike the default behavior, the four spaces preceding `function` are retained in the output.

I would make this explicit rather than require authors to use special margin characters on every line.

Both modifiers can be combined:

For source formatting, a formatter should treat blank lines as separators while keeping the significant indentation and newline modifiers unchanged.

#### D00.6. Whitespace-processing pipeline

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text weight="medium" size="sm">Raw block lines</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Including source indentation</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text size="sm">Identify block boundaries</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text size="sm">Remove surrounding blank lines</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text size="sm">Strip common margin unless KEEP MARGIN</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text size="sm">Evaluate variable interpolation</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text weight="medium" size="sm">Apply explicit trailing newlines</text>
</box>
</box>

Internally, the engine should normalize line endings to `\n`. A host application can convert them to `\r\n` when exporting files if necessary.

These rules should apply consistently to `OUTPUT`, `TEXT`, and reusable text fragments. For `ABOUT`, `HINT`, and `EXAMPLE`, the runtime can retain internal line breaks but strip surrounding empty lines for presentation.

---

### E00. Guided inputs and question definitions

Questions are a core language feature, not merely metadata attached to placeholders.

Every explicit question should define what the user is being asked and, when appropriate, provide enough guidance to help them formulate an answer.

#### E00.1. Required input

`ASK REQUIRED` means the runtime must collect a nonempty value before finalizing the template.

The variable `$input` receives that answer.

The question is human-readable text. It is not a variable identifier and does not need to be unique.

#### E00.2. Optional input

An optional input may be skipped. The engine should preserve the distinction between an unanswered optional input and an explicitly provided value when evaluating conditions.

An absent optional value renders as empty text if interpolated directly. However, the editor should warn when an optional value is used in an unconditional sentence, since that can produce awkward output.

#### E00.3. Defaults

A default value should be part of the input declaration.

Defaults are actual proposed values. Examples are illustrations and should never silently become answers.

For a required field, a valid default counts as an answer once the user accepts it.

#### E00.4. Multiple examples with explanations

This is an improvement I would make over the original PowerShell experience.

An author should be able to provide several examples, including an explanation of why each is useful.

I propose that a simple `EXAMPLE: value` and an expanded `EXAMPLE:` with `VALUE:` and `NOTE:` are both valid.

This gives us a way to construct a small, reusable instructional system inside a template.

The CLI can display examples as a numbered set. A web application can present them as expandable examples or selectable suggestions. Choosing an example could populate the corresponding input field without making it mandatory.

#### E00.5. Grammatical guidance

The `FORM` property is descriptive metadata.

It tells the user how to phrase an answer.

For instance, this template:

would produce an awkward sentence if `$processing` contained "the application validates the document."

The question should instead guide the user toward "validates the document."

I would not attempt to automatically enforce grammar in the initial language. Natural language is too flexible for simple hard-coded checks.

The runtime can display the expected form, provide examples, and let the user evaluate the live output.

#### E00.6. Input presentation hints

For more advanced templates, a field can suggest a control type.

Proposed values include `text`, `multiline`, `date`, and `lines`.

`INPUT: lines` produces a collection of entered lines, which can be consumed by `FOR EACH`.

These are interface hints, not statically enforced variable types. The CLI and web application may display different controls for the same input.

### F00. Output definitions and formats

Output is the primary result of a template.

#### F00.1. Default output

A template can define one unnamed default output.

#### F00.2. Multiple named outputs

The runtime collects the three answers once, then renders whichever output format the user chooses.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
{@body const [format,setFormat] = DIL.useState("summary")}
{@body const sample = {summary:"Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.",detailed:"Input: pasted JSON\nProcessing: checks it for syntax errors\nResult: a readable formatted version",markdown:"**Input:** pasted JSON\n\n**Processing:** checks it for syntax errors\n\n**Output:** a readable formatted version"}}
<text weight="medium">Output format preview</text>
<segmented-control block size="md" value={format} onChange={setFormat} options={[{label:"Summary",value:"summary"},{label:"Detailed",value:"detailed"},{label:"Markdown",value:"markdown"}]}/>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} minHeight="100px">
<text preserveWhitespace size="sm">{sample[format]}</text>
</box>
<row justify="end">
<button size="md" color="secondary" variant="outline" onClick={()=>GenUI.copy(sample[format])}><icon name="copy" size="sm"/> Copy output</button>
</row>
</box>

A named output such as `$summary` is an output identifier, not an ordinary question variable. The shared `$` syntax indicates that it is a named value in the template system.

To prevent ambiguity, question variables and output identifiers should not reuse the same name within one template.

When a template has several named outputs but no default, the runner asks the user which one to produce.

#### F00.3. Implicit questions

The language should also allow this:

There are no explicit `ASK` declarations.

The interpreter discovers `$name`, `$topic`, and `$date` in order of first appearance and generates basic questions.

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
<text size="xs" color="secondary" weight="medium">AUTOMATIC QUESTIONS</text>
<text size="sm" preserveWhitespace>{`1. Enter name:
2. Enter topic:
3. Enter date:`}</text>
</box>

I recommend limiting implicit question creation to templates without any explicit `ASK` or `CHOOSE` declarations.

Once an author starts declaring fields explicitly, an undeclared variable should instead produce a validation error. This prevents a typo such as `$proccessing` from silently creating an unwanted question.

#### F00.4. Output selection versus output formatting

A template may have several outputs that represent different formats, not different answers.

This distinction means the runtime should preserve collected values when the user switches between outputs.

For example, generating an HTML description and then switching to Markdown should not restart the interview.

Likewise, editing `$processing` should automatically update every output that depends on it.

### G00. Choices, conditions, and iteration

This is where the DSL becomes a scripting language.

The key design objective is to make control flow obvious while keeping raw text blocks free of programming instructions.

#### G00.1. Choices

The `OPTION` identifier is the actual value assigned to `$status`. The text after the colon is its display label.

For example, selecting "Cannot reproduce" assigns the string `cannot-reproduce`.

This avoids having to escape option labels with spaces.

#### G00.2. Conditional questions

If the user selects `no`, the technology question is not shown.

Changing the answer from `yes` to `no` should deactivate that question in the generated result. The runtime may retain the previous answer in the current editing session, so switching back to `yes` does not force the user to retype it.

However, inactive answers should not be treated as provided during output generation.

#### G00.3. Conditions

The initial conditional vocabulary can be small.

`IS` compares values without implicit conversion. For a choice field, it compares the chosen option identifier.

`IS PROVIDED` means the value exists and is not empty. `false` and numeric zero still count as provided values. An empty text value or empty list does not.

I would use `WHEN` as the canonical conditional keyword in version 1. It reads naturally for both conditional questions and conditional text generation. Adding `IF` as an alias is possible but not necessary.

#### G00.4. Iteration

A future structured output can iterate through the collected lines:

If the user provides three features, the output is:

An omitted `lines` input behaves as an empty collection. Other non-iterable values should produce a clear runtime error rather than being silently converted.

The loop variable `$feature` exists only inside the `FOR EACH` block.

I would deliberately omit indexed loops, mutation, arithmetic loops, and arbitrary expression evaluation from the first version.

### H00. Conditional output and reusable fragments

We previously considered putting `WHEN` directly inside an `OUTPUT` block.

After considering your emphasis on literal text and easy parsing, I would **not** do that.

An `OUTPUT` block should be entirely literal apart from interpolation. A different construct can handle text assembled through conditions.

I propose `COMPOSE`.

#### H00.1. Basic conditional composition

`COMPOSE` is a structured output definition. Like `OUTPUT`, it may be unnamed or named, and may use the same margin and trailing-newline modifiers.

Inside `COMPOSE`, `TEXT:` introduces a literal block. `WHEN` introduces a conditional block. The two remain unambiguous.

`JOIN WITH: blank-line` inserts one empty line between nonempty rendered segments.

The initial separator options could be `none`, `space`, `newline`, and `blank-line`, with `newline` as the default.

#### H00.2. Reusable fragments

For text that is shared among several outputs, introduce `FRAGMENT`.

A fragment behaves like a reusable text block. `USE` inserts its rendered content into the current composition.

Fragments are internal building blocks; named outputs and named compositions are user-selectable results.

This makes it possible to have dozens of internal text fragments without cluttering the output-selection interface.

#### H00.3. Conditional response example

A complete bug-report response might look like this:

The runtime evaluates the choice and includes exactly the matching text.

Each `TEXT` block has the same interpolation and whitespace rules as `OUTPUT`.

For deterministic evaluation, conditions should execute in source order. Every matching independent `WHEN` block can contribute text. An `ELSE` branch belongs to its immediately preceding `WHEN` and executes only when that condition is false.

If a choice is intended to select exactly one branch, its possible values should make those branches mutually exclusive, as in the example above.

---

### I00. Complete project-description template library

The five description styles from the PowerShell helper become five independent GTL templates.

They share a catalog, but each defines its own questions and sentence structure.

#### I00.1. Purpose + function

#### I00.2. Problem + solution

#### I00.3. Input + processing + output

The full guided version appears in section B00.

Its central output is:

For applications that need more than one format, the same template can replace that output with:

All three outputs reuse the answers collected by the same questions.

#### I00.4. Features + technology

#### I00.5. Structured project profile

This is the most comprehensive style. It can produce both a one-sentence description and a longer README overview.

This example demonstrates that GTL does not have to be limited to one-line snippets. The same collected information can generate a short summary, a README paragraph, or eventually a more detailed technical document.

### J00. Template catalogs and selection

A catalog groups related templates and gives the user a guided way to choose among them.

The entries under `PICK TEMPLATE:` are template identifiers, not variables. They do not need `$`.

The runtime resolves these identifiers from the installed template library and reads each template's `TITLE`, `ABOUT`, and top-level `EXAMPLE`.

That information is enough to generate an informative selection interface without separately maintaining descriptions in the catalog.

#### J00.1. Example terminal selection

<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="lg" gap={1}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs" weight="medium">GTL CLI | TEMPLATE CATALOG</text>
<text preserveWhitespace size="xs">{`PROJECT DESCRIPTION
======================================================

Choose how you want to describe your project.

[1] PURPOSE + FUNCTION
What it is, what it does, why it is useful.

Example:
A browser-based tool that formats and validates
JSON to make structured data easier to inspect.

[2] PROBLEM + SOLUTION
Who needs it, the problem, and the solution.

Example:
Helps developers inspect messy JSON by
validating its syntax and formatting the result.

[3] INPUT + PROCESSING + OUTPUT
What goes in, what happens, what comes out.

Example:
Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors,
and produces a readable formatted version.

[4] FEATURES + TECHNOLOGY
Implementation stack and notable capabilities.

Example:
A browser-based JSON utility built with vanilla
JavaScript, featuring syntax validation and
readable formatting.

[5] STRUCTURED PROJECT PROFILE
Audience, purpose, workflow, and implementation.

Example:
A browser-based utility for developers inspecting
JSON files that validates JSON and formats it
for reading, with immediate syntax feedback.

------------------------------------------------------
Select template [1-5]: 3`}</text>
</box>

The important aspect is that the interface is generated from the template descriptions. Authors do not need to duplicate the examples in JavaScript or PowerShell UI code.

### K00. Interactive execution model

After the user selects a template, the engine collects answers, evaluates any conditions, and continuously updates the output preview.

The expected grammatical form is visible, along with the example and a short explanation.

The runtime should not assume that the user remembers what the template's questions mean. This was one of the useful properties of the PowerShell wizard.

#### K00.2. Review and editing

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs" weight="medium">GTL CLI | REVIEW</text>
<text preserveWhitespace size="xs">{`GENERATED DESCRIPTION
======================================================

Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors,
and produces a readable formatted version.

------------------------------------------------------

[1] Accept and copy
[2] Edit input answers
[3] Edit final text
[4] Retry questions
[5] Switch template
[6] Change output format

Select action: _`}</text>
</box>

Editing input answers rerenders the output.

Editing the final text creates an override of the rendered result for the current session. It should not silently change the underlying template or rewrite the original input values.

The `REVIEW:` declaration controls which operations are available. Some operations depend on context: switching templates only makes sense when the template was launched from a catalog, and changing output formats requires multiple outputs.

The terminal may use color and bold typography to distinguish questions, hints, examples, warnings, and generated output. Those are presentation concerns, not language keywords. The interface must remain understandable when color is unavailable.

---

### L00. Lexer, parser, and runtime architecture

I would implement the core language in TypeScript, with no dependency on a particular UI framework.

The architecture would separate source parsing, validation, execution, and presentation.

#### L00.1. Lexer

The lexer should be line-oriented and indentation-aware.

The important token categories are:

| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Declaration keywords | `TEMPLATE`, `CATALOG`, `TITLE`, `ASK` |
| Control keywords | `WHEN`, `ELSE`, `FOR EACH`, `USE` |
| Modifiers | `REQUIRED`, `OPTIONAL`, `KEEP MARGIN` |
| Delimiters | `:`, `{{`, `}}` |
| Variable references | `$input`, `$project-name` |
| Indentation | `INDENT`, `DEDENT` |
| Literal text | Raw lines inside text blocks |

The lexer should not tokenize every word inside literal blocks. Doing so adds work without improving the language.

Instead, it should retain those blocks as raw text segments, except for recognized interpolation expressions.

#### L00.2. Parser

A useful conceptual AST for our central example would be:

I would keep source locations on every AST node so the editor can report useful errors.

For example:

A good diagnostic system will matter more than complicated syntax features.

#### L00.3. Semantic validation

The validator should detect duplicate field identifiers, references to undefined variables, invalid choice defaults, duplicate output identifiers, fragment-reference cycles, unsupported keywords, and malformed indentation.

It should also validate scope. A variable introduced by `FOR EACH` cannot be used after the loop finishes.

The validator should not reject text merely because its wording or grammatical form is unconventional.

#### L00.4. Runtime data model

The runtime should maintain an answer map, a set of active questions, and the currently selected output.

Conceptually:

The answer map is an internal representation. Authors should never need to write JSON to create templates.

The engine evaluates template conditions against the current answers, generates the active question sequence, and renders the selected output.

Changing an answer invalidates the previous render and produces a new one.

The renderer should not mutate the user's answers.

#### L00.5. Runtime conversion rules

Since GTL is dynamically typed, rendering still needs predictable rules.

Strings render directly. Numbers render using a documented invariant representation. Booleans render as `true` or `false`. Missing optional text values render as empty strings.

Lists and objects should not silently become arbitrary text. Authors should use iteration or future explicit formatting operations for those values. Attempting to interpolate an unsupported structured value directly should produce a runtime diagnostic.

This is compatible with a dynamically typed language: the engine checks whether an operation is valid at execution time rather than requiring variable type declarations.

### M00. Grammar and parsing contract

This is a simplified grammar sketch, not yet a complete formal grammar.

Block-ending behavior is determined by indentation, not by the particular keyword that begins the next statement.

#### M00.1. Contextual interpretation

The parser should use its current context to interpret keywords.

For example, `OPTION` is a valid statement inside `CHOOSE`, while `HINT` is a property inside `ASK`.

The line:

has no valid meaning inside an `OUTPUT` text block's instruction grammar, because the entire block is literal text.

Similarly:

should output those two lines literally. It must not activate a conditional.

This is why I would favor a context-sensitive, indentation-aware lexer over a large collection of regular expressions.

#### M00.2. Source formatting

I would define a canonical formatter for `.gtl` files.

It would use two spaces per indentation level, preserve authored literal text, retain blank lines within literal blocks, and normalize spacing between declarations.

The formatter must never change the number of explicit trailing newlines or the meaning of `KEEP MARGIN`.

The parser and formatter should also be able to preserve comments once comment syntax is introduced. I would initially reserve `#` at the beginning of structural lines for comments, while treating it literally inside text blocks.

### N00. Expected behavior and conformance tests

These are representative tests that the first parser and renderer should pass.

| Test | Required behavior |
|---|---|
| `$input` inside ordinary text | Remains literal |
| `{{$input}}` inside output | Interpolates the variable |
| `\{{$input}}` inside output | Produces literal `{{$input}}` |
| `WHEN` inside `OUTPUT` | Treated as text |
| `WHEN` inside `COMPOSE` | Evaluates a condition |
| Extra blank lines before the next declaration | Removed from the preceding output's end |
| Blank lines between paragraphs | Preserved |
| `TRAILING NEWLINES 2` | Produces exactly two final newline characters |
| Default margin behavior | Removes common indentation |
| `KEEP MARGIN` | Retains authored indentation |
| Missing required answer | Prevents final acceptance |
| Missing optional answer | Allowed |
| Unknown variable in an explicitly declared template | Validation error |
| Unknown variable in a minimal implicit-input template | Creates an implicit question |
| Invalid choice default | Validation error |
| Empty optional `lines` input | Iterates zero times |
| Undefined template in a catalog | Resolution error |

The language should have a small executable conformance suite built around source files, example answers, and expected rendered strings.

Whitespace tests should compare exact strings, including newline characters. Visual comparison alone will not catch those errors.

### O00. Integration with the earlier PowerShell workflow

The PowerShell helper that inspired this discussion is a useful first integration target.

The responsibilities should be separated:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center">
**GTL template**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Defines questions and generates the description</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center">
**GTL CLI**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Runs the guided interview and returns the accepted text</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center">
**Add-Project.ps1**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Creates the HTML/RSS entries and updates the project index</text>
</box>
</box>

GTL should not contain special knowledge of `index.html`, RSS, or Git repositories.

The PowerShell helper can consume the generated description through the CLI's standard output or a structured result returned by the runtime API.

This separation would allow you to reuse the same description templates for README files, GitHub project summaries, release notes, and other writing tasks.

For direct integration, I would eventually support noninteractive invocation, where the host supplies initial answers and the runner only asks for missing required values.

### P00. Template libraries, distribution, and text expansion

I would use `.gtl` as the provisional file extension.

A template library could have this structure:

A library manager could install these files from local directories or GitHub repositories.

The initial DSL should remain declarative. It should not execute arbitrary JavaScript, PowerShell, or commands supplied by downloaded templates.

The runtime and host application would handle filesystem access, clipboard operations, browser insertion, or other privileged actions.

For text expansion, this syntax would be sufficient:

A future text-expansion adapter could detect `:reply`, open the guided question interface, render the result, and insert it into the active application with the user's approval.

The same template could also be launched manually from the web editor or terminal.

### Q00. Development plan

I would divide implementation into four stages, without requiring all language features in the first prototype.

| Stage | Scope | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Core language | Lexer, parser, variables, interpolation, `ASK`, `OUTPUT`, whitespace handling | Interactive CLI runner |
| 2. Guided workflows | `CHOOSE`, `WHEN`, `REVIEW`, catalogs, examples, live previews | Complete project-description wizard |
| 3. Composition | `FRAGMENT`, `COMPOSE`, `FOR EACH`, named outputs, formatting controls | Reusable document and message templates |
| 4. Application | Web editor, template library, GitHub distribution, text-expansion integration | General-purpose template management application |

The strongest early test would be to implement all five project-description templates in GTL, reproduce the original PowerShell wizard's behavior, and verify that the source files remain easier to understand than equivalent programmatic definitions.

### R00. Design decisions and remaining questions

Most of the fundamental syntax is now consistent enough to prototype.

| Design question | Proposed decision |
|---|---|
| How are variables identified? | `$name` |
| How are variables interpolated? | `{{$name}}` |
| How are declarations written? | Uppercase keywords with colons |
| How are blocks scoped? | Indentation |
| How are required and optional questions expressed? | `ASK REQUIRED` and `ASK OPTIONAL` |
| Are types mandatory? | No, values are dynamically typed |
| How is question guidance provided? | `HINT`, `EXAMPLE`, `FORM` |
| How are choices expressed? | `CHOOSE` and `OPTION` |
| How are conditions expressed? | `WHEN`, `ELSE` |
| How are raw and conditional outputs distinguished? | `OUTPUT` versus `COMPOSE` |
| How are fragments reused? | `FRAGMENT` and `USE` |
| Are leading/trailing blank lines retained? | No, unless explicitly requested |
| How is indentation preserved? | `KEEP MARGIN` |
| How are trailing newlines expressed? | `TRAILING NEWLINES n` |
| Can ordinary text contain language keywords? | Yes, inside literal text blocks |
| Is the language tied to a particular UI? | No |

Two design choices deserve further experimentation before freezing version 1.

First, whether `COMPOSE` is the best name for structured output assembly. It is clear, but it introduces a second way to generate output. Alternatives include `BUILD` or `RENDER`. The distinction between raw text and structured composition is more important than the exact keyword.

Second, how far we should take natural-language expression syntax. I would initially support only explicit comparisons and presence checks, rather than building arithmetic, function calls, or arbitrary expression evaluation. If real templates eventually need them, we can add narrow operations based on demonstrated requirements.

For the first prototype, my preference is to implement **A00 through F00 and the five description templates before introducing composition**. That gives us a usable, testable language early, with enough real examples to expose weaknesses in its syntax.

# Guided Template Language (GTL)

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Language specification | Draft 0.2 | Consolidated design</text>

This is the complete revised specification, retaining the previous A-R section structure, examples, parsing rules, runtime design, and implementation plan.

The principal change is the simplified `OPTION` syntax:

These display their identifiers exactly as written. A colon introduces an explicit display label:

Both forms can coexist within a `CHOOSE` block. Identifiers with internal hyphens remain supported.

Updated sections are B01, G01, L01, M01, N01, and R01. All other sections retain their previous revision identifiers.

---

## A00. Language philosophy

GTL is a domain-specific scripting language for creating guided text templates. It combines ordinary text, interactive questions, examples, conditional logic, reusable text fragments, and output generation.

It is intended to support project descriptions, README files, messages, bug-report responses, documentation, and reusable text-expansion templates.

The language has five principal design goals.

1. **Readable at first sight.** Keywords such as `ASK`, `WHEN`, `ELSE`, and `FOR EACH` should communicate their purpose without requiring knowledge of formal programming syntax.
2. **Minimal punctuation.** Colons separate declarations from values or introduce blocks. Indentation determines scope. Standalone actions do not require punctuation.
3. **Literal text by default.** Writing HTML, Markdown, email messages, source code, and other text should require almost no escaping.
4. **Progressive complexity.** A three-line template should be valid. Authors add guided questions, choices, and conditions only when needed.
5. **Independent of the interface.** The same template should run in a terminal, browser, desktop application, or text-expansion tool.

A template is primarily a description of an interaction and its resulting text. GTL is not intended to become a general-purpose programming language.

## B01. Core syntax

The following is the reference example for the language.

### B01.1. Complete input + processing + output template

This example illustrates the principal syntax conventions.

Variables begin with `$`, declarations use uppercase keywords, colons introduce values or blocks, and indentation determines scope.

Text after `QUESTION:`, `HINT:`, `EXAMPLE:`, or inside `OUTPUT:` is ordinary language.

### B01.2. Statement conventions

| Syntax | Interpretation |
|---|---|
| `TEMPLATE: name` | Declare a template |
| `TITLE: text` | Assign a human-readable title |
| `ABOUT:` | Start a description block |
| `ASK REQUIRED $name QUESTION: text` | Declare a required input |
| `ASK OPTIONAL $name QUESTION: text` | Declare an optional input |
| `CHOOSE REQUIRED $name QUESTION: text` | Declare a required choice |
| `OPTION key` | Declare an option; display its key verbatim |
| `OPTION key: Label` | Declare an option with a custom display label |
| `HINT: text` | Provide guidance |
| `EXAMPLE: text` | Provide an example |
| `OUTPUT:` | Define the default output |
| `OUTPUT $summary:` | Define a named output |
| `REVIEW:` | Declare review behavior |
| `ALLOW EDIT` | Enable a standalone action |
| `WHEN $status IS fixed:` | Introduce a conditional block |
| `ELSE:` | Introduce an alternative branch |
| `FOR EACH $item IN $items:` | Introduce iteration |

The primary convention is that a colon introduces a value or block, while a standalone instruction does not require punctuation.

`OPTION fixed` is therefore valid because the option identifier is sufficient to define both the stored value and its visible label.

`OPTION fixed: Fixed` introduces a separate display label.

### B01.3. Capitalization

Structural keywords are uppercase and case-sensitive.

`WHEN` and `IS` are language keywords. `fixed` is an ordinary value.

Variable names, template identifiers, and option identifiers are case-sensitive.

Human-readable text retains its original capitalization.

Uppercase words inside literal text blocks do not become instructions.

## C00. Variables and interpolation

Every variable reference begins with `$`.

Variable identifiers may contain letters, digits, underscores, and hyphens. The first character after `$` must be a letter or underscore.

Internal hyphens are supported intentionally.

### C00.1. Variable interpolation

Inside generated text, variables use double braces while retaining the dollar sign.

Rendered output:

<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md">
Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.
</box>

The braces distinguish interpolation from ordinary text.

For example:

`$25` remains literal.

### C00.2. Dynamic values without declared types

GTL does not use a static type system.

Runtime values may be strings, numbers, booleans, lists, objects, or missing values.

Authors do not need declarations such as:

The parser recognizes variable references. The runtime determines their values.

Individual operations still have requirements. For example, `FOR EACH` expects an iterable value. That requirement is checked when the operation executes.

Properties such as `FORM: noun-phrase` and `INPUT: date` are descriptive guidance or input presentation hints. They do not establish static types.

### C00.3. Literal interpolation characters

A backslash before an interpolation opener prevents interpolation.

Rendered output:

Outside recognized interpolation escapes, backslashes remain ordinary characters.

A general-purpose string-escaping system is not required.

## D00. Indentation, margins, and newlines

Whitespace is both part of the language syntax and part of the text being generated.

GTL distinguishes structural whitespace from literal content whitespace.

### D00.1. Indentation and block boundaries

The recommended indentation is two spaces per level.

The parser may accept deeper consistent space indentation. Tabs are not supported for structural indentation in version 1.

A block begins after its declaration and ends at the next nonblank line whose indentation is equal to or less than the declaration's indentation.

No `END`, `ENDIF`, closing brace, or terminator is necessary.

Blank lines do not close blocks.

Only a nonblank line's indentation determines a block boundary.

### D00.2. Default margin stripping

Consider:

The engine determines the common indentation of nonblank content lines and removes it.

Rendered output:

Intentional relative indentation remains intact.

### D00.3. Default newline behavior

The proposed default rule is:

**Preserve blank lines inside text, remove blank lines surrounding the text, and do not automatically append a trailing newline.**

Example:

```text
OUTPUT:
First paragraph.

Second paragraph.

OUTPUT $other:
Another result.
```

The first output is exactly:

There are two newline characters between the paragraphs and zero after the final period.

The two empty source lines before `OUTPUT $other:` are visual separation, not additional generated content.

### D00.4. Explicit trailing newlines

Authors can request an exact number of trailing newline characters.

The generated string ends with exactly two `\n` characters:

The default is equivalent to:

An optional `LEADING NEWLINES 2` modifier may be introduced later.

### D00.5. Keeping indentation

The `KEEP MARGIN` modifier preserves authored indentation.

Unlike default margin stripping, the four spaces preceding `function` are retained.

Modifiers can be combined:

### D00.6. Whitespace-processing sequence

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
{#each [
"Read raw block lines",
"Identify block boundaries",
"Remove surrounding blank lines",
"Strip common margin unless KEEP MARGIN",
"Evaluate variable interpolation",
"Apply explicit trailing newlines"
] as item,i}
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text size="sm" weight={i===0||i===5?"medium":"normal"}>{item}</text>
</box>
{#if i<5}
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
{/if}
{/each}
</box>

Internally, line endings should be normalized to `\n`.

Host applications may convert them to `\r\n` during file export.

These rules apply consistently to `OUTPUT`, `TEXT`, and reusable fragments. Guidance blocks such as `ABOUT`, `HINT`, and `EXAMPLE` preserve meaningful internal line breaks but omit surrounding blank lines for presentation.

## E00. Guided inputs and question definitions

Guided questions are a core language feature.

Every question can describe what the user should provide, explain how to phrase the answer, and provide examples.

### E00.1. Required input

`ASK REQUIRED` means the runner must collect a nonempty answer before finalizing the output.

The answer is assigned to `$input`.

### E00.2. Optional input

An optional question may be skipped.

An absent optional value is distinct from a provided value when evaluating conditions.

If an optional variable is interpolated directly, its absence renders as empty text. The editor should warn when such usage might produce an awkward sentence.

### E00.3. Defaults

A default proposes an actual value.

An example merely illustrates an answer and must never be silently selected.

A required field with an accepted, valid default counts as answered.

### E00.4. Multiple examples with explanations

A question can provide several examples, with optional explanatory notes.

Both forms are valid:

and:

The CLI can display numbered examples. The web application can provide expandable explanations or selectable suggestions.

Selecting an example may populate the corresponding input field, but examples do not automatically become answers.

### E00.5. Grammatical guidance

The `FORM` property describes the expected grammatical structure.

For example:

The processing question should encourage the answer "validates its syntax", not "the application validates its syntax".

GTL does not need automatic grammatical enforcement in its initial version.

Guidance, examples, and a live output preview should help users create grammatically compatible answers.

### E00.6. Input presentation hints

Initial presentation hints may include:

`INPUT: lines` collects a list of entered lines, which can be processed using `FOR EACH`.

These are interface hints, not static type declarations.

## F00. Output definitions and formats

An output defines the text generated from collected values.

### F00.1. Default output

A template may define one unnamed default output.

### F00.2. Multiple named outputs

The runtime asks the questions once and can render any of the three formats.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
{@body const [format,setFormat] = DIL.useState("summary")}
{@body const examples = {summary:"Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.",detailed:"Input: pasted JSON\nProcessing: checks it for syntax errors\nResult: a readable formatted version",markdown:"**Input:** pasted JSON\n\n**Processing:** checks it for syntax errors\n\n**Output:** a readable formatted version"}}
<text weight="medium">Output format preview</text>
<segmented-control block value={format} onChange={setFormat} options={[{label:"Summary",value:"summary"},{label:"Detailed",value:"detailed"},{label:"Markdown",value:"markdown"}]}/>
<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md" minHeight="106px">
<text preserveWhitespace size="sm">{examples[format]}</text>
</box>
<row justify="end">
<button color="secondary" variant="outline" onClick={()=>GenUI.copy(examples[format])}><icon name="copy" size="sm"/> Copy output</button>
</row>
</box>

Named outputs reuse the `$` syntax for identifiers.

Question variables and output identifiers must not reuse the same name in one template.

If a template has several named outputs but no default, the runtime asks the user which output to render.

### F00.3. Implicit questions

A minimal template can omit explicit question declarations.

The engine discovers the variables and creates basic questions.

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="lg" padding={3}>
<text size="xs" color="secondary" weight="medium">AUTOMATIC QUESTIONS</text>
<text preserveWhitespace size="sm">{`1. Enter name:
2. Enter topic:
3. Enter date:`}</text>
</box>

Implicit question creation should only be enabled when the template contains no explicit `ASK` or `CHOOSE` declarations.

Once an author explicitly declares fields, undefined variables become validation errors.

This prevents a misspelled reference from silently creating another input.

### F00.4. Output selection and editing

Collected answers should remain available when switching outputs.

Editing `$processing` should update every output that uses it.

The renderer should not mutate the original answers.

## G01. Choices, conditions, and iteration

This section incorporates the revised `OPTION` syntax.

Choices are values selected from a predefined set of valid identifiers. Each identifier may optionally have a separate human-readable display label.

### G01.1. Choice declarations

A complete choice block may use explicit display labels.

The simplified equivalent is:

Both are valid.

The first displays the custom labels.

The second displays the option identifiers exactly as authored.

### G01.2. Implicit and explicit display labels

There are two valid forms:

Their semantics are:

| Declaration | Stored value | Visible label |
|---|---|---|
| `OPTION investigating` | `investigating` | `investigating` |
| `OPTION investigating: Investigating` | `investigating` | `Investigating` |
| `OPTION fixed` | `fixed` | `fixed` |
| `OPTION fixed: Fixed` | `fixed` | `Fixed` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce` | `cannot-reproduce` | `cannot-reproduce` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce: Cannot reproduce` | `cannot-reproduce` | `Cannot reproduce` |
| `OPTION yes` | `yes` | `yes` |
| `OPTION no: No` | `no` | `No` |

**A missing colon means the identifier itself is the display label.**

The runtime must not automatically capitalize, split hyphens, convert underscores to spaces, or otherwise humanize shorthand labels.

For example:

must display:

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2}>
<text size="sm">cannot-reproduce</text>
</box>

Not "Cannot reproduce".

If that presentation is desired, the author writes:

This rule is simple, predictable, and avoids hidden formatting behavior.

### G01.3. Mixed option declarations

Shorthand and explicit labels may coexist.

The visible options are:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
{@body const [status,setStatus] = DIL.useState("investigating")}
<radio-group direction="col" value={status} onChange={setStatus}>
<radio value="investigating">investigating</radio>
<radio value="fixed">Fixed</radio>
<radio value="cannot-reproduce">Cannot reproduce</radio>
<radio value="pending-review">pending-review</radio>
</radio-group>
<divider color="subtle"/>
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text size="sm" color="secondary">Stored value</text>
<text weight="medium" size="sm"><code>{status}</code></text>
</row>
</box>

The stored value is always the option identifier, independent of its displayed label.

### G01.4. Option identifiers

Option identifiers follow the same general naming rules as variable identifiers, except that they do not use `$`.

Valid examples:

Identifiers must start with a letter or underscore and may contain letters, digits, underscores, and hyphens.

Identifiers cannot contain spaces.

For example:

is invalid because the identifier contains a space.

The author should use either:

or:

### G01.5. Option validation

Choice declarations are validated during template compilation.

The validator must ensure that every `CHOOSE` block defines at least one option, every option identifier is valid, option identifiers are unique within the same `CHOOSE`, and any `DEFAULT` references a declared option identifier.

For example:

is invalid because the identifier `fixed` is declared twice.

Likewise:

is invalid because `completed` is not a declared option.

An explicit label must contain nonempty text:

is invalid. To reuse the identifier as the label, write:

Labels may contain spaces and punctuation:

Option labels are display-only text. They are not used for conditional comparisons.

### G01.6. Conditional questions

Because `OPTION yes` has no colon, the user sees `yes`.

The runtime stores `yes`.

The conditional compares `$include-technology` against that identifier.

If the user selects `no`, the technology question is not displayed.

Changing the choice from `yes` to `no` deactivates that question. The runtime may retain its previous answer in the current editing session, allowing it to be restored if the user selects `yes` again.

Inactive answers should not count as provided during rendering.

### G01.7. Conditions

The initial condition vocabulary includes:

`IS` compares values without implicit conversion.

For choice fields, it compares the selected identifier, never the visible label.

For example:

The condition matches `fixed`.

It does not compare against `Fixed successfully`.

`IS PROVIDED` means the value exists and is not empty.

Boolean `false` and numeric zero count as provided values. Empty strings and empty lists do not.

`WHEN` is the canonical conditional keyword in version 1. `IF` may be considered later as an alias, but is not necessary initially.

### G01.8. Iteration

A composition can iterate through the collected lines.

For three entered features:

the result is:

An empty optional `lines` input behaves as an empty collection.

Attempting to iterate over a non-iterable value produces a runtime error.

The loop variable `$feature` exists only within the corresponding `FOR EACH` block.

Indexed loops, mutable counters, arbitrary arithmetic expressions, and general-purpose evaluation are outside the initial scope.

## H00. Conditional output and reusable fragments

`OUTPUT` blocks should remain literal text apart from interpolation.

Conditional composition should use a separate construct named `COMPOSE`.

### H00.1. Basic conditional composition

A `COMPOSE` block defines a structured output.

A `TEXT` block defines literal content within that composition.

`WHEN` determines whether its child segments are included.

### H00.2. Joining generated segments

The initial separators are:

Their meaning is:

| Value | Separator |
|---|---|
| `none` | Empty string |
| `space` | One space |
| `newline` | One `\n` |
| `blank-line` | Two `\n` characters |

The default separator is `newline`.

Empty rendered segments do not generate separators.

This permits optional sections without creating unnecessary blank lines.

### H00.3. Reusable fragments

Fragments are reusable text components.

`USE` inserts the rendered fragment into the current composition.

Fragments are internal building blocks. Named outputs and named compositions are selectable results.

### H00.4. Complete conditional response example

Conditions are evaluated in source order.

Independent matching `WHEN` blocks may each contribute text.

An `ELSE` belongs to its immediately preceding `WHEN` and executes only if that condition is false.

Choice-based conditions can be written as mutually exclusive branches, as in this example.

The five description styles from the original PowerShell workflow become independent GTL templates.

Each template defines its own guidance and sentence structure.

### I00.2. Problem + solution

### I00.5. Structured project profile

## J00. Template catalogs and selection

A catalog groups related templates and defines their selection order.

Entries under `PICK TEMPLATE:` are template identifiers and do not require `$`.

The runtime reads each template's `TITLE`, `ABOUT`, and top-level `EXAMPLE`.

### J00.1. Example terminal selection

## K00. Interactive execution model

After choosing a template, the engine collects answers, evaluates conditions, and updates the output preview.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
{#each [
["Choose template","Catalog title, purpose, and example"],
["Guided questions","QUESTION, HINT, EXAMPLE, FORM"],
["Evaluate and render","Variables, WHEN, FOR EACH, OUTPUT, COMPOSE"],
["Review and edit","Preview, revise answers, edit final text"],
["Deliver text","Copy, insert, export, or send to a host application"]
] as item,i}
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
<text weight="medium">{item[0]}</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">{item[1]}</text>
</box>
{#if i<4}<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>{/if}
{/each}
</box>

### K00.1. Guided question example

The question, guidance, expected grammatical form, and example should remain visible.

The user should not need to remember what a template expects.

### K00.2. Review and editing

Editing an input rerenders the output.

Editing the generated text creates a final-text override for the current session. It does not modify the template or its original input values.

The `REVIEW:` block controls available operations.

Some operations are contextual. Switching templates requires a catalog, while changing formats requires multiple outputs.

Terminal colors, emphasis, padding, and alignment belong to the interface implementation, not the GTL syntax.

## L01. Lexer, parser, and runtime architecture

The proposed implementation language is TypeScript.

The core engine should have no dependency on a particular UI framework.

### L01.1. Architecture
