### L01.2. Lexer

The lexer should be line-oriented and indentation-aware.

| Token category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Declaration keywords | `TEMPLATE`, `CATALOG`, `TITLE`, `ASK`, `CHOOSE` |
| Control keywords | `WHEN`, `ELSE`, `FOR EACH`, `USE` |
| Choice keywords | `OPTION`, `DEFAULT` |
| Modifiers | `REQUIRED`, `OPTIONAL`, `KEEP MARGIN` |
| Delimiters | `:`, `{{`, `}}` |
| Variable references | `$input`, `$project-name` |
| Identifiers | `fixed`, `cannot-reproduce`, `needs_review` |
| Indentation | `INDENT`, `DEDENT` |
| Literal text | Raw text lines |

The lexer must recognize both option forms:

The colon distinguishes the explicit-label form from the shorthand form.

The parser should not attempt to infer display labels by modifying identifiers.

Literal blocks should remain raw text except for interpolation expressions.

### L01.3. Parser

A conceptual AST for a guided question might look like:

For a choice:

This AST representation demonstrates the new rule.

A shorthand option receives the same key and label.

An explicitly labeled option preserves its key while storing a separate label.

`explicitLabel` is useful for source-preserving editors and formatters, even though runtime selection does not require it.

### L01.4. Source diagnostics

Every AST node should preserve its source location.

Example:

For duplicate choices:

For an invalid default:

A useful diagnostic system is more valuable than adding complicated language features.

### L01.5. Semantic validation

Validation should detect:

1. Duplicate variable identifiers.
2. Undefined variable references.
3. Duplicate output identifiers.
4. Duplicate option identifiers within a `CHOOSE` block.
5. Missing option declarations.
6. Invalid option identifiers.
7. Empty explicit option labels.
8. Defaults that reference undeclared choices.
9. Invalid fragment references and reference cycles.
10. Invalid variable scope and malformed indentation.

Option labels are not required to be unique. Two options may display the same label, although the editor may warn about ambiguity.

The validator should not reject unconventional grammatical phrasing.

### L01.6. Runtime data model

The runtime maintains an answer map, active questions, selected choices, and the selected output.

Conceptually:

For choice input:

The stored status is the identifier, not the visible label.

The runtime evaluates conditions against current values, generates active questions, and renders the selected output.

Changing an answer invalidates the previous render.

### L01.7. Runtime conversion rules

Strings render directly.

Numbers use a documented invariant representation.

Booleans render as `true` or `false`.

Missing optional text values render as empty strings.

Lists and objects should not silently convert to arbitrary text. Authors should use iteration or future explicit formatting operations.

Attempting to interpolate an unsupported structured value should produce a runtime diagnostic.

This maintains dynamic typing while keeping runtime behavior predictable.

## M01. Grammar and parsing contract

The following is a simplified grammar sketch, not yet a complete formal grammar.

### M01.1. Option parsing

The option parser has two branches.

Produces:

While:

produces:

The existence of the colon determines which branch is used.

No automatic label generation or string formatting is performed.

### M01.2. Contextual interpretation

Keyword interpretation depends on the current structural context.

`OPTION` is valid within a `CHOOSE` block.

`HINT` is a property inside an `ASK` block.

`TEXT` belongs to `COMPOSE`.

Inside literal text, keyword-looking content is preserved.

For example:

This outputs the literal words rather than evaluating a condition.

Likewise:

outputs those two lines as text.

### M01.3. Source formatting

The canonical source formatter should use two spaces per structural indentation level and preserve authored literal text.

It should normalize spacing between declarations without changing output semantics.

It must preserve the distinction between:

and:

Even though they have identical runtime labels, the declarations are syntactically different.

The formatter should also preserve `KEEP MARGIN`, explicit trailing newlines, and meaningful literal blank lines.

Comments may be introduced using `#` at the beginning of structural lines. A `#` inside a literal text block remains ordinary text.

## N01. Expected behavior and conformance tests

The parser and runtime should have executable conformance tests based on source files, supplied answers, and exact expected output.

| Test | Required behavior |
|---|---|
| `$input` inside ordinary text | Remains literal |
| `{{$input}}` inside output | Interpolates variable |
| `\{{$input}}` inside output | Produces literal `{{$input}}` |
| `OPTION fixed` | Key `fixed`, label `fixed` |
| `OPTION fixed: Fixed` | Key `fixed`, label `Fixed` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce` | Label `cannot-reproduce` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce: Cannot reproduce` | Label `Cannot reproduce` |
| Mixed labeled/unlabeled options | Accepted |
| Duplicate option identifiers | Validation error |
| Duplicate display labels | Allowed |
| Unknown default option | Validation error |
| Empty explicit option label | Validation error |
| Option identifier with internal hyphen | Accepted |
| Option identifier containing spaces | Validation error |
| Choice-based `WHEN` | Compares option keys |
| `WHEN` inside `OUTPUT` | Treated as literal text |
| `WHEN` inside `COMPOSE` | Evaluates condition |
| Extra blank lines before next declaration | Removed from output end |
| Blank lines between paragraphs | Preserved |
| `TRAILING NEWLINES 2` | Exactly two final newline characters |
| Default margin behavior | Removes common indentation |
| `KEEP MARGIN` | Preserves authored indentation |
| Missing required answer | Prevents acceptance |
| Missing optional answer | Allowed |
| Undefined variable in explicit template | Validation error |
| Unknown variable in implicit-input template | Creates a question |
| Empty optional `lines` input | Iterates zero times |
| Undefined catalog template | Resolution error |

## O00. Integration with the PowerShell workflow

The PowerShell helper remains a useful first integration target.

Responsibilities should be separated.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md" align="center" gap={1}>
**GTL template**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Defines questions and generates descriptions</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**GTL CLI**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Runs the interview and returns accepted text</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Add-Project.ps1**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Updates HTML and RSS entries</text>
</box>
</box>

GTL should not contain repository-specific knowledge about `index.html`, RSS, or Git.

The PowerShell helper can consume the generated description through standard output or a structured runtime API.

The same description templates can then be reused for README files, GitHub summaries, release notes, and other writing tasks.

A future noninteractive invocation can allow host applications to supply some answers and request only the missing values.

## P00. Template libraries, distribution, and text expansion

The provisional file extension is `.gtl`.

A template library may use the following structure:

Template libraries may be installed from local directories or GitHub repositories.

The initial DSL should remain declarative.

Downloaded templates must not execute arbitrary JavaScript, PowerShell, or operating-system commands.

Filesystem access, clipboard operations, browser insertion, and similar privileged operations belong to host applications.

### P00.1. Text-expansion example

A text-expansion adapter could detect `:reply`, open the guided questions, render the result, and insert it into the current application with user approval.

The same template should also be executable from a CLI or web editor.

## Q00. Development plan

The implementation should proceed in stages.

| Stage | Scope | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Core language | Lexer, parser, variables, interpolation, `ASK`, `OUTPUT`, whitespace handling | Interactive CLI runner |
| 2. Guided workflows | `CHOOSE`, shorthand/labeled `OPTION`, `WHEN`, `REVIEW`, catalogs, live previews | Complete project-description wizard |
| 3. Composition | `FRAGMENT`, `COMPOSE`, `FOR EACH`, named outputs, formatting controls | Reusable document and message templates |
| 4. Application | Web editor, template library, GitHub distribution, text-expansion integration | General-purpose template management application |

The first substantive test should implement all five description styles in GTL and reproduce the earlier PowerShell wizard's behavior.

The choice parser should support both option forms before the guided-workflow stage is considered complete.

## R01. Design decisions and remaining questions

The current proposed language is summarized below.

| Design question | Proposed decision |
|---|---|
| How are variables identified? | `$name` |
| Can identifiers contain hyphens? | Yes, internally |
| How are variables interpolated? | `{{$name}}` |
| How are declarations written? | Uppercase keywords with colons |
| How are blocks scoped? | Indentation |
| How are required and optional questions expressed? | `ASK REQUIRED`, `ASK OPTIONAL` |
| Are static types mandatory? | No |
| How is question guidance provided? | `HINT`, `EXAMPLE`, `FORM` |
| How are choices expressed? | `CHOOSE` and `OPTION` |
| Can option labels be omitted? | Yes |
| How does `OPTION fixed` display? | `fixed` exactly |
| How does `OPTION fixed: Fixed` display? | `Fixed` |
| Are custom labels used as stored values? | No |
| Are option keys validated? | Yes |
| Can option keys contain hyphens? | Yes |
| Are duplicate option keys allowed? | No |
| Are duplicate labels allowed? | Yes |
| Are defaults validated against option keys? | Yes |
| How are conditions expressed? | `WHEN`, `ELSE` |
| How are raw and conditional outputs distinguished? | `OUTPUT` versus `COMPOSE` |
| How are fragments reused? | `FRAGMENT`, `USE` |
| Are surrounding blank lines retained? | No, unless explicitly requested |
| How is indentation preserved? | `KEEP MARGIN` |
| How are trailing newlines expressed? | `TRAILING NEWLINES n` |
| Can ordinary text contain keywords? | Yes, in literal text blocks |
| Is the language tied to a particular UI? | No |

### R01.1. Outstanding design questions

Two design decisions remain worth testing before freezing version 1.

First, whether `COMPOSE` is the best name for structured output assembly. Alternatives include `BUILD` or `RENDER`, but the distinction between raw text and structured composition is more important than the exact keyword.

Second, how much expression syntax the language should eventually support. The current proposal intentionally limits conditions to comparisons and presence checks. Arithmetic, arbitrary functions, and general-purpose expression evaluation should only be introduced in response to concrete use cases.

### R01.2. Recommended implementation boundary

The specification is mature enough to begin a parser prototype.

The first implementation should prioritize sections A00-F00, including indentation and newline behavior, followed by the complete `CHOOSE` and `OPTION` rules in G01.

That gives the first usable interpreter a small syntax, predictable behavior, and a practical set of project-description templates for validation.

The central language principle remains unchanged: **authors describe the questions and the text they want, while the runtime handles prompting, variable storage, condition evaluation, and rendering.**

# Guided Template Language (GTL)

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Language specification | Draft 0.3 | Consolidated design</text>

This is the complete specification with all previously proposed design decisions accepted.

The Design Question / Decision format is retained, with the second column renamed from "Proposed Decision" to "Decision" in section R02.

All other language rules, examples, parsing behavior, runtime architecture, and implementation plans are preserved.

---

## A00. Language philosophy

GTL is a domain-specific scripting language for creating guided text templates. It combines ordinary text, interactive questions, examples, conditional logic, reusable text fragments, and output generation.

It is intended to support project descriptions, README files, messages, bug-report responses, documentation, and reusable text-expansion templates.

The language has five principal design goals.

1. **Readable at first sight.** Keywords such as `ASK`, `WHEN`, `ELSE`, and `FOR EACH` should communicate their purpose without requiring knowledge of formal programming syntax.
2. **Minimal punctuation.** Colons separate declarations from values or introduce blocks. Indentation determines scope. Standalone actions do not require punctuation.
3. **Literal text by default.** Writing HTML, Markdown, email messages, source code, and other text should require almost no escaping.
4. **Progressive complexity.** A three-line template should be valid. Authors add guided questions, choices, and conditions only when needed.
5. **Independent of the interface.** The same template should run in a terminal, browser, desktop application, or text-expansion tool.

A template is primarily a description of an interaction and its resulting text. GTL is not intended to become a general-purpose programming language.

## B01. Core syntax

The following is the reference example for the language.

### B01.1. Complete input + processing + output template

This example illustrates the principal syntax conventions.

Variables begin with `$`, declarations use uppercase keywords, colons introduce values or blocks, and indentation determines scope.

Text after `QUESTION:`, `HINT:`, `EXAMPLE:`, or inside `OUTPUT:` is ordinary language.

### B01.2. Statement conventions

| Syntax | Interpretation |
|---|---|
| `TEMPLATE: name` | Declare a template |
| `TITLE: text` | Assign a human-readable title |
| `ABOUT:` | Start a description block |
| `ASK REQUIRED $name QUESTION: text` | Declare a required input |
| `ASK OPTIONAL $name QUESTION: text` | Declare an optional input |
| `CHOOSE REQUIRED $name QUESTION: text` | Declare a required choice |
| `OPTION key` | Declare an option; display its key verbatim |
| `OPTION key: Label` | Declare an option with a custom display label |
| `HINT: text` | Provide guidance |
| `EXAMPLE: text` | Provide an example |
| `OUTPUT:` | Define the default output |
| `OUTPUT $summary:` | Define a named output |
| `REVIEW:` | Declare review behavior |
| `ALLOW EDIT` | Enable a standalone action |
| `WHEN $status IS fixed:` | Introduce a conditional block |
| `ELSE:` | Introduce an alternative branch |
| `FOR EACH $item IN $items:` | Introduce iteration |

The primary convention is that a colon introduces a value or block, while a standalone instruction does not require punctuation.

`OPTION fixed` is therefore valid because the option identifier is sufficient to define both the stored value and its visible label.

`OPTION fixed: Fixed` introduces a separate display label.

### B01.3. Capitalization

Structural keywords are uppercase and case-sensitive.

`WHEN` and `IS` are language keywords. `fixed` is an ordinary value.

Variable names, template identifiers, and option identifiers are case-sensitive.

Human-readable text retains its original capitalization.

Uppercase words inside literal text blocks do not become instructions.

## C00. Variables and interpolation

Every variable reference begins with `$`.

Variable identifiers may contain letters, digits, underscores, and hyphens. The first character after `$` must be a letter or underscore.

Internal hyphens are supported intentionally.

### C00.1. Variable interpolation

Inside generated text, variables use double braces while retaining the dollar sign.

Rendered output:

<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md">
Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.
</box>

The braces distinguish interpolation from ordinary text.

For example:

`$25` remains literal.

### C00.2. Dynamic values without declared types

GTL does not use a static type system.

Runtime values may be strings, numbers, booleans, lists, objects, or missing values.

Authors do not need declarations such as:

The parser recognizes variable references. The runtime determines their values.

Individual operations still have requirements. For example, `FOR EACH` expects an iterable value. That requirement is checked when the operation executes.

Properties such as `FORM: noun-phrase` and `INPUT: date` are descriptive guidance or input presentation hints. They do not establish static types.

### C00.3. Literal interpolation characters

A backslash before an interpolation opener prevents interpolation.

Rendered output:

Outside recognized interpolation escapes, backslashes remain ordinary characters.

A general-purpose string-escaping system is not required.

## D00. Indentation, margins, and newlines

Whitespace is both part of the language syntax and part of the text being generated.

GTL distinguishes structural whitespace from literal content whitespace.

### D00.1. Indentation and block boundaries

The recommended indentation is two spaces per level.

The parser may accept deeper consistent space indentation. Tabs are not supported for structural indentation in version 1.

A block begins after its declaration and ends at the next nonblank line whose indentation is equal to or less than the declaration's indentation.

No `END`, `ENDIF`, closing brace, or terminator is necessary.

Blank lines do not close blocks.

Only a nonblank line's indentation determines a block boundary.

### D00.2. Default margin stripping

Consider:

The engine determines the common indentation of nonblank content lines and removes it.

Rendered output:

Intentional relative indentation remains intact.

### D00.3. Default newline behavior

The default rule is:

**Preserve blank lines inside text, remove blank lines surrounding the text, and do not automatically append a trailing newline.**

Example:

```text
OUTPUT:
First paragraph.

Second paragraph.

OUTPUT $other:
Another result.
```

The first output is exactly:

There are two newline characters between the paragraphs and zero after the final period.

The two empty source lines before `OUTPUT $other:` are visual separation, not additional generated content.

### D00.4. Explicit trailing newlines

Authors can request an exact number of trailing newline characters.

The generated string ends with exactly two `\n` characters:

The default is equivalent to:

An optional `LEADING NEWLINES 2` modifier may be introduced later.

### D00.5. Keeping indentation

The `KEEP MARGIN` modifier preserves authored indentation.

Unlike default margin stripping, the four spaces preceding `function` are retained.

Modifiers can be combined:

### D00.6. Whitespace-processing sequence

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
{#each [
"Read raw block lines",
"Identify block boundaries",
"Remove surrounding blank lines",
"Strip common margin unless KEEP MARGIN",
"Evaluate variable interpolation",
"Apply explicit trailing newlines"
] as item,i}
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2} align="center">
<text size="sm" weight={i===0||i===5?"medium":"normal"}>{item}</text>
</box>
{#if i<5}
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
{/if}
{/each}
</box>

Internally, line endings should be normalized to `\n`.

Host applications may convert them to `\r\n` during file export.

These rules apply consistently to `OUTPUT`, `TEXT`, and reusable fragments. Guidance blocks such as `ABOUT`, `HINT`, and `EXAMPLE` preserve meaningful internal line breaks but omit surrounding blank lines for presentation.

## E00. Guided inputs and question definitions

Guided questions are a core language feature.

Every question can describe what the user should provide, explain how to phrase the answer, and provide examples.

### E00.1. Required input

`ASK REQUIRED` means the runner must collect a nonempty answer before finalizing the output.

The answer is assigned to `$input`.

### E00.2. Optional input

An optional question may be skipped.

An absent optional value is distinct from a provided value when evaluating conditions.

If an optional variable is interpolated directly, its absence renders as empty text. The editor should warn when such usage might produce an awkward sentence.

### E00.3. Defaults

A default proposes an actual value.

An example merely illustrates an answer and must never be silently selected.

A required field with an accepted, valid default counts as answered.

### E00.4. Multiple examples with explanations

A question can provide several examples, with optional explanatory notes.

Both forms are valid:

and:

The CLI can display numbered examples. The web application can provide expandable explanations or selectable suggestions.

Selecting an example may populate the corresponding input field, but examples do not automatically become answers.

### E00.5. Grammatical guidance

The `FORM` property describes the expected grammatical structure.

For example:

The processing question should encourage the answer "validates its syntax", not "the application validates its syntax".

GTL does not need automatic grammatical enforcement in its initial version.

Guidance, examples, and a live output preview should help users create grammatically compatible answers.

### E00.6. Input presentation hints

Initial presentation hints may include:

`INPUT: lines` collects a list of entered lines, which can be processed using `FOR EACH`.

These are interface hints, not static type declarations.

## F00. Output definitions and formats

An output defines the text generated from collected values.

### F00.1. Default output

A template may define one unnamed default output.

### F00.2. Multiple named outputs

The runtime asks the questions once and can render any of the three formats.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
{@body const [format,setFormat] = DIL.useState("summary")}
{@body const examples = {summary:"Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.",detailed:"Input: pasted JSON\nProcessing: checks it for syntax errors\nResult: a readable formatted version",markdown:"**Input:** pasted JSON\n\n**Processing:** checks it for syntax errors\n\n**Output:** a readable formatted version"}}
<text weight="medium">Output format preview</text>
<segmented-control block value={format} onChange={setFormat} options={[{label:"Summary",value:"summary"},{label:"Detailed",value:"detailed"},{label:"Markdown",value:"markdown"}]}/>
<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md" minHeight="106px">
<text preserveWhitespace size="sm">{examples[format]}</text>
</box>
<row justify="end">
<button color="secondary" variant="outline" onClick={()=>GenUI.copy(examples[format])}><icon name="copy" size="sm"/> Copy output</button>
</row>
</box>

Named outputs reuse the `$` syntax for identifiers.

Question variables and output identifiers must not reuse the same name in one template.

If a template has several named outputs but no default, the runtime asks the user which output to render.

### F00.3. Implicit questions

A minimal template can omit explicit question declarations.

The engine discovers the variables and creates basic questions.

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="lg" padding={3}>
<text size="xs" color="secondary" weight="medium">AUTOMATIC QUESTIONS</text>
<text preserveWhitespace size="sm">{`1. Enter name:
2. Enter topic:
3. Enter date:`}</text>
</box>

Implicit question creation should only be enabled when the template contains no explicit `ASK` or `CHOOSE` declarations.

Once an author explicitly declares fields, undefined variables become validation errors.

This prevents a misspelled reference from silently creating another input.

### F00.4. Output selection and editing

Collected answers should remain available when switching outputs.

Editing `$processing` should update every output that uses it.

The renderer should not mutate the original answers.

## G01. Choices, conditions, and iteration

Choices are values selected from a predefined set of valid identifiers. Each identifier may optionally have a separate human-readable display label.

### G01.1. Choice declarations

A complete choice block may use explicit display labels.

The simplified equivalent is:

Both are valid.

The first displays the custom labels.

The second displays the option identifiers exactly as authored.

### G01.2. Implicit and explicit display labels

There are two valid forms:

Their semantics are:

| Declaration | Stored value | Visible label |
|---|---|---|
| `OPTION investigating` | `investigating` | `investigating` |
| `OPTION investigating: Investigating` | `investigating` | `Investigating` |
| `OPTION fixed` | `fixed` | `fixed` |
| `OPTION fixed: Fixed` | `fixed` | `Fixed` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce` | `cannot-reproduce` | `cannot-reproduce` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce: Cannot reproduce` | `cannot-reproduce` | `Cannot reproduce` |
| `OPTION yes` | `yes` | `yes` |
| `OPTION no: No` | `no` | `No` |

**A missing colon means the identifier itself is the display label.**

The runtime must not automatically capitalize, split hyphens, convert underscores to spaces, or otherwise humanize shorthand labels.

For example:

must display:

<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={2}>
<text size="sm">cannot-reproduce</text>
</box>

Not "Cannot reproduce".

If that presentation is desired, the author writes:

This rule is simple, predictable, and avoids hidden formatting behavior.

### G01.3. Mixed option declarations

Shorthand and explicit labels may coexist.

The visible options are:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
{@body const [status,setStatus] = DIL.useState("investigating")}
<radio-group direction="col" value={status} onChange={setStatus}>
<radio value="investigating">investigating</radio>
<radio value="fixed">Fixed</radio>
<radio value="cannot-reproduce">Cannot reproduce</radio>
<radio value="pending-review">pending-review</radio>
</radio-group>
<divider color="subtle"/>
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text size="sm" color="secondary">Stored value</text>
<text weight="medium" size="sm"><code>{status}</code></text>
</row>
</box>

The stored value is always the option identifier, independent of its displayed label.

### G01.4. Option identifiers

Option identifiers follow the same general naming rules as variable identifiers, except that they do not use `$`.

Valid examples:

Identifiers must start with a letter or underscore and may contain letters, digits, underscores, and hyphens.

Identifiers cannot contain spaces.

For example:

is invalid because the identifier contains a space.

The author should use either:

or:

### G01.5. Option validation

Choice declarations are validated during template compilation.

The validator must ensure that every `CHOOSE` block defines at least one option, every option identifier is valid, option identifiers are unique within the same `CHOOSE`, and any `DEFAULT` references a declared option identifier.

For example:

is invalid because the identifier `fixed` is declared twice.

Likewise:

is invalid because `completed` is not a declared option.

An explicit label must contain nonempty text:

is invalid. To reuse the identifier as the label, write:

Labels may contain spaces and punctuation:

Option labels are display-only text. They are not used for conditional comparisons.

### G01.6. Conditional questions

Because `OPTION yes` has no colon, the user sees `yes`.

The runtime stores `yes`.

The conditional compares `$include-technology` against that identifier.

If the user selects `no`, the technology question is not displayed.

Changing the choice from `yes` to `no` deactivates that question. The runtime may retain its previous answer in the current editing session, allowing it to be restored if the user selects `yes` again.

Inactive answers should not count as provided during rendering.

### G01.7. Conditions

The initial condition vocabulary includes:

`IS` compares values without implicit conversion.

For choice fields, it compares the selected identifier, never the visible label.

For example:

The condition matches `fixed`.

It does not compare against `Fixed successfully`.

`IS PROVIDED` means the value exists and is not empty.

Boolean `false` and numeric zero count as provided values. Empty strings and empty lists do not.

`WHEN` is the canonical conditional keyword in version 1. `IF` may be considered later as an alias, but is not necessary initially.

### G01.8. Iteration

A composition can iterate through the collected lines.

For three entered features:

the result is:

An empty optional `lines` input behaves as an empty collection.

Attempting to iterate over a non-iterable value produces a runtime error.

The loop variable `$feature` exists only within the corresponding `FOR EACH` block.

Indexed loops, mutable counters, arbitrary arithmetic expressions, and general-purpose evaluation are outside the initial scope.

## H00. Conditional output and reusable fragments

`OUTPUT` blocks should remain literal text apart from interpolation.

Conditional composition should use a separate construct named `COMPOSE`.

### H00.1. Basic conditional composition

A `COMPOSE` block defines a structured output.

A `TEXT` block defines literal content within that composition.

`WHEN` determines whether its child segments are included.

### H00.2. Joining generated segments

The initial separators are:

Their meaning is:

| Value | Separator |
|---|---|
| `none` | Empty string |
| `space` | One space |
| `newline` | One `\n` |
| `blank-line` | Two `\n` characters |

The default separator is `newline`.

Empty rendered segments do not generate separators.

This permits optional sections without creating unnecessary blank lines.

### H00.3. Reusable fragments

Fragments are reusable text components.

`USE` inserts the rendered fragment into the current composition.

Fragments are internal building blocks. Named outputs and named compositions are selectable results.

### H00.4. Complete conditional response example

Conditions are evaluated in source order.

Independent matching `WHEN` blocks may each contribute text.

An `ELSE` belongs to its immediately preceding `WHEN` and executes only if that condition is false.

Choice-based conditions can be written as mutually exclusive branches, as in this example.

## J00. Template catalogs and selection

A catalog groups related templates and defines their selection order.

Entries under `PICK TEMPLATE:` are template identifiers and do not require `$`.

The runtime reads each template's `TITLE`, `ABOUT`, and top-level `EXAMPLE`.

### J00.1. Example terminal selection

<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="lg" gap={2}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs" weight="medium">GTL CLI | TEMPLATE CATALOG</text>
<box gap={1}>
**PROJECT DESCRIPTION**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Choose how you want to describe your project.</text>
</box>
{#each [
["1","PURPOSE + FUNCTION","What it is, what it does, why it is useful.","A browser-based tool that formats and validates JSON to make structured data easier to inspect."],
["2","PROBLEM + SOLUTION","Who needs it, the problem, and the solution.","Helps developers inspect messy JSON by validating its syntax and formatting the result."],
["3","INPUT + PROCESSING + OUTPUT","What goes in, what happens, what comes out.","Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version."],
["4","FEATURES + TECHNOLOGY","Implementation stack and notable capabilities.","A browser-based JSON utility built with vanilla JavaScript, featuring syntax validation and readable formatting."],
["5","STRUCTURED PROJECT PROFILE","Audience, purpose, workflow, and implementation.","A browser-based utility for developers inspecting JSON files that validates JSON and formats it for reading, with immediate syntax feedback."]
] as item}
<box gap={1}>
<text weight="medium" size="sm">{item[0]}. {item[1]}</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">{item[2]}</text>
<text size="xs">Example: {item[3]}</text>
</box>
{#if item[0]!=="5"}<divider color="subtle"/>{/if}
{/each}
<text color="secondary" size="sm">Select template [1-5]: 3</text>
</box>

The catalog does not duplicate the examples. They come directly from the referenced template definitions.

## K00. Interactive execution model

After choosing a template, the engine collects answers, evaluates conditions, and updates the output preview.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
{#each [
["Choose template","Catalog title, purpose, and example"],
["Guided questions","QUESTION, HINT, EXAMPLE, FORM"],
["Evaluate and render","Variables, WHEN, FOR EACH, OUTPUT, COMPOSE"],
["Review and edit","Preview, revise answers, edit final text"],
["Deliver text","Copy, insert, export, or send to a host application"]
] as item,i}
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
<text weight="medium">{item[0]}</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">{item[1]}</text>
</box>
{#if i<4}<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>{/if}
{/each}
</box>

### K00.1. Guided question example

The question, guidance, expected grammatical form, and example should remain visible.

The user should not need to remember what a template expects.

### K00.2. Review and editing

Editing an input rerenders the output.

Editing the generated text creates a final-text override for the current session. It does not modify the template or its original input values.

The `REVIEW:` block controls available operations.

Some operations are contextual. Switching templates requires a catalog, while changing formats requires multiple outputs.

Terminal colors, emphasis, padding, and alignment belong to the interface implementation, not the GTL syntax.

## L01. Lexer, parser, and runtime architecture

The implementation language is TypeScript.

The core engine should have no dependency on a particular UI framework.

### L01.2. Lexer

The lexer should be line-oriented and indentation-aware.

| Token category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Declaration keywords | `TEMPLATE`, `CATALOG`, `TITLE`, `ASK`, `CHOOSE` |
| Control keywords | `WHEN`, `ELSE`, `FOR EACH`, `USE` |
| Choice keywords | `OPTION`, `DEFAULT` |
| Modifiers | `REQUIRED`, `OPTIONAL`, `KEEP MARGIN` |
| Delimiters | `:`, `{{`, `}}` |
| Variable references | `$input`, `$project-name` |
| Identifiers | `fixed`, `cannot-reproduce`, `needs_review` |
| Indentation | `INDENT`, `DEDENT` |
| Literal text | Raw text lines |

The lexer must recognize both option forms:

The colon distinguishes the explicit-label form from the shorthand form.

The parser should not attempt to infer display labels by modifying identifiers.

Literal blocks should remain raw text except for interpolation expressions.

### L01.3. Parser

A conceptual AST for a guided question might look like:

For a choice:

A shorthand option receives the same key and label.

An explicitly labeled option preserves its key while storing a separate label.

`explicitLabel` is useful for source-preserving editors and formatters, even though runtime selection does not require it.

### L01.4. Source diagnostics

Every AST node should preserve its source location.

Example:

For duplicate choices:

For an invalid default:

A useful diagnostic system is more valuable than adding complicated language features.

### L01.5. Semantic validation

Validation should detect:

1. Duplicate variable identifiers.
2. Undefined variable references.
3. Duplicate output identifiers.
4. Duplicate option identifiers within a `CHOOSE` block.
5. Missing option declarations.
6. Invalid option identifiers.
7. Empty explicit option labels.
8. Defaults that reference undeclared choices.
9. Invalid fragment references and reference cycles.
10. Invalid variable scope and malformed indentation.

Option labels are not required to be unique. Two options may display the same label, although the editor may warn about ambiguity.

The validator should not reject unconventional grammatical phrasing.

### L01.6. Runtime data model

The runtime maintains an answer map, active questions, selected choices, and the selected output.

Conceptually:

For choice input:

The stored status is the identifier, not the visible label.

The runtime evaluates conditions against current values, generates active questions, and renders the selected output.

Changing an answer invalidates the previous render.

### L01.7. Runtime conversion rules

Strings render directly.

Numbers use a documented invariant representation.

Booleans render as `true` or `false`.

Missing optional text values render as empty strings.

Lists and objects should not silently convert to arbitrary text. Authors should use iteration or future explicit formatting operations.

Attempting to interpolate an unsupported structured value should produce a runtime diagnostic.

This maintains dynamic typing while keeping runtime behavior predictable.

## M01. Grammar and parsing contract

The following is a simplified grammar sketch, not yet a complete formal grammar.

### M01.1. Option parsing

The option parser has two branches.

Produces:

While:

produces:

The existence of the colon determines which branch is used.

No automatic label generation or string formatting is performed.

### M01.2. Contextual interpretation

Keyword interpretation depends on the current structural context.

`OPTION` is valid within a `CHOOSE` block.

`HINT` is a property inside an `ASK` block.

`TEXT` belongs to `COMPOSE`.

Inside literal text, keyword-looking content is preserved.

For example:

This outputs the literal words rather than evaluating a condition.

Likewise:

outputs those two lines as text.

### M01.3. Source formatting

The canonical source formatter should use two spaces per structural indentation level and preserve authored literal text.

It should normalize spacing between declarations without changing output semantics.

It must preserve the distinction between:

and:

Even though they have identical runtime labels, the declarations are syntactically different.

The formatter should also preserve `KEEP MARGIN`, explicit trailing newlines, and meaningful literal blank lines.

Comments may be introduced using `#` at the beginning of structural lines. A `#` inside a literal text block remains ordinary text.

## N01. Expected behavior and conformance tests

The parser and runtime should have executable conformance tests based on source files, supplied answers, and exact expected output.

| Test | Required behavior |
|---|---|
| `$input` inside ordinary text | Remains literal |
| `{{$input}}` inside output | Interpolates variable |
| `\{{$input}}` inside output | Produces literal `{{$input}}` |
| `OPTION fixed` | Key `fixed`, label `fixed` |
| `OPTION fixed: Fixed` | Key `fixed`, label `Fixed` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce` | Label `cannot-reproduce` |
| `OPTION cannot-reproduce: Cannot reproduce` | Label `Cannot reproduce` |
| Mixed labeled/unlabeled options | Accepted |
| Duplicate option identifiers | Validation error |
| Duplicate display labels | Allowed |
| Unknown default option | Validation error |
| Empty explicit option label | Validation error |
| Option identifier with internal hyphen | Accepted |
| Option identifier containing spaces | Validation error |
| Choice-based `WHEN` | Compares option keys |
| `WHEN` inside `OUTPUT` | Treated as literal text |
| `WHEN` inside `COMPOSE` | Evaluates condition |
| Extra blank lines before next declaration | Removed from output end |
| Blank lines between paragraphs | Preserved |
| `TRAILING NEWLINES 2` | Exactly two final newline characters |
| Default margin behavior | Removes common indentation |
| `KEEP MARGIN` | Preserves authored indentation |
| Missing required answer | Prevents acceptance |
| Missing optional answer | Allowed |
| Undefined variable in explicit template | Validation error |
| Unknown variable in implicit-input template | Creates a question |
| Empty optional `lines` input | Iterates zero times |
| Undefined catalog template | Resolution error |

### N01.2. Explicit-label test

## O00. Integration with the PowerShell workflow

The PowerShell helper remains a useful first integration target.

Responsibilities should be separated.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
<box background="surface-secondary" padding={3} radius="md" align="center" gap={1}>
**GTL template**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Defines questions and generates descriptions</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>

**GTL CLI**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Runs the interview and returns accepted text</text>

<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Add-Project.ps1**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Updates HTML and RSS entries</text>
</box>
</box>

GTL should not contain repository-specific knowledge about `index.html`, RSS, or Git.

The PowerShell helper can consume the generated description through standard output or a structured runtime API.

The same description templates can then be reused for README files, GitHub summaries, release notes, and other writing tasks.

A future noninteractive invocation can allow host applications to supply some answers and request only the missing values.

## P00. Template libraries, distribution, and text expansion

The provisional file extension is `.gtl`.

A template library may use the following structure:

Template libraries may be installed from local directories or GitHub repositories.

The initial DSL should remain declarative.

Downloaded templates must not execute arbitrary JavaScript, PowerShell, or operating-system commands.

Filesystem access, clipboard operations, browser insertion, and similar privileged operations belong to host applications.

### P00.1. Text-expansion example

A text-expansion adapter could detect `:reply`, open the guided questions, render the result, and insert it into the current application with user approval.

The same template should also be executable from a CLI or web editor.

## Q00. Development plan

The implementation should proceed in stages.

| Stage | Scope | Result |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Core language | Lexer, parser, variables, interpolation, `ASK`, `OUTPUT`, whitespace handling | Interactive CLI runner |
| 2. Guided workflows | `CHOOSE`, shorthand/labeled `OPTION`, `WHEN`, `REVIEW`, catalogs, live previews | Complete project-description wizard |
| 3. Composition | `FRAGMENT`, `COMPOSE`, `FOR EACH`, named outputs, formatting controls | Reusable document and message templates |
| 4. Application | Web editor, template library, GitHub distribution, text-expansion integration | General-purpose template management application |

The first substantive test should implement all five description styles in GTL and reproduce the earlier PowerShell wizard's behavior.

The choice parser should support both option forms before the guided-workflow stage is considered complete.

## R02. Design decisions and remaining questions

All decisions in the following table are accepted.

| Design Question | Decision |
|---|---|
| How are variables identified? | `$name` |
| Can identifiers contain hyphens? | Yes, internally |
| How are variables interpolated? | `{{$name}}` |
| How are declarations written? | Uppercase keywords with colons |
| How are blocks scoped? | Indentation |
| How are required and optional questions expressed? | `ASK REQUIRED`, `ASK OPTIONAL` |
| Are static types mandatory? | No |
| How is question guidance provided? | `HINT`, `EXAMPLE`, `FORM` |
| How are choices expressed? | `CHOOSE` and `OPTION` |
| Can option labels be omitted? | Yes |
| How does `OPTION fixed` display? | `fixed` |
| How does `OPTION fixed: Fixed` display? | `Fixed` |
| Are custom labels used as stored values? | No |
| Are option keys validated? | Yes |
| Can option keys contain hyphens? | Yes |
| Are duplicate option keys allowed? | No |
| Are duplicate labels allowed? | Yes |
| Are defaults validated against option keys? | Yes |
| How are conditions expressed? | `WHEN`, `ELSE` |
| How are raw and conditional outputs distinguished? | `OUTPUT` versus `COMPOSE` |
| How are fragments reused? | `FRAGMENT`, `USE` |
| Are surrounding blank lines retained? | No, unless explicitly requested |
| How is indentation preserved? | `KEEP MARGIN` |
| How are trailing newlines expressed? | `TRAILING NEWLINES n` |
| Can ordinary text contain keywords? | Yes, in literal text blocks |
| Is the language tied to a particular UI? | No |

### R02.1. Outstanding design questions

Two design decisions remain worth testing before freezing version 1.

First, whether `COMPOSE` is the best name for structured output assembly. Alternatives include `BUILD` or `RENDER`, but the distinction between raw text and structured composition is more important than the exact keyword.

Second, how much expression syntax the language should eventually support. The current specification intentionally limits conditions to comparisons and presence checks. Arithmetic, arbitrary functions, and general-purpose expression evaluation should only be introduced in response to concrete use cases.

### R02.2. Recommended implementation boundary

The specification is mature enough to begin a parser prototype.

The first implementation should prioritize sections A00-F00, including indentation and newline behavior, followed by the complete `CHOOSE` and `OPTION` rules in G01.

That gives the first usable interpreter a small syntax, predictable behavior, and a practical set of project-description templates for validation.

The central language principle remains unchanged: **authors describe the questions and the text they want, while the runtime handles prompting, variable storage, condition evaluation, and rendering.**

# GTL specification review: first grilling round

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Review baseline: Draft 0.3, sections A00-R02</text>

The current GTL specification has a coherent foundation. The language already separates guided questions, variable interpolation, choices, conditional execution, and text rendering.

I would preserve the accepted design decisions in R02. The purpose of this review is to identify what is still ambiguous, what might be unnecessarily complicated, and what is missing before implementing the parser.

I reviewed the specification in our conversation and the original `Add-Project.ps1` workflow. I did not find a GTL implementation repository, glossary, or ADR collection among the supplied files, so this round is a design review rather than a repository documentation update.

## Initial assessment

<box gap={2}>
<row align="center" justify="between">
**Keep as designed**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Established decisions</text>
</row>
The `$variable` syntax, `{{$variable}}` interpolation, uppercase keywords, indentation-based blocks, optional choice labels, literal `OUTPUT`, and structured `COMPOSE` all serve identifiable purposes. I see no reason to replace them.

<divider color="subtle"/>
<row align="center" justify="between">
**Simplify the initial implementation**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Scope control</text>
</row>
Several features can remain in the specification without being implemented in the first parser: `KEEP MARGIN`, advanced whitespace modifiers, reusable `FRAGMENT` blocks, `FOR EACH`, and multiple example annotations. They are useful, but none is required to reproduce the original five-description wizard.

<divider color="subtle"/>
<row align="center" justify="between">
**Resolve before implementation**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Behavioral gaps</text>
</row>
The most important gaps concern output-specific question selection, literal line wrapping, accessing choice display labels, template resolution, and a contradictory example involving conditional `OUTPUT` declarations.
</box>

## Findings from the current specification

| Finding | Sections | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| Questions are declared globally, but multiple outputs may need different answers | E00, F00, I00 | Underspecified |
| Source newlines are preserved, even when authors simply wrap a long sentence for readability | D00 | Needs an ergonomic test |
| Choice keys and display labels are stored separately, but output interpolation cannot explicitly request the label | G01, C00 | Missing use case |
| One example nests `OUTPUT` inside `WHEN`, although structured output is supposed to use `COMPOSE` | G01, H00 | Inconsistency |
| A catalog references templates by identifier, but resolution scope and duplicate handling are not fully specified | J00, P00 | Missing rule |
| Required fields are defined, but empty whitespace-only answers and list values need exact validation rules | E00, L01 | Underspecified |
| Template libraries can come from GitHub, but version pinning and compatibility are unspecified | P00 | Future requirement |
| Source grammar and parser behavior are illustrated, but do not yet constitute a complete formal grammar | M01 | Implementation prerequisite |

One concrete example of an issue worth fixing without changing your accepted syntax is the conditional-output case.

The specification contains:

But it also establishes that `COMPOSE` owns structured conditional output. My recommendation is to make `COMPOSE` the only conditional text-building mechanism for version 1 and reject nested `OUTPUT` declarations. This preserves the distinction you accepted earlier.

There is also a small prose-template defect: `A {{$type}}` can produce "A interactive application". This is not necessarily a parser defect. It illustrates why a live preview and editable generated text are core parts of the product rather than optional presentation features.

---

## Decision round 1

These four questions are independent. Each addresses a behavior that is not fully settled by the existing specification.

### Q1. Which questions should the wizard ask when a template has multiple outputs?

Consider the `structured-profile` template. It has `$summary` and `$readme` outputs. The `$purpose` answer is used only in the README.

Should the runner collect every required field in the entire template, or only the fields needed for the output currently selected?

**Recommendation: Ask only questions needed for the selected output.** If the user switches to another output, collect any newly required answers. Preserve previously entered answers within the session.

This prevents long templates from requiring answers that will not be used.

### Q2. How should a choice's display label be inserted into output?

We have accepted that `OPTION cannot-reproduce: Cannot reproduce` stores `cannot-reproduce` and displays `Cannot reproduce`.

Currently, `{{$status}}` renders the stored identifier. We have not defined how to render the human-readable label.

I see two reasonable approaches.

**A.** Keep interpolation limited to values. Authors use `WHEN` branches when different wording is needed.

**B.** Keep `{{$status}}` for stored values, but introduce explicit label interpolation, perhaps `{{LABEL $status}}`.

**Recommendation: B.** A text-template language will sometimes need to reproduce what the user selected without duplicating that wording in conditional branches.

The syntax is provisional; we are deciding whether the capability belongs in the language.

### Q3. How should authors write long text without introducing unwanted newlines?

The existing D00 rule preserves line breaks inside output blocks, which is predictable.

However, this:

produces two lines, even if the author intended a single sentence.

Possible approaches are to retain exact line preservation and require one physical line for a single-line result, or introduce an explicit text-folding modifier that joins wrapped source lines.

**Recommendation: Preserve the existing behavior for version 1.** Do not make newline handling implicit. Test whether an explicit folding modifier is actually needed before adding one.

This can be evaluated later with a small prototype that shows source text beside its exact rendered output.

### Q4. How should catalogs resolve template identifiers?

A catalog currently contains:

But the language does not define which files are searched, whether names must be globally unique, or what happens if two installed libraries contain `purpose-function`.

**Recommendation: Resolve identifiers within the catalog's installed library for version 1.** Reject duplicate identifiers within that library. Treat cross-library references and versioned dependencies as later features.

This establishes deterministic behavior without introducing an import system prematurely.

---

## What comes after this round

Once these decisions are settled, I would review the next layer: question dependencies, branching behavior, value validation, error recovery, template loading, and the precise AST and grammar contract.

In particular, I want to test cases where users change an earlier answer, deactivate a required question, switch outputs, or resume an unfinished interview. Those interactions can expose problems that ordinary static template examples do not.

I would also separate the eventual implementation contract from the language tutorial. The current specification explains GTL well, but an implementer will additionally need unambiguous grammar rules and executable conformance fixtures.

**Please answer Q1-Q4.** You can accept all four recommendations or modify any individually. I will treat the existing A00-R02 decisions as settled unless a concrete conflict requires revisiting one.

ChatGPTSat, Oct 24, 9:36 AM

Your answers settle Q1, Q2, and Q3. Q4 points toward a useful simplification: we can remove explicit catalog management from the language without sacrificing template selection.

There is also an important distinction between an undefined variable and a variable whose answer has not yet been collected. I would treat those cases differently.

## Decisions settled

| Design Question | Decision |
|---|---|
| Q1. Which questions should the wizard ask? | Only questions needed for the selected output, including conditions and referenced fragments. |
| Q2. What does choice interpolation produce? | The display label. If no label is declared, use the option identifier verbatim. |
| Q3. How should output newlines behave? | Preserve newlines exactly as authored within the text content, subject to the previously defined margin stripping and boundary trimming. |
| Q4. How should catalogs resolve templates? | Still open. Simplification recommended below. |

### Q1. Questions should follow dependencies, not execution order

Suppose a template defines:

If the user selects `$summary`, the wizard asks for `$type` and `$workflow`.

It does not ask for `$purpose`.

If the user later selects `$readme`, the wizard asks for `$purpose` while retaining the previous answers.

This also applies to conditional branches. The engine evaluates dependencies first and asks for the information needed to determine which questions are active.

**A variable appearing in output before its corresponding question has been displayed is not inherently an error.** GTL is declarative, so the engine should resolve dependencies rather than execute declarations strictly from top to bottom.

### Q2. Choice interpolation uses display labels

We now have a simpler rule:

Selecting the third option produces:

But conditions compare identifiers:

The runtime can represent a choice internally as `{ key, label }`. Conditions compare `key`; interpolation renders `label`.

We do not need a separate `LABEL` operator.

### Q3. Literal newlines remain literal

Agreed. We should not introduce automatic line folding.

Produces:

The existing D00 rules for surrounding blank lines, margin stripping, and explicit trailing newlines remain intact.

---

## Q4. Simplify template discovery

I think we can remove the `CATALOG` declaration from the first version entirely.

Instead of maintaining a catalog that references templates by identifier, **a directory itself becomes the template collection**.

For example:

The application opens a directory, discovers `.gtl` files, and displays the templates in filename order.

Each `.gtl` file contains exactly one `TEMPLATE` declaration.

The title, description, examples, and questions remain inside that template.

This eliminates the need for explicit `CATALOG`, `PICK TEMPLATE`, a global template registry, and cross-file template references in version 1.

It also avoids complicated resolution behavior when two libraries happen to contain templates with the same identifier.

We can still support several named outputs within one template. Those are local to the file and do not create the same resolution problem.

**Recommendation Q4: Accept directory-based discovery, one template per file, and no cross-file references in version 1.**

This is a genuine reduction in language complexity rather than just postponing implementation.

## Q5. What should happen when a variable has no value?

This is the new decision raised by your response.

I recommend distinguishing three cases rather than introducing a configurable global error policy immediately.

| Situation | Recommended behavior |
|---|---|
| Declared optional variable, no answer | Interpolate as an empty string |
| Declared required variable, no answer yet | Show an incomplete live preview and request the answer before acceptance |
| Variable not declared anywhere | Report a validation error, without crashing the application |

For example:

If the user skips the question, the output becomes:

There is no crash.

Now consider a typo:

I would not silently replace the unknown `$proccessing` with an empty string.

The application should report:

This is a validation diagnostic, not an unhandled exception. The editor and application remain usable.

There is one existing exception: in a minimal template with no explicit `ASK` or `CHOOSE` declarations, variables discovered through interpolation intentionally create implicit questions.

### Do we need a global missing-variable policy?

You suggested something like:

Or:

This could be useful eventually, but I would avoid it in version 1.

The combination of `ASK REQUIRED`, `ASK OPTIONAL`, implicit question discovery, and separate validation errors already provides predictable behavior.

A configurable policy introduces another layer of semantics that every template author must understand.

**Recommendation Q5: Use the three fixed behaviors above. Do not introduce global missing-variable configuration yet.**

ChatGPTSat, Oct 24, 9:49 AM

I think we should restart the design, not just simplify the existing specification.

The problem is that we started designing a programming language for controlling a questionnaire. But the original PowerShell tool succeeded because it helped you **compose text through examples and guided questions**. The questionnaire was an implementation detail.

We gradually made the implementation detail the center of the language.

I would reverse that.

**The document should be the primary object. The questions, choices, and guidance should exist only to help the user fill in that document.**

That gives us a much simpler foundation.

</pressable>
</box>
<text inline>.</text>
</row>
</box>
{#each fields.filter(f=>f.id===focused) as f}
<box key={f.id} gap={2}>
<text weight="medium">{f.hint}</text>
<text size="sm" color="secondary">Example: {f.example}</text>
<input value={f.value} onChange={f.set} placeholder={f.example}/>
<row justify="end">
<button color="secondary" variant="outline" size="sm" onClick={()=>f.set(f.example)}>Use example</button>
</row>
</box>
{/each}
<divider color="subtle"/>
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text size="xs" color="secondary">Select a placeholder to edit its value.</text>
<button size="sm" onClick={()=>GenUI.copy(`Takes ${input}, ${processing}, and produces ${result}.`)}><icon name="copy" size="sm"/> Copy text</button>
</row>
</box>

This is a small interactive approximation of the editor.

The actual application would support editing ordinary text directly, inserting placeholders at the cursor, selecting them, and filling them through a nearby guidance panel.

The important difference from the original PowerShell wizard is that there is no mandatory question sequence. You can answer the third question first, change the first answer later, or rewrite the surrounding text whenever you want.

The text is always available.

## B00. A minimal DSL

I would start with this:

That's the entire template.

There are essentially four concepts:

`TEMPLATE` identifies the document, `TEXT` contains the resulting text, `{{$variable}}` represents an editable placeholder, and `$variable:` optionally provides guidance for that placeholder.

`HINT` and `EXAMPLE` are simply annotations.

No `ASK`, `REQUIRED`, `OPTIONAL`, `OUTPUT`, `COMPOSE`, `JOIN`, `WHEN`, `ELSE`, or `FOR EACH`.

We don't need those constructs to reproduce the first useful PowerShell experience.

In fact, we can go even shorter.

That is a valid template too.

The application discovers the placeholders and lets the user fill them. Adding hints and examples is optional.

This is what progressive complexity should mean: **the minimum valid template is almost identical to the text you want to produce.**

## C00. Choices should select documents, not execute conditions

I think this is where we took the biggest wrong turn with the earlier DSL.

We introduced `CHOOSE`, `OPTION`, `WHEN`, `ELSE`, conditional rendering, and eventually structured composition.

But our original use case was much simpler: you wanted to choose between five ways of describing a project.

Why not model those as five alternative text versions?

Now the application displays a version selector.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
{@body const [version,setVersion] = DIL.useState("Input + processing + output")}
<text weight="medium">Project description</text>
<radio-group direction="col" value={version} onChange={setVersion}>
<radio value="Input + processing + output">Input + processing + output</radio>
<radio value="Features + technology">Features + technology</radio>
<radio value="Problem + solution">Problem + solution</radio>
</radio-group>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} gap={1}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">CURRENT VERSION</text>
{#if version==="Input + processing + output"}
Takes [input], [processing], and produces [result].
{:else if version==="Features + technology"}
A [type] built with [technology], featuring [feature].
{:else}
Helps [audience] [problem] by [solution].
{/if}
</box>
<caption>Previously entered values would remain available when switching versions.</caption>
</box>

This covers the primary case without any conditional programming language.

A `VERSION` is simply another text document belonging to the same template.

The application selects one. Answers are stored by placeholder name, so changing versions does not erase them. If two versions use the same placeholder, they can reuse its value.

For a bug-report reply, we could use the same mechanism:

No conditions. No branching expressions. No special choice-variable semantics.

The application handles switching versions. The DSL declares the alternatives.

This covers one decision dimension per template, which I consider a reasonable first-version restriction. We should test that restriction before introducing nested decisions.

## D00. The editor is responsible for interactivity

This is the architectural change I would make.

Previously, the language specified what the application should do:

Under the new approach, the language specifies what exists:

The application already knows how to display questions, select placeholders, maintain answers, and update text.

We don't need authors to program those behaviors repeatedly.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Template definition**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Text, placeholders, guidance, versions</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Reactive document model**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Selected version + stored placeholder values</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<grid columns={2} gap={2}>
<grid-item>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} gap={1} align="center">
<icon name="file-pen-line" color="secondary"/>
**Document editor**
<text color="secondary" size="xs" textAlign="center">Live text and editable placeholders</text>
</box>
</grid-item>
<grid-item>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} gap={1} align="center">
<icon name="message-square-text" color="secondary"/>
**Guidance panel**
<text color="secondary" size="xs" textAlign="center">Hints, examples, and values</text>
</box>
</grid-item>
</grid>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Rendered text**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Copy, paste, or export</text>
</box>
</box>

What happens when someone clicks a placeholder is application behavior, not DSL behavior.

What happens when someone switches versions is application behavior, not DSL behavior.

What happens when someone leaves a placeholder empty is also mostly application behavior. The editor can keep the placeholder visibly marked, while the plain-text renderer substitutes an empty string and warns before copying if values are missing.

This avoids the configurable missing-variable policies we were beginning to discuss.

## E00. Initial implementation boundaries

I would deliberately keep the first language small.

| Design Question | Decision for the new prototype |
|---|---|
| What is the primary artifact? | An editable text document |
| How are placeholders expressed? | `{{$name}}` |
| How are placeholders described? | `$name: Question`, with optional hints and examples |
| Must every placeholder have a declaration? | No |
| How are different descriptions selected? | `VERSION` |
| Can answers be entered in any order? | Yes |
| Does editing an answer immediately affect the text? | Yes |
| Are previously entered answers retained? | Yes |
| Can the user edit ordinary text directly? | Yes |
| Are source newlines preserved? | Yes, with common indentation removed |
| Are conditional expressions supported? | Not initially |
| Are loops or functions supported? | No |
| Are fragments and composition rules supported? | No |
| Are static types required? | No |
| Does the DSL control the user interface? | No |

We can still build a proper parser. It will just parse a much smaller language.

The initial AST might only need `Template`, `Version`, `Text`, `Placeholder`, and `Guidance` nodes.

That is closer to the kind of domain-specific language you originally wanted.

---

## What I would do differently this time

I would not start by writing another 18-section language specification.

I would first build a small prototype of the editor using the five original PowerShell description styles. It would support direct text editing, placeholders, hints, examples, and switching versions without losing answers.

Then we would try creating several templates ourselves, especially ones that are slightly different from project descriptions.

When a real template becomes awkward or impossible to express, that is evidence that the DSL needs another capability.

For example, if multiple independently selectable sections become a frequent requirement, we can examine whether `VERSION` should support independent groups. But we should not invent nested conditions, expression grammar, variable scopes, and branch resolution rules before demonstrating that those are needed.

**My strongest recommendation is to treat GTL as a document format with interactive placeholders, not as a scripting language for questionnaires.** Its value would come from how little a template author needs to learn, while the application provides the sophisticated editing experience underneath.

## 1. Root cause identified

The failure comes from a Windows CRLF line-ending bug in the PowerShell script.

Your uploaded `rss.xml` contains a valid publication date on line 9:

```
<lastBuildDate>Fri, 23 Oct 2026 12:00:00 GMT</lastBuildDate>
```

The original script searches for that line using:

```
$datePattern = '(?m)^(?<indent>[ \t]*)<lastBuildDate>[^<]*</lastBuildDate>[ \t]*$'
```

With Windows CRLF endings, the line ends in `\r\n`. The expression allows spaces and tabs before `$`, but not `\r`. In .NET's regex engine, that means the pattern fails to match the otherwise valid line.

The fix is:

```
$datePattern = '(?m)^[ \t]*<lastBuildDate>[^<]*</lastBuildDate>[ \t]*\r?$'
```

Your RSS marker was present, and the RSS content was not the problem. The bug was in the script's handling of line endings.

The existing script also performs this validation after collecting the entire project description, which explains why you lost several minutes of input before discovering the error.

I'm addressing both defects, along with the requested draft recovery.

## 2. Updated PowerShell script

Download Add-Project.zip

Contains the revised Add-Project.ps1

The updated script includes:

- Preflight validation: Checks the selected HTML section, insertion points, RSS chronology, XML validity, and duplicate project entries before requesting descriptions.
- CRLF compatibility: Fixes the failing RSS date expression.
- Draft recovery: Saves entered publication values to a project-specific JSON file under the Windows temporary directory.
- Automatic restoration: Previously entered values appear as defaults when you run the script again.
- Detailed diagnostics: Reports the affected file, error explanation, and nearby source lines when validation fails.
- No repository backups: Git remains responsible for version history.

### 3. Draft recovery

The draft filename is derived from the normalized project directory using SHA-256:

```
%TEMP%\add-project-<24-character-hash>.json
```

Only publication-related answers are retained: title, category, description style, individual description fields, final description, and landing path.

Answers are saved as they are entered, and the draft is retained after successful publication or an ordinary failure. Technical confirmations are not stored.

### 4. Validation results

I checked the supplied files and confirmed that all five HTML sections are identifiable, the 147 RSS items are in descending publication-date order, and the failed project's entry is not already present.

The corrected date expression matches your CRLF RSS file.

Testing limitation: I verified the matching and file structure but could not execute the full interactive PowerShell workflow because PowerShell is unavailable in this environment. Runtime validation on Windows remains necessary.

Replace the existing `docs/Add-Project.ps1`, then run:

```

```

Your answers from the failed run cannot be recovered automatically because the earlier script never saved them. Subsequent runs will use the new recovery mechanism.

Reasoning / progress:
I’ll inspect the ZIP’s component tree, styles, Shadow DOM usage, and interaction/state flow. Then I’ll separate what the existing project actually does from the UX patterns worth reusing for the template editor. I won’t draft the new application specification yet.

One important finding: the project uses Web Components, but the inspected UI components render into the light DOM rather than attaching Shadow DOM roots. Its strongest reusable patterns are the document-like message layout, sidebar navigation, event-driven components, contextual right-side information panel, and restrained background treatment. I’m checking the CSS and state handling next.

# UI/UX analysis: SovietRDTL as inspiration for the template editor

I inspected the supplied `sovet-rdtl-0.13.1-source` archive, including its Web Components, application state, CSS, responsive layout, background assets, and interaction handling.

**The strongest idea to reuse is the combination of a conversation-history layout and a document-centered reading experience.** For our application, each historical item would represent an editable document rather than a conversation. The center would contain the complete document, with inline placeholders and contextual guidance instead of a chat input.

We can preserve much of the reference application's visual character without reproducing its chat mechanics.

## 1. What the reference project actually contains

The reference application uses a fairly small, dependency-free frontend built with standard JavaScript ES modules, Custom Elements, CSS, and browser APIs.

Its main UI modules are:

| Source component | Current responsibility | Potential use in our application |
|---|---|---|
| `sov-app` | Application shell, event coordination, navigation, dialogs | Main application shell |
| `sov-sidebar` | Chat history, selection, creation, deletion | Vertical document tabs and history |
| `sov-chat` | Scrollable message history | Document workspace |
| `sov-message` | Individual message presentation | Editable document card |
| `sov-origin-panel` | Contextual information in a right-side panel | Placeholder guidance panel |
| `sov-backdrop` | Decorative background images | Document atmosphere |
| `sov-composer` | Message input, drafts, attachments, recording | Not needed as a chat composer |
| `sov-audio` | Audio playback | Not relevant initially |

A technical distinction worth preserving: **the source uses Web Components, but it does not use Shadow DOM for these UI components.** The components extend `HTMLElement`, render into the light DOM, and share `styles.css`.

We can introduce Shadow DOM in our application, but we should treat that as our architectural choice rather than something already demonstrated by this project.

## 2. Visual model and layout

The reference application's desktop structure is approximately:

<caption>Schematic reconstruction from the source code and CSS, not a screenshot.</caption>

The real implementation uses a 256 px desktop sidebar, a scrollable central region, and a message column with a maximum width of 840 px. Its primary message text is comparatively large: 20 px on desktop, 19 px on mobile, with an even larger opening message.

This produces a reading-oriented interface rather than a dense dashboard.

For our editor, I would preserve the reading column and sidebar proportions, but introduce an optional third region for contextual guidance.

### Proposed layout

<box border radius="lg" padding={2} gap={2}>
