The essential change is that the middle region is no longer a transcript of messages. It is an editable document that feels like a message in a historical conversation.

---

## 3. The vertical document history

The current sidebar is a good starting point.

In `src/ui/components/sov-sidebar.js`, the component creates a new-chat button, a navigation region, and rows for previously created conversations. The selected item receives a `.selected` class and `aria-current="page"`.

The app service provides selection, creation, deletion, and a subscription mechanism for updates.

For our application, we can rename these concepts:

| SovietRDTL concept | Template editor concept |
|---|---|
| Chat | Document |
| New chat | New document |
| Chat title | Document title |
| Chat history | Document collection |
| Selected chat | Active document |
| Chat messages | Document content |
| Message draft | Document edits |
| Conversation background | Document background |
| Clear history | Manage or clear documents |

I would use a vertically arranged document list, not literal browser-style tabs along the top.

Each item should have a stable identity, its own template selection, placeholder values, edited content, and change history.

Clicking a document should restore its editor state. It should not restart a questionnaire.

The list should eventually support search, renaming, sorting by recent activity, duplication, and deletion. These are application features, not requirements that individual templates must declare.

## 4. Creating a new document

Your proposed "New Template" action is the right starting point, with one terminology distinction: the user creates a document *from* a template. Editing a template definition is a separate authoring operation.

I would use **New Document** for the primary action, and reserve **Edit Template** for the template authoring interface.

When the user clicks New Document, a new document opens immediately in the central workspace. At the top of that document, there is a template selector with a meaningful description and example.

For example:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
{@body const [kind,setKind] = DIL.useState("ipo")}
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text weight="medium">New document</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Choose a writing style</text>
</row>
<radio-group direction="col" value={kind} onChange={setKind}>
<radio value="purpose">Purpose + function</radio>
<radio value="problem">Problem + solution</radio>
<radio value="ipo">Input + processing + output</radio>
<radio value="features">Features + technology</radio>
<radio value="profile">Structured project profile</radio>
</radio-group>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} gap={2}>
<text size="xs" weight="medium" color="secondary">DOCUMENT PREVIEW</text>
{#if kind==="ipo"}
Takes [input], [processing], and produces [result].
{:else if kind==="purpose"}
A [tool] that [action] to [benefit].
{:else if kind==="problem"}
Helps [audience] [solve problem] by [approach].
{:else if kind==="features"}
A [tool] built with [technology], featuring [feature 1] and [feature 2].
{:else}
A [tool] for [audience], providing [workflow] through [detail].
{/if}
</box>
<caption>Illustration of the template-selection step. This is not a running instance of the uploaded project.</caption>
</box>

Once selected, the text becomes the editable document. The user can revisit the selector and change the version.

Values shared between versions remain available. Values unique to another version remain remembered but do not clutter the active document.

This is the same successful behavior that motivated the original PowerShell tool, but without requiring a step-by-step CLI wizard.

## 5. Inline placeholders and contextual guidance

This is the central interaction we need to design carefully.

The uploaded project already has a useful model for contextual information. Every eligible assistant message has an information button. Clicking it opens `sov-origin-panel`, which loads explanatory content into a right-side panel.

Its relevant behavior includes remembering which control opened the panel, returning keyboard focus when it closes, and preventing stale asynchronous responses from overwriting newer content.

Those are useful patterns, but **I would change the panel from a modal dialog to a persistent, nonmodal editing companion**.

A modal is suitable for inspecting an explanation. It is less suitable when the user needs to read a hint and write simultaneously.

### Proposed interaction

When the user clicks or moves the caret into a placeholder, the guidance panel should display the corresponding question, expected grammatical form, hints, and examples.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
{@body const fieldData = [{id:"input",question:"What does the user provide?",hint:"Describe the original data, file, or information. Use a short noun phrase.",example:"pasted JSON",form:"Noun phrase"},{id:"processing",question:"What happens to the input?",hint:"Start with a present-tense verb that fits naturally after the input.",example:"checks it for syntax errors",form:"Verb phrase"},{id:"result",question:"What does the application produce?",hint:"Describe the resulting data or artifact as a noun phrase.",example:"a readable formatted version",form:"Noun phrase"}]}
{@body const [active,setActive] = DIL.useState("processing")}
{@body const [values,setValues] = DIL.useState({input:"pasted JSON",processing:"",result:"a readable formatted version"})}
{@body const field = fieldData.find(f=>f.id===active)}
<row align="center" justify="between">
<text weight="medium">Document editor</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Editing example</text>
</row>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} gap={2}>
<text size="xs" weight="medium" color="secondary">DESCRIPTION</text>
<row wrap="wrap" align="center" gap={1}>
<text inline>Takes</text>
<pressable inline onClick={()=>setActive("input")} background={active==="input"?"surface-tertiary":undefined} radius="sm" padding={{x:1,y:"2px"}} border={{size:1,color:active==="input"?"strong":"subtle"}}><text size="sm">{values.input||"[input]"}</text></pressable>
<text inline>,</text>
<pressable inline onClick={()=>setActive("processing")} background={active==="processing"?"surface-tertiary":undefined} radius="sm" padding={{x:1,y:"2px"}} border={{size:1,color:active==="processing"?"strong":"subtle"}}><text size="sm">{values.processing||"[processing]"}</text></pressable>
<text inline>, and produces</text>
<pressable inline onClick={()=>setActive("result")} background={active==="result"?"surface-tertiary":undefined} radius="sm" padding={{x:1,y:"2px"}} border={{size:1,color:active==="result"?"strong":"subtle"}}><text size="sm">{values.result||"[result]"}</text></pressable>
<text inline>.</text>
</row>
</box>
<box border={{size:1,color:"default"}} radius="md" padding={3} gap={2}>
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text weight="medium" size="sm">{field.question}</text>
<text color="secondary" size="xs">{field.form}</text>
</row>
<text color="secondary" size="sm">{field.hint}</text>
<input value={values[active]} placeholder={field.example} onChange={v=>setValues(old=>({...old,[active]:v}))}/>
<row justify="between" align="center">
<text size="xs" color="secondary">Example: {field.example}</text>
<button size="sm" color="secondary" variant="outline" onClick={()=>setValues(old=>({...old,[active]:field.example}))}>Use example</button>
</row>
</box>
<box gap={1}>
<text color="secondary" size="xs" weight="medium">LIVE RESULT</text>
<text size="sm">{`Takes ${values.input}, ${values.processing||"[processing]"}, and produces ${values.result}.`}</text>
</box>
<caption>Interactive illustration of placeholder selection and contextual guidance.</caption>
</box>

In the final application, the guidance would ordinarily appear beside the editable sentence rather than underneath it. This example stacks the regions to fit the conversation.

### Guidance placement and focus behavior

The panel should not cover the user's current editing location. On a wide desktop, it can occupy a docked right-hand region with independent scrolling. On a narrower screen, it can appear in an adjustable lower panel or replace unused auxiliary space.

A hint should remain visible while the user types. It should not vanish after a timeout, close when the user copies an example, or jump unpredictably to another location.

I would support three states: following the active placeholder, pinned to a specific placeholder, and collapsed. A pinned panel is particularly useful when the user wants to reference one example while editing another part of the document.

The panel can show multiple examples, and its text should remain selectable. Copying text from the panel must not clear the current editor selection or accidentally change the current placeholder.

On mobile, the active paragraph needs priority over the hint. The application should use the visual viewport to keep the focused text visible above the on-screen keyboard. A guidance panel should never force the user to scroll continuously between the input and its explanation.

### Placeholder presentation

I would avoid presenting placeholders as large form fields embedded in the sentence. That makes ordinary prose look like a form.

Instead, placeholders could have a restrained visual treatment: a subtle underline, a slightly different text color, and a stronger outline only when selected.

A filled placeholder should continue to look like text, not a button.

An unfilled placeholder can display its identifier in a distinguishable style. It should be obvious that the field still needs an answer, particularly before copying the final result.

## 6. Direct document editing

You mentioned Emacs, and I think the relevant property is not the appearance of Emacs but its interaction model: the text itself is the workspace.

A user should be able to put the caret into the document and write. The template should assist editing rather than impose a separate sequence of forms.

This is more demanding than the reference app's message rendering. `sov-message` displays content in a regular DOM text node with `white-space: pre-wrap`. It does not provide a rich-text editor.

Our application will need a new editing subsystem.

I would use a structured document model underneath the editor, where ordinary text and placeholders are distinct nodes.

For example, the sentence:

could be represented internally as:

This matters because the user can change ordinary text without changing the definition or identity of a placeholder.

It also allows the same placeholder to appear in multiple locations and update consistently.

For a first prototype, I would evaluate a `contenteditable` document with stable, noneditable placeholder nodes and a dedicated placeholder editing control. However, I would test keyboard navigation, selection, paste, IME composition, undo, and cursor preservation before committing to that implementation. A simplistic approach that rebuilds the entire document DOM on every keystroke would cause focus and selection problems.

I would separate the underlying document state from rendered DOM elements. Shadow DOM should not become an obstacle to selections spanning ordinary text and placeholders.

### Essential editor interactions

The experience should support placing the caret anywhere in ordinary text, selecting and replacing phrases, inserting and removing placeholders, pasting text, moving through placeholders with the keyboard, and undoing edits.

Clicking a placeholder should focus the corresponding value editor or place it into an inline editing mode. Changes should update the document without moving the user's selection unexpectedly.

When a placeholder appears twice, editing either occurrence should update the shared value.

We also need to distinguish editing a document instance from modifying the source template. Rewriting "Takes" into "Accepts" in one document should not rewrite every document created from that template.

## 7. Live result and rich-text copying

I agree that the result should appear at the bottom of the same document.

I would not put it into a separate conversation message or modal.

A document could be organized as:

For a longer document, the result can be further down the page, with an optional Copy action also available in the document header.

### Rich-text clipboard support

I would support both `text/plain` and `text/html` clipboard representations.

Plain-text copying provides compatibility with terminals, source editors, and text inputs. Rich-text copying preserves semantic formatting, such as paragraphs, headings, bold text, italics, and lists, when pasted into applications that support HTML clipboard content.

The application should construct a sanitized export from the document model, not copy arbitrary editor DOM. Placeholder controls, guidance decorations, selection outlines, and background images should never appear in the copied result.

A crucial distinction is that copying as rich text should preserve the actual document formatting, not forcibly reproduce the application's dark appearance. Otherwise, text pasted into a light-themed email or document could become unreadable.

For a browser implementation, the primary path would use the Clipboard API with both `text/plain` and `text/html` representations, with a plain-text fallback where rich clipboard access is unavailable.

## 8. Dark theme and background assets

The reference project's visual identity comes from a combination of nearly black surfaces, restrained typography, subtle color variation, and atmospheric background imagery.

I examined the background assets included in the archive. There are 20 distinct room scenes, each with a desktop and mobile WebP variant.

They include dimly lit desks, rooms, windows, lamps, furniture, and other interiors. Many have relatively dark, low-detail central regions, which help foreground text remain legible.

The current application uses `sov-backdrop` to render these images. The component loads the selected background asynchronously, caches up to three image entries, uses a mobile-specific source at widths below 768 px, and falls back to a solid background if loading fails.

The CSS places the background behind the content, prevents it from receiving pointer events, and applies dark gradients over it.

This is worth reusing conceptually.

### Suggested visual direction for our application

I would retain the dark interface but shift the atmosphere from domestic interiors toward quiet writing spaces.

<grid columns={2} gap={3}>

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Muted structure, low contrast, suitable for longer writing.</text>
</grid-item>

<AsyncImage query="dark minimal computer workstation evening monitor ambient blue grey lighting mostly black photographic background no text no person" aspectRatio="5:3"/>

<caption>Visual references illustrating possible directions. These are not assets from the supplied project or finalized artwork.</caption>

I would generate fewer backgrounds than the original project's 20 scenes. Six carefully chosen backgrounds would be sufficient for an initial application, including at least one purely abstract choice and a plain black default.

Each background should be optimized for desktop and mobile. The central reading region must remain legible regardless of the image.

The background is decorative, not an interactive element. It should not alter document layout, editing behavior, or exported HTML.

### Color system

The reference CSS provides a useful starting point.

| UI element | Reference color | Suggested approach |
|---|---|---|
| Main background | `#101113` | Retain |
| Sidebar | `#0d0e10` | Retain |
| Raised surface | `#27282b` | Retain or slightly adjust |
| Main text | `#f2f1ef` | Retain |
| Secondary text | `#b2b2b7` | Retain |
| Divider | `#343539` | Retain |
| Keyboard focus | `#d1d9ec` | Retain |
| Placeholder accent | New | Muted amber or cool blue |

The original application also distinguishes different message tones through typography and border treatments, not color alone.

We can apply the same principle to editor states. The focused placeholder, an unfilled placeholder, a completed value, and an error should be distinguishable through shape, underline, or other styling as well as color.

## 9. Component architecture and communication

The source has a particularly useful application-level communication pattern.

Its `src/ui/dom.js` exposes a small `emit()` utility that creates bubbling `CustomEvent` objects with `composed: true`. Child components emit semantic events, while `sov-app` coordinates application actions.

For example, the sidebar emits `select-chat` and `new-chat`, while the application component responds by calling the chat service. The service then notifies subscribers, which causes the UI to update.

This is a reasonable foundation for our app.

I would use a similar separation:

These are proposed Custom Element names, not components already present in the archive.

### Proposed event flow

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={1}>
<box background="surface-secondary" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Document editor**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">User edits placeholder or surrounding text</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Application state**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Apply change to active document</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>
<box border radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
**Dependent state**
<text color="secondary" size="xs">Update affected placeholders, result, and guidance</text>
</box>
<row justify="center"><icon name="arrow-down" color="secondary"/></row>

<grid-item>

<text color="secondary" size="xs" textAlign="center">Persist when enabled</text>
</box>
</grid-item>

</box>

Proposed events could include `document-select`, `document-create`, `template-select`, `placeholder-focus`, `placeholder-change`, `document-change`, and `copy-result`.

Custom events are suitable for communicating user intentions out of components. For sharing state between components, I would retain one application-level state model rather than having every component listen to every other component.

That prevents a tangled event system.

### Shadow DOM strategy

Since you want to use Shadow DOM, I would adopt it selectively.

The sidebar, settings, template selector, and guidance panel can be independently encapsulated Web Components with Shadow DOM and component-specific styles.

The document editor requires special consideration. Text selection across placeholder elements, rich text, clipboard handling, composition events, and keyboard shortcuts are more complicated than ordinary form controls.

I would prototype the editor in light DOM first, then compare that with a Shadow DOM implementation before establishing the boundary.

If we use Shadow DOM, CSS custom properties and `::part()` can expose supported styling points without allowing arbitrary external CSS to affect internal component structure.

The editor should not be forced into Shadow DOM simply for architectural consistency.

## 10. Document persistence and editing history

The reference application currently uses `localStorage`, not IndexedDB.

Its `src/adapters/local-storage.js` serializes application state, validates it, and detects conflicting updates from another tab. The chat service maintains subscribers and saves changes as conversations are created or modified.

Its composer also keeps per-chat drafts in memory.

These are useful examples of separating application state from the persistence mechanism.

For our application, I would use your proposed Dexie-based IndexedDB adapter as the primary persistent store, but retain a storage-independent document model.

### Storage settings

I would distinguish three modes rather than treating Local Storage and IndexedDB as interchangeable.

| Mode | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Do not persist | Documents remain in memory only and disappear when the session ends |
| Browser localStorage | Simple browser-local persistence for smaller documents |
| IndexedDB through Dexie | Primary persistent storage for documents, templates, and revision history |

The choice should be accessible from Settings.

The selected mode should be clearly communicated. Switching modes raises an important question about whether existing documents should be migrated, copied, or left in their previous store. That belongs in the eventual specification.

### History matters more than simple recovery

You previously raised the idea of saving entered form values after a failed PowerShell run.

For this application, I would go further and distinguish autosaving from revision history.

Autosave protects the current working state. Revision history allows the user to inspect or restore earlier states.

For example, changing a placeholder should update the document immediately. Changes can be debounced and written to the selected storage adapter. Significant editing milestones can create revision snapshots.

An illustrative document model:

Revision snapshots would be associated with the document ID rather than embedded indefinitely in the current document record.

We should preserve both the selected template version and values entered in other versions. Switching templates should not discard work.

One additional UX detail worth keeping from the reference: when saving fails, the user should retain the in-memory document and receive an actionable message. A storage error must not wipe the editor.

## 11. What assets and UI resources should we prepare?

For the eventual implementation, I would prepare the following assets and reusable design resources.

| Asset | Initial scope | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Background artwork | 6 original themes, desktop/mobile variants | Atmospheric dark workspace |
| Color tokens | One dark theme with semantic states | Consistent surfaces, text, focus, and placeholder styling |
| Icon library | Small set of SVG icons | New, edit, copy, history, settings, close, pin |
| Placeholder styles | Empty, filled, focused, invalid | Inline editing affordances |
| Guidance designs | Docked, pinned, compact/mobile | Nonintrusive contextual help |
| Document layouts | Short snippet, longer note, rich-text document | Test editing at different lengths |
| Sample templates | Original five PowerShell description styles plus quick reply and bug report | Real UX fixtures |
| Copy/export styles | Plain text and semantic HTML | Portable clipboard output |

I would not generate dozens of backgrounds, illustrations, or component variations before testing the editor. The artwork should support the writing experience rather than become the project's primary development effort.

The most valuable early visual assets would actually be two editor mockups: one for desktop with a persistent guidance panel, and one for mobile with an active placeholder and the keyboard visible.

## 12. Responsive behavior and accessibility

The reference app changes navigation behavior at 767 px. On small screens, the sidebar becomes an off-canvas panel, the header exposes a menu button, and the main region becomes inert while the navigation is open.

It also traps focus inside the mobile menu, restores focus when the menu closes, handles safe-area insets, and respects reduced-motion preferences.

I would retain these principles.

For the template editor, desktop and mobile interaction models should differ only where the available space requires it.

On desktop, a three-region layout is viable: document navigation, editor, and guidance.

On mobile, the document occupies the main screen. Navigation becomes a drawer, while guidance appears in a dedicated compact region that can be opened without obscuring the active caret. The full document remains scrollable.

The interface should be operable with a keyboard. Moving between placeholders should have predictable focus order, while ordinary text selection and editing shortcuts should continue working.

I would also avoid automatically scrolling the document to the bottom whenever its result changes. The reference chat scrolls when messages are added and the user is near the bottom. That behavior is appropriate for a chat, but would be disruptive in a document editor where the user might be editing the middle of a long paragraph.

## 13. What to reuse, adapt, and exclude

| Reference capability | Decision for our application |
|---|---|
| Dark shell and typography | Reuse visual principles |
| Vertical chat history | Adapt into document history |
| Large, readable message content | Adapt into document editing |
| Right-side explanatory dialog | Convert to nonmodal guidance |
| Decorative image backgrounds | Reuse the layering and loading model |
| Custom Elements | Reuse the architectural approach |
| Custom event communication | Reuse |
| Local state service | Adapt to document state |
| localStorage persistence | Replace or supplement with configurable storage adapters |
| Per-chat draft handling | Adapt to per-document draft and revisions |
| Text message composer | Remove |
| Assistant response engine | Remove |
| Typing delay and thinking animation | Remove |
| Microphone and media attachments | Exclude initially |
| Message alignment by speaker | Replace with unified document layout |

The key is to reuse architectural patterns without inheriting the reference application's domain model.

---

## 14. Recommended first UX prototype

Before writing the complete application specification, I would build or mock one representative experience.

A user opens the application and sees a dark workspace with a left document history. They click New Document, select Input + processing + output, and immediately see the complete editable sentence.

They click `$processing`, and a guidance panel displays the question, hints, and examples. They enter a value and see the sentence update immediately. They can edit the ordinary text around it, switch to another template version without losing answers, scroll to the result, and copy it as plain or rich text.

They can leave the document, return through the vertical history, and recover their previous working state according to the selected persistence setting.

This prototype would validate the most difficult part of the application: whether guided writing can feel like normal document editing rather than form completion.

**The main principle I would carry into the specification is that the document is the interface.** The sidebar organizes documents, the guidance panel explains placeholders, the state layer preserves work, and the template language defines only the text, variables, and useful authoring guidance. Everything else should be handled by the application itself.

# Source-code review: architecture for the static GTL application

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Reference: `browser-extension-src.zip` | October 2026</text>

The archive provides several useful implementation references: vendored JavaScript libraries, Dexie-backed IndexedDB, a centralized background worker, typed runtime messages, settings schemas, and reusable Shadow DOM components.

I recommend using it as a **source of established coding patterns**, not as a project skeleton to copy unchanged. The browser extension has accumulated considerably more infrastructure than our guided document editor needs.

The target stack can be:

| Area | Technology |
|---|---|
| Application language | TypeScript 7 |
| Build and development | Bun |
| Runtime | Browser-native JavaScript |
| Interface | Native Web Components, primarily Shadow DOM |
| Persistent document storage | IndexedDB through vendored Dexie |
| Background execution | Browser worker with feature-owned handlers |
| Template definitions | Static text files loaded on demand |
| Runtime package dependencies | None installed from npm |
| Development dependencies | Allowed for type checking, testing, and build tooling |
| Deployment | Static HTML, JavaScript, CSS, and assets |

**TypeScript version:** As of October 25, 2026, the npm registry identifies TypeScript **7.0.2** as the latest stable release, with 7.1.0 in beta. I would pin TypeScript 7.0.2 for the initial implementation rather than use a beta. <Cite ref="turn533049search2"/>

Bun supports TypeScript bundling, browser-targeted output, and multi-entry builds. Its bundler does not replace TypeScript's semantic type checking, so those remain separate build steps. <Cite refs={["turn533049search3","turn533049search1"]}/>

There is one architectural distinction we need to make immediately.

## Extension background worker versus website background worker

The reference project uses a Chrome extension Manifest V3 service worker. Our application will be a normal static website, not an extension.

A website can register a service worker and communicate with it, but that worker is not a permanently running application backend. The browser may terminate it when idle and restart it for later events. It also has no DOM or `localStorage` access. <Cite refs={["turn320997search1","turn320997search3"]}/>

For our application, I recommend a **Dedicated Web Worker as the primary data and processing worker**, with a browser service worker reserved for future offline caching and application updates.

A Dedicated Worker can use IndexedDB and communicate with the main thread through `postMessage()`. <Cite ref="turn320997search8"/>

This preserves your feature-owned background processing architecture without making ordinary document editing depend on service-worker registration, activation, or restart behavior.

The rest of the review distinguishes what the extension already implements from the architecture I recommend for the website.

---

## 2. Vendor libraries: two patterns in the archive

The archive demonstrates two different forms of vendoring, and both are worth retaining.

### 2.1. Prebuilt third-party libraries

The extension keeps runtime-ready JavaScript under `src/content/libs/`.

| Exact file in archive | Version | Potential GTL use |
|---|---|---|
| `src/content/libs/dexie-4.2.0.js` | 4.2.0 | IndexedDB documents, revisions, and history |
| `src/content/libs/jszip-3.10.1.js` | 3.10.1 | Exporting multiple documents or a backup as ZIP |
| `src/content/libs/markdown-it-14.2.0.js` | 14.2.0 | Markdown preview and HTML rendering |
| `src/content/libs/turndown-7.0.0.js` | 7.0.0 | Converting HTML content into Markdown |
| `src/content/libs/readability-0.6.0.js` | 0.6.0 | Extracting articles; not needed initially |
| `src/content/libs/microlight-0.0.7-mod-2026-05-24.js` | Modified 0.0.7 | Syntax highlighting; optional |

The Dexie file is especially relevant. Its header identifies it as Dexie.js 4.2.0, distributed under Apache 2.0. It uses a UMD wrapper that exposes `globalThis.Dexie`.

The extension loads it inside its service worker using:

It then creates the database:

This is a workable vendoring pattern, although our modern module worker should not copy the classic `importScripts()` arrangement unchanged.

For our application, I recommend retaining the unmodified vendored JavaScript and wrapping its API in a typed adapter. We should test how Bun packages this UMD version for a module worker. If necessary, use a vendored ESM distribution instead.

### 2.2. Vendored editable TypeScript source

The more interesting example is:

`src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/`

This contains an entire reusable Web Components library, including source, build scripts, themes, component implementations, and documentation.

For example:

The source repeatedly establishes a useful ownership rule: generic library changes belong in the vendored library; application-specific behavior belongs in an adapter outside that library.

I would keep that rule.

For GTL, the corresponding structure could be:

Application-owned wrappers would live elsewhere, for example:

The wrapper creates a stable boundary. If a library is updated later, domain features do not have to change their imports or understand how that library is loaded.

Vendored distributions should retain their license and attribution files. The versions in the archive are verified as reference versions, not necessarily the latest versions available.

### 2.3. Should we vendor the entire RetroOS UI library?

I would not copy the entire library into the new application immediately.

It contains many components our editor does not need: movable windows, simulated browser panels, extensive menu infrastructure, window management, and bookmarklet-specific overlays.

However, several files are excellent references for building the interface:

| Reference file | What to study |
|---|---|
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/field.ts` | Labels, help text, invalid states, slots |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/input.ts` | Encapsulated input controls |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/textarea.ts` | Multiline inputs |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/tabs.ts` | Selection and accessible tabs |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/listbox.ts` | Option selection |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/dialog.ts` | Dialog behavior |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/toast.ts` | Transient notifications |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/core/styles.ts` | Shared Shadow DOM styling |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/core/define.ts` | Idempotent Custom Element registration |
| `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/core/sanitize.ts` | HTML sanitization |

For example, `field.ts` uses `attachShadow({ mode: "open" })`, named slots, CSS custom properties, and semantic attributes such as `help`, `error`, and `required`.

This approach fits our placeholder guidance panel.

The best reuse would be the **component design conventions**, with only the specific components we need.

---

## 3. Dexie and the data-access architecture

There are two particularly valuable data-layer references.

`src/background/service_worker.ts` owns database initialization, schema versions, and several record operations.

`src/background/tab_trail_service.ts` shows a cleaner boundary between domain logic and persistence.

### 3.1. What the current database does

The service worker opens a Dexie database named `lpBookmarksDb` and declares multiple schema versions. The visible tables support bookmarks, labels, URL signals, reminders, usage statistics, and Tab Trail records.

The important part is not those tables, but the pattern:

The schema is versioned and managed centrally.

For GTL, the corresponding conceptual tables could be:

| Table | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| `documents` | Saved document metadata and content |
| `revisions` | Historical document snapshots |
| `templates` | User-created or imported template definitions |
| `settings` | Application settings when using IndexedDB |
| `metadata` | Schema metadata and migration information |

I would not persist every keystroke as an individual revision. The editor should support fast undo/redo independently, while history stores meaningful recoverable states.

### 3.2. A better reference: Tab Trail's store interface

In `src/background/tab_trail_service.ts`, the service defines storage operations through an interface rather than scattering Dexie calls throughout the domain logic.

Its `createDexieTabTrailStore()` adapter implements operations such as:

The same file also includes an in-memory store implementation, which is useful for tests and environments where persistence is unavailable.

That is almost exactly the approach I would use for GTL.

A document feature could define:

Then provide implementations such as `DexieDocumentRepository`, `MemoryDocumentRepository`, and eventually `LocalStorageDocumentRepository`.

The feature depends on `DocumentRepository`, not Dexie.

This makes the storage setting meaningful without forcing the rest of the application to know which persistence mechanism is active.

### 3.3. Keep database schema ownership centralized

I would make one deliberate adjustment to your proposed feature architecture.

Features should own their repository interfaces and database operations, but a central database initializer should own the complete Dexie schema version.

Otherwise, separate features independently changing their database versions could create migration problems.

Each feature may export its table declarations for assembly, but the application must produce one coherent versioned database schema.

For document history, document edits and revision creation should use a single transaction when both must succeed together.

---

## 4. Settings: the strongest reusable subsystem

I consider this one of the most valuable parts of the supplied project.

The relevant files are:

`src/settings/settings_runtime.ts`

`src/settings/settings.ts`

`src/settings/settings.html`

`src/settings/settings.css`

### 4.1. Schema-driven settings

`settings_runtime.ts` defines a settings document with categories and individual setting definitions.

A typical setting resembles:

This is a good pattern.

Settings are not simply anonymous key-value pairs. They contain enough metadata for the UI to construct an appropriate control and explain its meaning.

The runtime also supports normalization, validation, schema versions, resetting individual settings, and resetting all settings.

### 4.2. Store overrides rather than complete copies of defaults

The source defines `DEFAULT_SETTINGS_DOCUMENT`, then persists only differences from that document.

The relevant functions include:

This is worth preserving.

If a default changes in a future release, users who never customized that setting receive the new default naturally. Explicit user overrides remain intact.

For our application, a setting might be:

The UI should be generated from the setting definition rather than independently implementing each setting's control.

### 4.3. Immediate propagation

In the extension, the settings runtime writes changes to `chrome.storage.local`.

The worker registers:

`src/background/service_worker.ts` also computes a signature from the relevant settings and skips repeated, identical updates.

This is significant: the settings model supports both initial configuration and live changes after the application has started.

For the static website, we need to replace Chrome's storage events with browser-native mechanisms.

I recommend an application settings service that notifies active components immediately, sends worker-relevant changes to the worker, and optionally broadcasts changes to other open tabs.

`BroadcastChannel` is suitable for cross-tab and worker notifications within its supported same-origin storage partition. <Cite ref="turn661484search0"/>

The result should be that changing the dark theme, guidance visibility, autosave behavior, or background image affects the running application without a reload.

### 4.4. An important storage limitation

You mentioned three persistence modes: do not persist, `localStorage`, and Dexie/IndexedDB.

A Dedicated Worker cannot directly access `localStorage`.

I would therefore use a storage coordinator instead of assuming every repository implementation runs in the worker.

The coordinator could route IndexedDB requests to the worker, while a browser-main-thread adapter handles `localStorage`. Memory-only mode could use in-memory repositories.

The rest of the application should still see the same repository contract.

This gives us a stable abstraction without incorrectly pretending that all browser storage APIs work in every execution context.

---

## 5. Background worker: feature-owned handlers

Your proposed vertical feature architecture is a direct improvement over the reference project's central worker.

### 5.1. What the extension currently does

The reference worker is:

`src/background/service_worker.ts`

It is approximately 135 KB of TypeScript source.

It imports several specialized services, including:

This is already partial modularization.

However, `service_worker.ts` still owns database setup, settings subscriptions, browser events, and a substantial message dispatcher.

Its `chrome.runtime.onMessage.addListener(...)` branches on message scopes, types, and actions, then forwards requests to the appropriate implementation.

A good example is its Tab Trail handling:

The architecture is moving in the direction you described, but the worker entry point still knows too much about individual features.

### 5.2. Proposed feature-based worker organization

I would make the worker entry point responsible only for initializing shared infrastructure, registering feature handlers, and dispatching messages.

The router would know that a feature handles a scope such as `documents`, but would not know how a document is stored.

A feature would register a handler resembling:

The worker entry point could then be almost entirely declarative:

This is illustrative proposed code, not code from the archive.

I would use static imports and registration. We do not need a dynamic plugin loader, dependency injection framework, or runtime discovery system.

The feature system is simply an organizational boundary.

### 5.3. Reuse the extension's message envelope

There is an especially good reference in:

`src/content/runtime_messaging.ts`

It defines a request envelope containing:

And a response with:

Its message client validates response shape and ensures the response request ID matches the original request.

I would adopt this concept almost unchanged, replacing `chrome.runtime.sendMessage` with browser Worker messaging.

For additional type safety, the new system can define a central operation contract so that `documents.save` has an explicitly typed payload and result.

Runtime validation is still necessary because TypeScript types do not survive structured-clone messaging.

I would also add a protocol version and timeouts for worker failures. A request that never receives a response should not leave the UI indefinitely displaying a loading state.

### 5.4. Feature boundaries

I would interpret your domain-driven design idea pragmatically.

A feature should own a meaningful user capability, not necessarily one UI component or one database table.

For example, the Documents feature owns document creation, editing state, naming, and deletion. History owns recoverable revisions. Templates owns template loading and selection.

The worker does not need to be the owner of every behavior.

Typing into the editor should update main-thread state immediately. The worker can perform persistence and heavier transformations asynchronously.

This avoids introducing worker round trips into every keystroke.

## 6. Proposed project structure

This is the directory structure I would use as the initial architectural reference.

This is a proposed organization, not the extension's existing tree.

I would allow small features to omit `worker/`, `storage/`, or even `ui/` entirely. A folder should exist because the feature needs a responsibility, not because a convention requires every possible layer.

An important rule is that features should not freely import each other's internal files. When two features must communicate, they should do so through a small public contract or a shared domain operation.

For example, History may consume document-change events, but should not manipulate the private internals of `DocumentEditor`.

## 7. Template loading for a static website

Your recent DSL redesign makes the template loader much simpler.

We do not need runtime template discovery, npm packages, a template registry service, or a programming-language module loader.

The application can ship an explicit manifest:

The application loads `manifest.json` at startup, displays the available templates, and fetches the selected `.gtl` file when needed.

Templates should be cached after loading.

I recommend generating the manifest during the build from the source template directory. The deployed application still receives a normal static JSON manifest, but authors do not have to keep two separate lists synchronized manually.

The browser would fetch these assets from the same project directory.

This fits GitHub Pages and other static hosting environments.

Imported user templates can live in IndexedDB separately from the bundled templates. That gives us a useful distinction between built-in templates and user-owned templates without expanding the DSL itself.

## 8. TypeScript 7 and Bun configuration

The archive contains a useful TypeScript configuration in:

`src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/tsconfig.json`

It targets `ES2025`, uses `module: "Preserve"` and `moduleResolution: "bundler"`, and enables strict checking, isolated modules, verbatim module syntax, and unchecked-index safeguards.

I would use those settings as a reference, but with distinct browser and worker configurations.

### Recommended TypeScript baseline

This is a suggested common baseline, not a complete final `tsconfig.json`.

The UI configuration should include the DOM libraries, while the worker configuration should use the Web Worker libraries. Separate type-checking configurations avoid allowing DOM-only APIs accidentally in worker code.

I would also prefer explicit interfaces and discriminated unions over pervasive `unknown` records once message contracts and domain models become stable.

### Bun build

Your approach does not require a framework-specific build system.

I would nevertheless include a small explicit build script because we have at least two independent browser entry points: the application and the worker.

For example:

The production script can additionally copy the static HTML, template manifest, `.gtl` files, and required assets.

I would initially avoid a single-file compiled HTML artifact because this application benefits from independently addressable worker code and template files.

Bun supports browser-targeted bundles with multiple entry points and configurable output naming. <Cite ref="turn661484search2"/>

Bun itself may be installed globally, as you prefer. We can use Bun's built-in test runner, while keeping TypeScript and any additional test utilities as development-only dependencies.

The new application's `package.json` should contain no `dependencies` entry unless a genuine build-only exception is required. Vendored libraries should be committed explicitly rather than populated into the runtime through package installation.

### A note about the supplied archive

The archive includes a nested `package.json`, `bunfig.toml`, and build scripts for the vendored RetroOS UI framework. It does not include a complete top-level build configuration for the surrounding browser extension.

Therefore, we can reuse verified conventions, but cannot establish the original extension's entire build pipeline from this archive alone.

Also, the ZIP contains the UI framework's nested `node_modules`, generated distributions, temporary assets, and archived design documents. We should not reproduce those directories in the new application's runtime source tree.

## 9. Export and document history references

Two other areas of the archive are directly applicable.

### 9.1. Exporting documents

The extension already vendors JSZip, Markdown-It, and Turndown.

Relevant implementation references include:

`src/content/atools/session-snapshot-v001/js/session_snapshot_zip.ts`

`src/content/atools/markdown-to-html-v001/js/markdown-to-html-core.ts`

`src/content/atools/rich-text-to-markdown-v001/js/rich-text-to-markdown.ts`

These provide examples of separating conversion logic from user-interface code.

For GTL, I would make the document model authoritative, then provide independent exporters:

| Export format | Approach |
|---|---|
| Plain text | Render document text without editor controls |
| Markdown | Serialize semantic document formatting |
| HTML | Generate sanitized semantic HTML |
| ZIP | Package selected documents and metadata |

Rich-text clipboard support should provide both `text/plain` and `text/html` when supported.

The exporter should never serialize the dark application chrome, hint panels, placeholder outlines, or internal editing controls.

### 9.2. History and revisions

The Tab Trail code is not a document history system, but its separation of state, storage, and diagnostics is useful.

I would not treat document history as a collection of chat messages.

Instead, a document would contain its current editable state, and a separate History feature would manage revisions associated with that document.

For the initial version, a revision could contain a timestamp, revision ID, and a snapshot of document content and placeholder values.

The editor's immediate undo/redo mechanism should remain distinct from persisted history.

There is also a useful privacy convention in the extension's usage tracking: its comments explicitly prohibit storing sensitive minibuffer inputs, selected text, URLs, clipboard content, and output in usage-statistics records.

I would adopt a similar boundary for GTL. Diagnostic logs and usage counters should not accidentally contain users' document text.

## 10. What I would explicitly avoid copying

The extension is an established application with accumulated functionality. That does not mean every architectural mechanism belongs in the new project.

| Reference pattern | Treatment in GTL |
|---|---|
| Large centralized `service_worker.ts` | Replace with a small dispatcher and feature-owned handlers |
| Chrome-specific message APIs | Replace with browser Worker messaging |
| `chrome.storage.local` settings | Replace with a web-compatible settings service |
| Global `lpSettings` and `lpLog` objects | Prefer explicit TypeScript modules |
| Handwritten global Dexie declarations | Replace with a typed library adapter |
| Entire RetroOS window-management framework | Avoid unless needed |
| Browser extension permissions and manifest rewriting | Not applicable |
| Dynamic command registries and complex minibuffer logic | Avoid in the initial application |
| Multiple duplicated persistence mechanisms inside features | Centralize adapter selection |
| Background processing of every editor change | Keep immediate editor state in the main thread |
| Saving every keystroke as a historical revision | Use autosave and separate revision snapshots |

The most important risk is recreating the overcomplication we encountered while designing the DSL.

The language became unnecessarily complicated when we modeled every possible operation. The application architecture can encounter the same problem if every small interaction requires a message protocol, feature registration, repository, background handler, and event chain.

We should enforce meaningful feature boundaries without creating those layers for simple code.

## 11. Recommended reference files for the implementation agent

These are the exact files I would direct an implementation agent to inspect after extracting `browser-extension-src.zip`.

| Priority | File | Instruction to the agent |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `src/background/tab_trail_service.ts` | Study domain service separation, store interfaces, Dexie adapters, and in-memory testing patterns |
| 2 | `src/content/runtime_messaging.ts` | Reuse request/response envelopes, request IDs, and error handling concepts |
| 3 | `src/settings/settings_runtime.ts` | Study schema-driven settings, defaults, normalization, overrides, and reset semantics |
| 4 | `src/settings/settings.ts` | Study generation of settings controls and immediate updates |
| 5 | `src/background/service_worker.ts` | Inspect Dexie initialization, message dispatch, and settings-change handling; do not copy the monolithic structure |
| 6 | `src/types/globals.d.ts` | Identify global library typing conventions; prefer a more encapsulated adapter for GTL |
| 7 | `src/content/libs/dexie-4.2.0.js` | Reuse as the initial vendored Dexie distribution, subject to module-worker bundling verification |
| 8 | `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/field.ts` | Study Shadow DOM, slots, help text, error states, and styling |
| 9 | `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/core/styles.ts` | Study constructed stylesheet adoption and fallback behavior |
| 10 | `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/tsconfig.json` | Use as a reference for strict modern TypeScript compilation |
| 11 | `src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/scripts/build.mts` | Study explicit Bun-based build scripting |
| 12 | `src/content/atools/session-snapshot-v001/js/session_snapshot_zip.ts` | Study local ZIP creation for future export |
| 13 | `src/content/atools/markdown-to-html-v001/js/markdown-to-html-core.ts` | Study pure Markdown-processing code separated from UI |
| 14 | `src/content/text_expander_minibuffer_model.ts` | Study declarative parameter metadata, hints, examples, and completion values |

I would specifically tell the agent that the large `text_expander_*` subsystem is a source of ideas, not a subsystem to transplant. It contains much more command-processing functionality than our new document-template design requires.

---

## 12. Architecture decisions worth carrying forward

These are my recommendations from this exploration, not additional decisions already accepted into the GTL specification.

| Design Question | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| Which TypeScript version should we target? | TypeScript 7.0.2, pinned for reproducibility |
| Which build tool should we use? | Bun |
| Should runtime npm packages be permitted? | No; commit vendored runtime distributions |
| Should testing and development dependencies be permitted? | Yes |
| Which Dexie version should we start with? | Vendored 4.2.0 from the reference |
| Should we copy the entire extension? | No; copy selected patterns and compatible vendor assets |
| Should the worker contain business logic? | No; route requests to feature-owned handlers |
| Should the static website use an extension-style service worker for data access? | No; use a Dedicated Worker for processing and IndexedDB |
| Should feature folders contain UI, worker, and storage code? | Yes, when the feature actually needs those parts |
| Should small features be forced into every architectural layer? | No |
| Who owns database schema versions? | A central database initializer |
| How are settings defined? | Declarative schema with defaults and overrides |
| How do settings update running components? | In-process notifications, worker messages, and optional cross-tab broadcasts |
| How are built-in templates discovered? | Generated static manifest |
| How should documents be edited? | Immediate main-thread state with asynchronous persistence |
| Should history and autosave be the same system? | No |
| Which exports should be planned? | Text, Markdown, HTML, and later ZIP |
| Should the editor depend on the worker to remain responsive? | No |

The architecture I would carry into the next specification is therefore:

**A static TypeScript application built with Bun, organized into a small number of vertical features, with Web Components for the interface, an independent document model, a dedicated data worker, vendored Dexie for IndexedDB, a declarative settings service, and static template files loaded on demand.**

That preserves the strongest engineering practices demonstrated by the browser extension, while leaving the application substantially simpler than the extension itself.

The two technical experiments I would run before locking the implementation architecture are loading the supplied Dexie 4.2.0 distribution in a Bun-built module worker, and exercising the full document editor with Shadow DOM, placeholder selection, undo/redo, and rich-text copying. Those are the areas where source-code inspection alone cannot establish that the proposed integration will work correctly.

Guided Document Editor — Specification Workshop

Working specification 0.1 · Requirements clarification · Round 1

I reviewed our previous discussions and the GTL reference package, particularly docs/00-combined-reference.md, docs/03-proposed-app-brief.md, and docs/06-prototypes-and-tests.md.

We have enough material to define the application. What remains is to settle a few behaviors that would otherwise force the implementation agent to make product decisions on our behalf.

I suggest building the specification incrementally. Each round will contain three independent design questions, with concrete usage examples and a recommendation. Your answers become accepted requirements. We will revise only the affected sections rather than rewriting the entire document repeatedly.

The earlier complex GTL specification is superseded by the simpler, document-centered design.

A00. Product definition

The application is a static, browser-based editor for creating documents from reusable, guided text templates.

Its primary interaction is editing a document, not answering a sequential questionnaire and not conversing with an AI assistant.

A user creates a document, chooses a template or starts with a blank note, and edits the text directly. Named placeholders are embedded within that text. Selecting a placeholder displays its question, hint, and examples in a persistent, nonintrusive guidance panel.

Changes update the rendered result immediately. The result appears below the editing area and can be copied as plain or rich text.

The application also provides a vertical document history, selectable template versions, local persistence, editing history, and settings.

Typical usage

1. Create document

Select New Document → Project Description → Input + processing + output.

2. Edit directly

Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.

Each highlighted phrase is an editable placeholder with contextual guidance.

3. Review result

Takes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.

Copy, continue editing, change the template version, or return later.

The interface should feel like a capable notepad that understands templates, rather than a form with a text preview.

B00. Established requirements

These are the constraints I consider settled from our discussions.

Design QuestionDecision
What kind of application are we creating?A static website
What is its primary interaction?Direct document editing
Does it require a chat input?No
How are documents organized?Vertical, chat-history-like navigation
Can users create blank notes?Yes
How are templates used?Select a template or version; display its complete text
How are placeholders edited?Directly from the document, with contextual guidance
Can answers be entered in any order?Yes
Should changing a template version forget previous answers?No
Where is the final result displayed?Below the editor, within the same document
What can users copy?Plain text and rich text
What is the initial visual direction?Dark theme with optional atmospheric backgrounds
What UI technology is preferred?Native Web Components, primarily Shadow DOM where appropriate
What language do we use?Modern, strictly checked TypeScript
What builds the application?Bun, available globally
Are npm runtime dependencies allowed?No; runtime libraries are vendored
Are npm development dependencies allowed?Yes
What database library do we use?Vendored Dexie for IndexedDB
How should the code be organized?Vertical, feature-oriented modules
What should the worker do?Route background operations to feature-owned handlers
How are templates distributed initially?Static files with a known manifest
Do settings require reloading the app?No; applicable changes take effect immediately
Do we need recovery and history?Yes; autosave and revision history are distinct

The supplied reference package remains the implementation reference. Its original source paths should be cited in the eventual specification wherever a concrete coding pattern is required.

C00. Specification structure

I propose organizing the finished specification into a small number of substantive sections.

SectionCoverage
AProduct purpose, terminology, and representative workflows
BApplication shell, navigation, and document lifecycle
CEditor, placeholders, guidance, and template versions
DRendering, clipboard, and export
EStorage, autosave, history, and recovery
FSettings, appearance, and accessibility
GTemplate format, loading, and authoring
HTypeScript architecture, workers, features, and vendored libraries
IError behavior, acceptance scenarios, and explicit exclusions
JDecision register and unresolved questions

Each section will mix concise prose, behavior tables, examples, and small algorithms when appropriate.

For example, rather than spending several paragraphs explaining document restoration, we can eventually specify it with a decision table describing what happens when an existing document is reopened, the chosen storage mode is unavailable, or saved data cannot be decoded.

The specification should describe observable behavior precisely. It should not prescribe unnecessary implementation details that a capable coding agent can determine from the reference source and tests.

Round 1 — Three foundational decisions

These are the first three gaps I recommend resolving because they affect the application model and the editor experience.

Q1. What happens to custom text when switching template versions?

Consider this template:

Plain textVERSION: Input + processing + output TEXT: Takes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}. VERSION: Purpose + function TEXT: A {{$type}} that {{$action}} to {{$benefit}}.

The user selects the first version and changes the text itself:

Plain textAccepts {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and returns {{$result}}.

They then switch to Purpose + function, work on it, and later return to Input + processing + output.

Should the application restore the customized sentence, including the changed words "Accepts" and "returns", or should it regenerate the original template text?

Recommendation: Preserve a separate editable draft for each version within the document. Placeholder values are shared by name across versions, while custom prose edits belong to the version where they were made.

Editing an instance must not modify the original reusable template.

This gives predictable behavior without inventing complicated merging rules.

Q2. Should the document editor support rich formatting directly?

You requested rich-text copying. We still need to distinguish that from rich-text authoring.

For example, suppose someone creates a note:

Plain textProject overview This application helps developers inspect JSON. Key features - Syntax validation - Readable formatting

Should they be able to select "Project overview" and turn it into a heading, bold a phrase, or format a list inside the editor? Or should editing initially be plain text, with formatting limited to the exported representation?

Recommendation: Start with direct plain-text editing, preserving line breaks and paragraphs, while supporting both plain-text and HTML clipboard output. Add authorable rich formatting only after the core editing, selection, and placeholder interactions are reliable.

The important distinction is that text/html clipboard support does not necessarily require a full rich-text editor.

This recommendation deliberately limits the first implementation's complexity.

Q3. What is the default persistence behavior?

We previously discussed three storage modes: no persistence, browser localStorage, and IndexedDB through Dexie.

Imagine a user creates several documents while IndexedDB persistence is enabled. They then change Settings to "Do not persist."

What happens to documents that were already saved? What happens to the current editing session?

Recommendation: Use IndexedDB through Dexie as the default. Offer memory-only mode as an explicit privacy choice and localStorage as an alternative.

Changing the mode should affect subsequent saves without silently deleting previous data. Existing documents remain available in the current session. Copying or moving persisted documents between stores should require an explicit operation.

Storage failures must never discard the active in-memory document.

Decision register

Design QuestionDecision
Q1. How are custom edits retained across template versions?Awaiting your decision
Q2. Is rich formatting authored directly in the initial editor?Awaiting your decision
Q3. What persistence behavior should be the default?Awaiting your decision

You can answer simply Q1 yes, Q2 yes, Q3 yes to accept all three recommendations, or explain changes to any of them.

After those answers, we will address the next three gaps, keeping the specification centered on real document-editing scenarios.

No glossary or ADR files have been created: the supplied materials are reference archives, not an identified writable application repository. Once we have that repository, we can record qualifying terminology and architectural decisions there without mixing them into the product specification.

ChatGPTSun, Oct 25, 2:06 PM

Specification workshop — Decisions and requirements update

Round 1 completed · Preparing Round 2

Q1, Q2, and Q3 are accepted. Your additions also establish requirements for documentation, schema-driven settings, live synchronization, error handling, and diagnostic logging.

I will incorporate these into the specification as requirements, not leave them as implementation suggestions.

C01. Document drafts and branching

Decision: Each template version maintains its own editable draft. Placeholder values are shared by name, while changes to the surrounding text belong to the individual draft.

Draft branches can be retained indefinitely and deleted explicitly by the user. Their displayed names begin with a creation timestamp.

For example:

Plain textProject description Input + processing + output 2026-10-25 13:30 — Initial draft 2026-10-25 14:05 — Revised wording Purpose + function 2026-10-25 14:18 — Initial draft

Switching versions or returning to an earlier draft restores its text and associated editing state without rewriting the original template.

The interface must distinguish a template version, a saved draft, and the document currently being edited. We should not use "version" and "draft" interchangeably.

No automatic age-based draft cleanup is permitted. However, browser storage is not guaranteed permanent, so the application will eventually need a way to export and back up retained work.

D01. Editing and export

Decision: The editor uses plain-text authoring, preserving paragraphs and line breaks.

The final result can be copied as both text/plain and text/html. HTML export may represent semantic paragraphs and line breaks, but does not imply a full rich-text editor.

Template guidance and placeholder highlighting belong to the editing interface and are not included in exported content.

E01. Persistence

Decision: IndexedDB through vendored Dexie is the default storage mode.

The application also supports memory-only and localStorage modes. Switching storage modes must not silently erase existing documents, and a storage failure must not discard the active in-memory document.

Autosave and historical drafts are separate concepts. The precise rules for creating draft branches remain to be settled.

F01. Development documentation

Documentation is a development requirement, not a task postponed until the application is finished.

Documentation locationRequired content
Source-file headerFile responsibility, important dependencies, relevant usage examples
Public types and APIsMeaningful contracts, invariants, error behavior
Parser and template modulesSupported syntax, valid and invalid examples, rendering rules
Feature documentationDomain responsibility, UI/worker/storage boundaries, important workflows
Architecture documentationComponent relationships, message protocol, persistence model
TestsExecutable examples of accepted behavior and edge cases

For example, the template parser should explain the supported language near its implementation, with a separate language reference that describes the syntax from the template author's perspective.

Documentation should reflect actual implementation behavior. We should avoid maintaining multiple long documents that repeat the same information and gradually contradict each other.

F02. Settings and live synchronization

The settings experience should follow the schema-driven approach from the browser extension reference, particularly src/settings/settings_runtime.ts and src/settings/settings.ts.

Each setting has a stable identifier, feature grouping, title, description, control type, default, and validation constraints. The Settings page is generated from that schema.

Changing a setting should affect the current application immediately and notify other open instances.

A Dedicated Worker can participate in synchronization, but it should not be the sole mechanism responsible for it. Cross-tab notifications can use BroadcastChannel; every application instance must also load the effective settings from storage at startup and when it regains focus.

If synchronization fails, the current instance remains usable. Invalid persisted settings fall back to their documented defaults, while failed writes must be reported rather than silently treated as successful.

H01. Diagnostic logging

Logging should describe operations with explicit, stable event names and manually selected parameters.

Example:

TypeScript
log.info("document.draft.saved", {
  documentId,
  draftId,
  storageMode,
  durationMs
});

Structured objects should be serialized at the logging boundary, using JSON.stringify, so later object mutations cannot change the recorded event. Logging must also handle serialization errors without disrupting the user operation.

The logging system should distinguish an expected failure, such as unavailable storage, from a programming error. Neither case should cause the editor to lose its current contents.

Whether diagnostic events remain local or may be transmitted elsewhere is still an open product decision.

Round 2 — Three specific decisions

Q4. When exactly is a historical draft created?

We have agreed that drafts can be retained indefinitely, are clearly named with creation timestamps, and can be restored or deleted.

The remaining question is whether ordinary editing should continuously create historical drafts.

Consider this sequence:

  1. The user opens a project description.

  2. They change three words.

  3. They switch to another template version.

  4. They return to the first version the next day.

Recommendation: Each template version has one automatically saved working draft. Switching versions preserves that working draft without creating an additional history entry.

A separate historical draft is created when the user chooses Save Draft Copy.

For example:

Plain textInput + processing + output Working draft (autosaved) Saved drafts 2026-10-25 14:05 — Revised description 2026-10-25 13:30 — Initial description

Restoring a saved draft would replace the working draft only after the user confirms, with the option to save the current working draft first.

This distinguishes crash recovery from intentionally saved historical versions and prevents a new record from appearing on every keystroke.

Q4: Do you accept automatic working-draft saving with explicit historical draft creation?

Q5. What exactly should happen when copying an incomplete document?

Suppose the user has entered only one of three values:

Plain textTakes pasted JSON, [processing], and produces [result].

The editor knows that two placeholders are unfilled.

We need to decide whether copying is blocked, whether unfilled placeholders are silently removed, or whether the user can copy incomplete text.

Recommendation: Allow copying, but warn when unresolved placeholders remain. The copied text retains their visible placeholder markers rather than silently replacing them with empty strings.

For example, the user presses Copy and sees:

Two placeholders are unfilled: processing and result. Copy the incomplete document anyway?

They can continue editing or copy deliberately.

An intentionally entered empty value should be distinguishable from a placeholder that has never been answered.

Q5: Do you accept copying incomplete documents with an explicit warning and visible placeholders?

Q6. Should diagnostic logs ever leave the browser automatically?

You have specified detailed, structured diagnostic events. For example:

TypeScript
log.info("settings.changed", {
  key: "editor.showGuidance",
  previousValue: true,
  newValue: false,
  source: "settings-page"
});

However, our application may contain private notes, drafted correspondence, and other user-authored content.

Logging an entire document, clipboard contents, or raw text entered into placeholders would create unnecessary exposure.

Recommendation: Keep diagnostic telemetry local by default, with no automatic transmission to an external service.

Logs should capture event names, operation identifiers, duration, success/failure, and carefully selected diagnostic parameters. Do not log document bodies or placeholder answers.

Provide a diagnostic log viewer and an explicit Export Diagnostics action. Retain a bounded history rather than accumulating logs indefinitely.

Q6: Do you accept local-only diagnostic logging, with explicit export and no automatic transmission?

Specification completion requirements

I also recommend establishing these checks for the implementation agent:

AreaCompletion requirement
DocumentationNew or substantially modified modules have accurate source-level documentation and usage examples where relevant
Language implementationParser and renderer documentation includes valid, invalid, and boundary-case examples
SettingsEvery setting has a schema definition, a default, and a test for its effective value
SynchronizationSettings changes propagate to open instances; failures are recoverable
DiagnosticsImportant operations report structured, meaningful events without exposing user document content
Error handlingExpected failures produce actionable errors and preserve in-memory work
TestsBehavior demonstrated in the specification has corresponding acceptance coverage

For architecture and language documentation, source comments should explain local contracts while a concise module-level reference describes behavior spanning multiple files. We should not duplicate entire specifications in every source header.

These checks make documentation and observability part of implementation quality, not cleanup work at the end.

Please answer Q4, Q5, and Q6. We can then address editor interactions, especially caret movement, placeholder selection, direct prose editing, and undo/redo—behaviors where a small working prototype may be more useful than adding further abstract rules.
