Guided Document Editor — Specification Workshop

Round 2 completed · Requirements update · Preparing Round 3

Q4, Q5, and Q6 are settled. There is one important change from my earlier recommendation: we will not maintain historical revisions or manually saved draft copies. Instead, each document has automatically persisted working state, with separate working drafts for template versions that the user has actually modified.

The application should remain a notepad with guided placeholders, not become a version-control system.

C02. Draft lifecycle and automatic saving

A document is a persistent editing session. It can contain several template versions, each with its own working draft.

A draft is created only after the user makes a meaningful content change. Merely opening a template, previewing another version, navigating through available templates, or focusing a placeholder does not create a draft.

Draft creation rules

User actionCreate or update draft?
Open a templateNo
Switch to another template versionNo, unless the previous version has unsaved changes
Focus a placeholder or read its hintNo
Enter a placeholder valueYes
Change ordinary document textYes
Modify a previously entered valueYes
Return all content to its original, unfilled stateDiscard the now-unmodified draft
Switch away from a modified versionSave its pending changes
Return to a previously modified versionRestore its existing draft
Wait for the autosave intervalSave dirty drafts; create no historical copy

The underlying implementation should compare meaningful document state, not rely solely on whether keyboard input occurred. A user who types and then removes the text, returning to the original state, should not leave behind an unnecessary draft.

Example: switching between versions

Plain textProject description Input + processing + output Working draft — modified Purpose + function No working draft — unmodified

The user changes three words in the first version. This creates its working draft.

They switch to Purpose + function but do not edit anything. That version remains an unmodified template preview.

If they modify the second version, it receives its own working draft.

Returning to the first version restores its previous text. Placeholder values shared by name remain shared across versions; merely displaying a value inherited from another version does not create a new draft.

Drafts do not have separate historical revisions. A save updates the existing working state.

Autosave

The default autosave interval is 60 seconds, configurable on the Settings page.

Changes remain available immediately in memory. Persistence operates asynchronously.

The application should also attempt to flush pending changes when the user switches versions, changes documents, or the page becomes hidden.

A page-close event cannot guarantee that an asynchronous IndexedDB operation completes. The implementation must account for this limitation rather than advertise guaranteed recovery from every abrupt browser termination.

Draft naming

Stored working drafts display their creation timestamp, followed by the associated template version.

For example:

Plain text2026-10-25 14:05 — Input + processing + output 2026-10-25 14:18 — Purpose + function

The creation timestamp does not change each time autosave runs.

Users can delete working drafts explicitly. There is no automatic age-based cleanup and no revision-history interface.

D02. Required fields, incomplete output, and copying

We will retain a small but meaningful distinction between required and optional placeholders in the simplified template language.

The editor can render an incomplete document, but copying and exporting are disabled while required values remain unresolved.

For example:

Plain textTakes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}.

If $processing has no value, the result remains visible, but the unresolved placeholder is highlighted as a validation error.

LIVE RESULT

Takes pasted JSON,

processing required

, and produces formatted JSON.

1 required value is missing.

The error decoration is part of the interface, not the exported text.

A missing required field must not crash the renderer. Once all required values are provided, copying and exporting become available.

A deliberately empty optional field is not a validation error.

E02. Persistence and storage settings

IndexedDB through vendored Dexie remains the default storage engine.

The settings page will offer memory-only, localStorage, and IndexedDB persistence modes. Storage selection must not silently erase documents saved using another mode.

The settings architecture follows the schema-driven approach in the browser-extension reference package.

Settings have defaults, constraints, descriptions, and corresponding UI controls. Changes apply immediately and propagate to other open application instances.

A failed storage operation must preserve the active in-memory document and produce an actionable diagnostic. The application must not report an unsaved change as successfully persisted.

H02. Console diagnostics

Your latest requirements replace the earlier diagnostic-export proposal.

Diagnostic logging is console-only. There is no remote telemetry, persisted log database, diagnostic-export feature, or in-application log viewer.

The application provides a dedicated logger and separate formatter implementations.

Example:

TypeScript
logger.info("document.autosave.completed", {
  documentId,
  draftId,
  durationMs,
  storageMode
});

Every message includes a consistent project prefix. The default formatter uses restrained color and typography to distinguish severity, event names, and diagnostic parameters.

Settings determine both the verbosity and the formatter. The changes propagate to all open tabs and relevant workers.

Structured parameters are selected explicitly. When objects are included, they are serialized with JSON.stringify at the logging boundary so later mutations cannot change the recorded representation.

Logging must avoid document bodies, placeholder answers, clipboard contents, and other user-authored content by default. Serialization or formatting failures must never interrupt an application operation.

A practical default is info verbosity with the styled console formatter.

F01. Development documentation remains mandatory

The previously accepted documentation requirement remains unchanged.

Each substantive module should describe its responsibility, public contract, significant invariants, and usage examples where useful. Parser and template modules also need accurate syntax examples and boundary-case tests.

We should maintain a concise architectural reference reflecting the actual feature structure, with detailed local behavior documented near the code it governs.

Round 3 — Three remaining editor decisions

Q7. How should a template declare required and optional placeholders?

We agreed to remove the old ASK REQUIRED scripting syntax. We now need a minimal way to retain field validation.

I recommend the following:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Project description TEXT: A {{$tool}} that {{$action}}. $tool: What kind of tool is it? HINT: Use a short noun phrase. EXAMPLE: browser-based utility $action: What does it do? EXAMPLE: validates JSON $technology OPTIONAL: Which technology does it use? EXAMPLE: JavaScript

A declared placeholder is required by default. The author adds OPTIONAL only when necessary.

For the initial version, an absent optional placeholder renders as empty text; the renderer does not automatically rewrite surrounding sentences or punctuation. Template authors remain responsible for producing coherent text.

Undeclared placeholders are treated as required and receive automatically generated questions.

Recommendation: Accept required-by-default placeholders and the $name OPTIONAL: syntax.

Q8. Should the first release include a reusable template-authoring interface?

There are two different activities:

  • Editing a document created from a template.

  • Editing the reusable template definition itself.

For example, a user might change the sentence "Takes [input], [processing], and produces [result]" in a document. That modifies only the current document.

But if they want to change the question attached to $processing for all future documents, that modifies the template definition.

Recommendation: Support both, but keep them clearly separate.

The first editor is the primary document workspace. A secondary Edit Template mode lets authors create and modify template definitions using the same general text-editing experience.

Built-in templates should remain available as read-only originals. Users can duplicate them to create editable personal templates stored locally.

This avoids the need for a separate DSL development environment while preserving the distinction between editing a document and editing its source template.

Q8: Do you accept a separate, lightweight template-authoring mode?

Q9. What should happen when the user selects a placeholder?

Suppose the document contains:

Plain textTakes pasted JSON, [processing], and produces formatted JSON.

The user clicks [processing].

Recommendation: The placeholder becomes active, and its question, hint, examples, and current answer appear in the docked guidance panel.

The user edits the value through a focused control in that panel; the corresponding text updates immediately. Clicking ordinary text returns to direct prose editing.

The guidance panel remains visible and selectable, so the user can read and copy examples while writing. It must not obscure the active caret.

Keyboard navigation should also reach placeholders without requiring the mouse.

Q9: Do you accept this placeholder-focused editing behavior as the default?

We now have a substantially clearer product model: automatically saved, independently editable document drafts; a small declarative template format; visible validation of required fields; no unnecessary history or telemetry subsystems; and a browser-native, settings-driven implementation.

Once Q7-Q9 are resolved, the next useful review should concentrate on the document's actual editing mechanics and recovery behavior. Those are best expressed as a handful of concrete usage scenarios and acceptance tests rather than additional DSL constructs.

Guided Document Editor — Specification Workshop

Round 3 completed · Decisions Q7–Q9 accepted · Round 4

All three decisions are settled. The most important addition is that template authoring is a separate workspace with its own draft lifecycle and an explicit file-export workflow.

The application now has two distinct editing experiences: working with documents created from templates, and authoring the reusable templates themselves.

Accepted requirements

Design QuestionDecision
Q7. How are required fields declared?Required by default. Use $name OPTIONAL: for optional fields.
Q8. Can users author reusable templates?Yes, in a separate template-authoring workspace.
Q9. How does placeholder editing work?Selecting a placeholder activates its guidance and value editor in a nonmodal panel.

G01. Template authoring workspace

Template authoring is a separate feature, accessible from the main application. It is not mixed into ordinary document editing.

The author edits a template definition, previews its behavior, and downloads the resulting source file.

A simple template might contain:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Input + processing + output TEXT: Takes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}. $input: What does the user provide? HINT: Describe the original information. EXAMPLE: pasted JSON $processing: What happens to it? EXAMPLE: checks it for syntax errors $result: What comes out? EXAMPLE: readable formatted JSON $notes OPTIONAL: Additional notes?

$input, $processing, and $result are required by default. $notes is optional.

The template authoring workspace must support both creating a new template and editing an existing one.

G01.1. Authoring and publication lifecycle

Open Template Authoring

Create a definition or open an existing template

Edit source

Modify text, placeholders, hints, examples, or versions

Save working draft

Persist unfinished authoring work in IndexedDB

Preview and validate

Test the template with sample answers

Download template file

Export the completed source

Register in project

Add or replace the file and update the template manifest

The important distinction is between working storage and published templates.

LocationPurpose
IndexedDBUnfinished or ongoing template-authoring drafts
.gtl source filePortable template definition
Project template directoryPublished built-in templates
Template manifestList of templates the deployed application loads

Editing an existing built-in template does not modify the deployed source file. The author downloads the replacement file and adds it to the project.

This keeps the runtime simple. It does not require a filesystem-writing backend, Git integration, or a template marketplace.

G01.2. Independent authoring state

The application must not confuse a document draft with a template-authoring draft.

For example, changing the question attached to $processing in Template Authoring modifies the working template definition.

Changing the value of $processing in a project-description document modifies that document's answer.

These are different operations with different persistence records.

C03. Placeholder interaction

Selecting a placeholder activates a persistent guidance panel.

For example, while editing:

Plain textTakes pasted JSON, [processing], and produces formatted JSON.

Clicking [processing] opens:

What happens to the input?

Required

Describe the main operation in present tense. A short verb phrase is preferable.

Processing

Example: validates its syntax

The panel remains visible while the user edits the value or surrounding text. Its content is selectable, examples can be copied, and it must not obscure the current caret.

A placeholder can be reached through keyboard navigation as well as by clicking it.

The document updates immediately when its value changes.

Round 4 — Three editor and authoring decisions

Q10. What happens when the user deletes or pastes over a placeholder?

Suppose the document contains:

Plain textTakes [input], [processing], and produces [result].

The user selects the text containing [processing] and presses Delete.

Should the placeholder behave as ordinary characters that can be partially deleted, or as one identifiable editing element?

Recommendation: Treat placeholders as atomic editing elements.

Ordinary text remains freely editable. A placeholder can be selected, moved, copied, or removed as a whole, but its identifier cannot be accidentally damaged by deleting a few characters.

Removing the occurrence from a document does not delete the saved answer associated with that placeholder. Undo restores the removed occurrence.

This should make mixed prose-and-placeholder editing substantially more reliable.

Q10: Do you accept atomic placeholders surrounded by freely editable text?

Q11. Should Template Authoring validate source before allowing a final download?

Consider an author editing:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Example TEXT: Hello {{$name}}. $name: Who are you writing to?

Then accidentally changing the placeholder declaration to an invalid form.

The authoring workspace should identify the problem, preferably at its source location.

Recommendation: Allow unfinished authoring drafts to be saved at any time, but enable the final Download Template action only when the source passes validation.

The editor should report a specific problem rather than a generic failure:

Plain textLine 6: Invalid placeholder declaration. Expected: $name: Question text $name OPTIONAL: Question text

A live preview should remain available whenever enough of the source can be parsed.

This separates incomplete work from a distributable template without introducing an elaborate publishing system.

Q11: Do you accept validation before final template download?

Q12. How should autosave status and failures appear?

The working document saves on the configurable interval, initially 60 seconds, and attempts to flush pending changes when the user switches documents or template versions.

Suppose IndexedDB becomes unavailable while someone is writing.

The content should remain in memory, but the interface must not imply that it has been saved.

Recommendation: Display a small persistent save-status indicator, without interrupting editing.

StateDisplayBehavior
No content changesNo draft yetNo unnecessary persistence
Changes pendingUnsaved changesKeep changes in memory
Save runningSaving…Editing remains available
Save completedSavedContinue normally
Save failedNot saved · RetryPreserve content; provide error details

The same pattern can be used in the template-authoring workspace.

We should not show a toast after every successful autosave. Repeated success notifications would be distracting.

Q12: Do you accept a quiet save-status indicator with explicit failure and retry behavior?

These questions address three independent behaviors: document editing, template publication, and persistence feedback.

After Q10–Q12, the next substantial area is how the editor handles selections, undo/redo, and navigation between multiple documents. Those details deserve concrete interaction scenarios rather than more language features.

Guided Document Editor — Specification Workshop

Round 4 completed · Q10–Q12 settled · Specification corrections

Your decision on Q10 significantly simplifies the application. We are no longer building a general-purpose text editor with embedded placeholders. We are building a guided text generator that displays its complete text and lets users edit only the values inside placeholders.

That changes the editor architecture, draft model, keyboard behavior, and several examples in the working specification.

C04. Generated document editing

Decision: The template structure and generated result are read-only. Only placeholder values are editable.

For example, the user sees:

GUIDED TEXT

Takes,, and produces.

GENERATED RESULT

Takes pasted JSON, [processing], and produces formatted JSON.

This illustrates editable fields embedded in fixed text. The final application will style the fields more like ordinary prose and show their guidance in the contextual panel.

The user cannot delete or rewrite "Takes", "and produces", or the punctuation through the document interface. To change that structure, they must use the separate Template Authoring workspace.

The output itself cannot be edited. It is regenerated whenever an answer changes.

We should therefore remove free-prose editing, arbitrary placeholder insertion, atomic placeholder deletion, and rich-text authoring from the document editor requirements.

Importantly, a working draft now stores user-entered values and relevant selections, not a separately editable copy of the template's prose. The previous examples about changing "Takes" into "Accepts" are superseded.

G02. Template Authoring validation

Decision: Validation does not block downloading a template file.

The template source editor permits incomplete or invalid work to be saved as a local working draft and downloaded whenever the user chooses.

Validation runs during editing, without interrupting typing. Errors should identify the relevant source location and explain the problem.

A source line might display an error marker. Hovering or focusing the marker reveals a short explanation. A collapsible diagnostics panel on the right shows all currently unresolved errors, with a compact error count.

When an error is fixed, it disappears from the panel automatically.

The distinction is:

ActionInvalid template source
Edit sourceAllowed
Autosave authoring draftAllowed
Download .gtl sourceAllowed
Show validation errorsRequired
Preview valid portionsAllowed where feasible
Treat invalid source as a working templateNot guaranteed; use the last valid preview or explain why preview is unavailable

This preserves unfinished work without misrepresenting invalid templates as executable.

E03. Autosave feedback and diagnostics

Decision: Keep the quiet save-status indicator.

Normal operation should be unobtrusive. A small status element near the document header or footer can show Unsaved changes, Saving…, Saved, or Save failed · Retry.

The diagnostics panel is a separate mechanism. It can show unresolved template validation errors and actionable save failures, with a count badge. Successful autosaves do not generate notifications.

A failed save retains the user's data in memory, keeps the unsaved state visible, and offers retry.

C05. Keyboard navigation between placeholders

Your proposed behavior is worth implementing because it makes separate value editors behave like parts of one continuous sentence.

I inspected the relevant source in browser-extension-src.zip, especially:

src/content/atools/minibuffer-editor-v001/js/minibuffer-editor-core.ts

src/content/atools/minibuffer-editor-v001/js/minibuffer-editor.ts

src/content/text_expander_minibuffer_model.ts

The reference distinguishes native text editing from application-level keyboard actions. For example, resolveMinibufferArrowAction() decides whether Up/Down should navigate suggestions, history, or remain native.

The exact Left/Right movement between adjacent fields that you described is not implemented in those inspected files. We can adapt their approach—centralized, testable key-decision functions—but must implement the field-boundary navigation ourselves.

A proposed interaction:

Adjacent placeholder editors

Field AField B

FOCUS-TRANSFER RULES

Left at start of Field B

End of Field A

Right at end of Field A

Start of Field B

Behavioral illustration; final keyboard handling belongs to the actual editor components.

The key point is that ordinary cursor movement remains native inside a value. Focus changes only when the cursor reaches a field boundary and the user presses the corresponding arrow again.

The focused value editor also determines which guidance appears in the right panel.

For the implementation, cursor positions, selection ranges, keyboard composition, and focus transfers need explicit tests. Composition events must never cause unintended navigation.

H03. Unified contextual panel

We now have two kinds of contextual information:

  • Guidance about the active placeholder.

  • Currently unresolved errors associated with the document, template source, or persistence.

My initial recommendation is to keep them in one right-hand sidebar with independently expandable sections, rather than create several floating panels.

For example:

Context

2

Guidance — Processing

What happens to the input?

Describe the main operation using a present-tense verb.

Example: validates its syntax

Unresolved issues

2

Processing is required

Provide a value to enable copying.

Changes not saved

IndexedDB write failed. Retry available.

This remains a concept, not yet a settled layout decision.

Round 5 — Three questions

Q13. Should Left/Right arrows navigate between fields at text boundaries?

For single-line text fields, the proposed behavior is:

ActionBehavior
Left, cursor not at beginningMove one character left
Left, cursor at beginningFocus previous placeholder, caret at its end
Right, cursor not at endMove one character right
Right, cursor at endFocus next placeholder, caret at its beginning
Tab / Shift+TabMove forward/backward through focusable controls
Arrow movement during text compositionPreserve native editing behavior

For Up/Down, I recommend moving between single-line placeholders when no selection menu is open. Multiline text fields should retain normal line navigation.

At the first or last placeholder, arrows should not wrap around.

Recommendation: Yes. Implement field-boundary Left/Right navigation, context-aware Up/Down, and standard Tab navigation without trapping keyboard focus.

Q14. How should shared placeholder values affect draft creation?

We previously agreed that placeholders with the same name share their value across template versions.

Now consider this scenario:

  1. The user edits $input while viewing Version A. A working draft is created.

  2. They switch to Version B, which also uses $input.

  3. They see the existing value but do not edit anything in Version B.

Should Version B receive a working draft merely because it displays an answer entered elsewhere?

Recommendation: No. Shared placeholder values belong to the document. A template version acquires a distinct working draft only when the user makes a meaningful change while working in that version.

This prevents duplicate draft records caused by inherited answers.

The application must still persist the shared values reliably so both versions restore correctly.

Q15. Should the application retain its original blank-notepad feature?

We previously wanted the application to work as a notepad. Q10 now removes ordinary free-text editing from the generated document.

These requirements can coexist if a blank note is simply a template with one multiline placeholder.

For example:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Blank note TEXT: {{$body}} $body: Write your note. INPUT: multiline

The user sees one large text area containing their note, with no fixed surrounding prose.

This would provide straightforward note-taking without adding a second general-purpose document editor.

Recommendation: Yes. Retain Blank Note as a built-in template with one multiline placeholder.

Design QuestionDecision
Q10. Is ordinary template prose editable?No. Only placeholder values are editable.
Q11. Does invalid template source block download?No. Show live diagnostics and permit download.
Q12. Should save status be displayed?Yes. Quiet status with explicit failure and retry.
Q13. Should arrow keys support boundary-based field navigation?Awaiting decision
Q14. Should inherited shared values create additional drafts?Awaiting decision
Q15. Should Blank Note use a single multiline placeholder?Awaiting decision

Please answer Q13, Q14, and Q15. These decisions will establish the remaining essentials of keyboard behavior and the draft data model before we specify the complete editor interaction contract.

Guided Document Editor — Specification Workshop

Round 5 completed · Q13–Q15 reviewed · Round 6

I have recorded your decisions. Q13 and Q15 are settled. Your explanation of Q14 introduces an important distinction that I want to clarify before finalizing the document model.

Accepted requirements

C06. Keyboard navigation

Q13 accepted. Separate placeholder inputs should behave as though they belong to one continuous document.

Keyboard actionExpected behavior
Left within a valueMove the caret left normally
Left at the beginningMove to the previous placeholder, caret at its end
Right within a valueMove the caret right normally
Right at the endMove to the next placeholder, caret at its beginning
Up / Down in single-line inputsNavigate between placeholders
Tab / Shift+TabStandard forward/backward focus navigation
Text composition in progressPreserve native editing behavior

There is no focus wrapping at the first or last placeholder.

We will model navigation as a small, independently tested keyboard controller, following the approach of the minibuffer editor in the reference archive.

C07. Blank Note

Q15 accepted. The application retains a notepad capability through a built-in template containing one multiline placeholder.

Plain textTEMPLATE: Blank note TEXT: {{$body}} $body: Write your note. INPUT: multiline

This means we do not need a second general-purpose document editor.

Blank Note uses the same autosave, history navigation, validation, and copy functionality as other documents.

C08. Document and draft identity

Q14 needs one distinction clarified.

You accepted the recommendation not to create drafts merely because a user views another template version.

However, you also said that Version B should be treated as a separate document, and that its values should not appear there.

That differs from our earlier decision to share answers across template versions.

I am treating your newer explanation as the current design direction, but I want to confirm its precise meaning rather than accidentally preserve the old behavior.

Round 6 — Three questions

Q16. Are answers shared between template versions?

Consider two independently selected description styles.

Document A — Input + processing + output

The user enters:

Takes pasted JSON, checks its syntax, and produces formatted JSON.

Document B — Another description style

This template also declares $input.

Processes [input] to improve data readability.

Should Document B automatically inherit "pasted JSON" from Document A?

Recommendation: No. Each document should own its own answers. Placeholder names identify values within one document, not globally across documents.

If the user creates another document from the same template, that document also starts with empty answers.

Switching between existing documents restores their individual values and drafts.

This matches your latest description of separate documents and avoids surprising cross-document changes.

Q16: Do you want complete answer isolation between documents, including documents created from different versions of the same template?

Q17. How should arrow navigation behave inside multiline placeholders?

A multiline input introduces a special case.

Imagine the Blank Note template:

Plain textFirst line Second line| with the caret here Third line

If the user presses Up, they normally expect the caret to move into the first line, not to jump to an unrelated placeholder.

Recommendation: Preserve native Up/Down behavior inside multiline inputs. Use Tab and Shift+Tab to navigate between fields, while Left/Right transfer between placeholders only at the beginning or end of the entire value.

For single-line inputs, retain the Up/Down navigation we accepted in Q13.

This keeps ordinary note-taking predictable.

Q17: Do you accept native vertical navigation inside multiline inputs?

Q18. Should an untouched new document appear in saved history?

Consider this sequence:

  1. The user clicks New Document.

  2. They select Purpose + function.

  3. They inspect the text and examples.

  4. They navigate back to an existing document without entering anything.

We have agreed that this interaction must not create a saved working draft.

The remaining question is how the sidebar represents that temporary document.

Recommendation: Show the new document immediately as a temporary active item. It is not written to persistent storage until its content meaningfully changes.

If the user leaves without making any changes, the temporary document disappears from history.

For example:

Documents

+ New

Purpose + function

Not saved

Project description — October 25

Blank note — October 24

The temporary item is removed when the user navigates away without making a meaningful content change.

A template selection alone should not count as a content change.

Q18: Do you accept temporary documents that enter saved history only after meaningful user input?

These three decisions will settle the boundaries between documents, placeholder values, and working drafts.

Afterward, we can address template-loading errors, document deletion, and recovery. I also want to keep a clear distinction between the two uses of the word history: the document list, which we retain, and historical revisions, which you explicitly excluded.

ChatGPTSun, Oct 25, 2:57 PM

Guided Document Editor — Specification Workshop

Round 6 completed · Q16–Q18 accepted · Round 7

All three decisions are settled. Your clarification of Q16 supersedes the earlier proposal to share answers between template versions.

Accepted requirements

C09. Placeholder values are isolated

Q16 — Accepted. Placeholder answers are not shared between documents or template versions.

The same placeholder name does not imply the same value in different documents.

SituationExpected behavior
$input appears twice in one documentBoth occurrences display that document's answer
Version A contains $input = "pasted JSON"Version A retains "pasted JSON"
User opens Version B with $inputVersion B starts with its own empty value
User returns to Version A"pasted JSON" is restored
User creates another document from Version AThe new document starts with empty answers

In other words, the value is identified by the document and placeholder name, not by the placeholder name globally.

This also simplifies persistence: we don't need a shared-answer registry or synchronization of placeholder values between documents.

C10. Keyboard navigation

Q17 — Accepted. Multiline fields retain native vertical cursor movement.

The final combined rules for Q13 and Q17 are:

KeySingle-line inputMultiline input
LeftPrevious field only at beginningPrevious field only at beginning of entire value
RightNext field only at endNext field only at end of entire value
UpPrevious placeholderNative vertical cursor movement
DownNext placeholderNative vertical cursor movement
TabNext focusable controlNext focusable control
Shift+TabPrevious focusable controlPrevious focusable control

For multiline fields, Up and Down do not switch fields, including on the first and last lines. This avoids unexpected focus changes while writing notes.

The navigation controller should not intercept keyboard composition, active text selections, or editing shortcuts.

B02. Temporary documents

Q18 — Accepted. A newly opened document appears immediately in the interface but is not added to persistent history until the user makes a meaningful content change.

Example:

Plain textNew Document → Select "Purpose + function" → Preview fields and read hints → Switch to another document Result: No saved document was created.

However:

Plain textNew Document → Select "Purpose + function" → Enter "browser-based tool" into $tool → Switch to another document Result: Working document is persisted.

If the user enters a value and subsequently removes it, returning to the original unmodified state, the application should avoid retaining an empty working document.

The previously accepted 60-second autosave interval remains configurable. Navigating away from modified content triggers a save attempt independently of that interval.

Round 7 — Three remaining lifecycle decisions

Q19. Does selecting another template version create a separate document-history entry?

We have settled that answers are isolated between versions. What is not yet completely settled is how those versions appear in the left-hand history.

Consider:

  1. Create a document using Input + processing + output.

  2. Enter three answers.

  3. Select Purpose + function.

  4. Enter different answers.

  5. Reopen the application tomorrow.

Two reasonable history layouts are possible.

A. Separate documents

Input + processing + output

Purpose + function

Each is a standalone document with its own answers and save state.

B. Grouped drafts

Project description

Input + processing + output

Purpose + function

One parent document contains multiple isolated drafts.

Recommendation: A — Separate documents.

It matches your latest description and provides the simpler data model: one saved document has one selected template version and its own placeholder values.

Switching styles after entering content opens a separate document without overwriting the existing one. Untouched previews still do not create saved history entries.

Q19: Should each modified template version appear as an independent document in the sidebar?

Q20. What should happen when someone deletes a saved document?

We have ruled out historical revisions and automatic draft accumulation.

Deletion therefore needs an explicit policy.

Recommendation: Deleting a saved document requires confirmation identifying the document being deleted.

After confirmation, remove its saved content and associated working state from the active persistence store. Do not implement a recycle bin or revision-based recovery in the initial release.

Deleting an untouched temporary document requires no confirmation because nothing has been persisted.

Q20: Do you accept confirmed, permanent deletion of saved documents, without a recycle bin?

Q21. What happens when a bundled template is updated?

Imagine a document created in October from:

Plain textTakes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}.

In November, the application is redeployed and that template's source changes:

Plain textAccepts {{$input}} and returns {{$result}}.

The user opens their October document.

Should it silently change its sentence to the new structure, or preserve the original?

Recommendation: Persist a snapshot of the template definition with a document when that document first becomes save-worthy.

Existing documents continue using the template structure they were created with. Updated bundled templates apply to newly created documents.

For example:

DocumentTemplate structure used
Created October 25Original October definition
Created November 3Updated November definition
October document reopened in NovemberOriginal October definition

This prevents future deployments from unexpectedly changing existing documents or invalidating their saved answers.

It also means a document can be reopened if its original bundled template is subsequently removed from the template manifest.

Q21: Should saved documents retain their original template definition, even after application updates?

We are converging on a compact implementation model: independently saved guided documents, fixed template text, editable placeholder values, separate template authoring, automatic persistence, and no revision-history subsystem.

These three decisions will finish the key document-lifecycle rules. Afterward, I recommend shifting from abstract design questions toward realistic end-to-end acceptance scenarios and assembling the first complete specification draft.

Final decisions: Q19–Q21

QuestionDecisionReason
Q19. How do template versions appear in history?Separate documents. Each modified version has its own history entry and answers.Matches your decision that versions are independent.

Example: independent documents

A user selects Input + processing + output, enters three answers, and later switches to Purpose + function.

The first version remains a saved document. The second starts with empty answers, even if both versions contain a placeholder named $input.

If the user edits the second version, it becomes another saved document.

Both documents can be reopened from the sidebar, with their original answers and template structures intact.

Merely previewing a version creates no persistent document.

Additional decisions I will make without further questions

AreaDefault implementation decision
Placeholder validationRequired by default; optional fields explicitly declared
Meaningful changesActual changes to user-authored values or document metadata, not navigation or focus
Autosave60-second configurable interval, plus save attempts when navigating away
Draft historyOne working state per document; no historical revisions
Undo/redoNative field editing and ordinary undo behavior; no version-control subsystem
Template changesSource templates can be edited only in the separate authoring workspace
Template downloadsAlways permitted, including for invalid drafts; diagnostics remain visible
EditorFixed prose with editable placeholder values
KeyboardBoundary-aware Left/Right, native multiline navigation, standard Tab behavior
CopyingDisabled while required values are missing
SettingsSchema-driven, immediately applied, synchronized across open tabs where supported
LoggingBrowser console only, structured, configurable verbosity and formatter
ErrorsInline indication plus a collapsible unresolved-issues panel
StorageDexie/IndexedDB by default, with alternative modes and safe failure handling
ArchitectureFeature-oriented TypeScript, Bun, vendored runtime libraries, small background-worker dispatcher
DocumentationSource-level explanations, examples, module contracts, and dedicated language/parser references

These defaults are implementation decisions rather than new DSL features.

One important correction to our earlier reference package

Some of the initial reference documentation describes behaviors we subsequently rejected.

The final specification must explicitly supersede those proposals:

  • No freely editable prose around placeholders.

  • No sharing of answers between template versions or documents.

  • No historical revision system.

  • No manually saved historical draft copies.

  • No general-purpose rich-text editor.

  • No chat composer or conversation engine.

  • No procedural scripting language for templates.

The reference package remains useful for its source code, UX patterns, diagrams, and vendor libraries. However, the new specification will be the authoritative source of product requirements wherever the older material disagrees.

How I would organize the specification

I recommend one primary specification, supported by smaller technical references that follow the feature structure.

DocumentResponsibility
SPEC.mdAuthoritative product behavior, interaction rules, examples, acceptance criteria
docs/language.mdTemplate syntax, required/optional placeholders, multiline input, examples
docs/architecture.mdFeature boundaries, Web Components, worker routing, message contracts
docs/storage.mdDocument lifecycle, Dexie schema, autosave, recovery, settings persistence
docs/ui-ux.mdLayout, guidance panel, keyboard behavior, responsiveness, visual design
docs/development.mdBun commands, TypeScript conventions, vendoring, testing, documentation expectations

For implementation, each feature should also have concise documentation close to its code. The parser and renderer should include actual valid and invalid language examples. Tests should illustrate important rules rather than merely test internal functions.

The main specification will favor concrete workflows, decision tables, short algorithms, and acceptance scenarios over lengthy theoretical explanations.

For example, a full scenario will cover creating a document, selecting a template, entering answers, navigating between placeholders, autosaving, switching versions, reopening the application, and copying the result. That single scenario can establish several behaviors precisely without repeating them across numerous sections.

The requirements interview is complete. No additional product decisions are needed before drafting. Anything genuinely ambiguous that emerges during implementation can be resolved against the established principles: minimize complexity, preserve user work, keep document versions independent, and make the editing experience predictable.

We can now proceed directly to the comprehensive specification without another question round.

File 1: .specs/suggestions001-01-url-main-specification.md

Guided Document Editor — Main Application Specification

Created: 2026-10-25 Specification: suggestions001-01 Status: Proposed implementation specification Application type: Static browser application Primary technologies: TypeScript, Bun, HTML, CSS, Web Components

A00. Purpose

Build a browser application for creating short documents and reusable text from guided templates.

The application displays the complete template text, with editable placeholders embedded in the sentence or document. Users fill those placeholders in any order, receive contextual hints and examples, and immediately see the generated result.

The experience should resemble a capable, lightweight notepad combined with a document history interface.

It is not a conversational AI application, a conventional form wizard, or a general-purpose rich-text editor.

The primary interaction is:

  1. Create a document.

  2. Choose a template and, where applicable, a writing style.

  3. See the complete text immediately.

  4. Select placeholders and enter answers.

  5. Review the generated result.

  6. Copy or export the completed text.

  7. Reopen saved documents later.

There is no chat composer, conversation engine, or sequential question-answer workflow.

The visual organization draws inspiration from the supplied SovietRDTL chat application. The TypeScript, settings, persistence, vendoring, and component architecture draw inspiration from the supplied browser-extension source archive.

The application must remain simpler than either reference project.

A01. Terminology

Use the following terms consistently in implementation, documentation, and UI text.

TermDefinition
TemplateA reusable source definition containing fixed text, placeholders, and guidance.
Template versionA selectable text variation declared within a template, such as Purpose + function.
DocumentAn independent instance created using a particular template version, with its own answers and saved state.
PlaceholderA named location within a template that accepts a user-provided value.
AnswerThe value associated with a placeholder in one document.
Working draftThe latest modified, potentially persisted state of a document.
Temporary documentAn opened document that has not received meaningful user input and has not been persisted.
Template-authoring draftAn editable source definition stored locally until the author downloads its source file.
Generated resultRead-only text produced by substituting the document's answers into the template.
Document historyThe sidebar collection of saved documents, not a history of revisions.

A template version is not a historical revision of a document.

Different template versions produce independent documents and do not share answers.

A02. Main application layout

The default desktop interface has three functional areas.

RegionResponsibility
Left sidebarDocument history, New Document, Template Authoring, Settings.
Central workspaceTemplate selection, guided text, placeholder editing context, generated result, copying.
Right sidebarActive-placeholder guidance and unresolved validation or persistence errors.

The central workspace is the main reading surface. It should resemble a document presented within a modern dark-themed chat interface, without speech bubbles, speaker roles, or a message composer.

A narrow, readable content column is preferred over a full-width form.

The interface supports scrolling through the document. Guidance stays accessible without covering the text currently being edited.

The left sidebar behaves like vertical document tabs: selecting an item restores its document.

On smaller screens, navigation and guidance adapt to the available space.

See .specs/suggestions001-01-url-ui-ux.md.

A03. Creating a document

The primary action is New Document.

Opening it displays the template selection interface within the central workspace.

For example, the built-in Project Description template offers:

Writing stylePattern
Purpose + functionA [tool] that [action] to [benefit].
Problem + solutionHelps [audience] [problem] by [solution].
Input + processing + outputTakes [input], [processing], and produces [result].
Features + technologyA [tool] built with [technology], featuring [feature 1] and [feature 2].
Structured project profileA [tool] for [audience], providing [workflow] through [detail].

Choosing a style immediately displays its complete text.

A selection alone does not create a persistent document.

The user can inspect different styles, read their hints, and leave without creating anything in storage.

A document becomes save-worthy when the user enters or meaningfully modifies content. An explicit user-authored document title also counts as meaningful input.

A04. Guided editing

The generated document's fixed prose cannot be edited in the document workspace.

Suppose the selected template contains:

Plain textTakes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}.

Initially, the user sees:

Plain textTakes [input], [processing], and produces [result].

The placeholder locations are visually distinguishable.

Selecting $input opens contextual guidance:

Plain textWhat does the user provide? Hint: Describe the original data or information. Example: pasted JSON

The user types:

Plain textpasted JSON

The document immediately becomes:

Plain textTakes pasted JSON, [processing], and produces [result].

The user may fill $result before $processing. There is no enforced question order.

After all fields are completed:

Plain textTakes pasted JSON, checks it for syntax errors, and produces a readable formatted version.

Only placeholder values are editable.

To change fixed wording, placeholder names, or questions, the user must enter the separate Template Authoring workspace.

Placeholder guidance is optional metadata. A placeholder without a declared question remains editable using an automatically generated label.

A05. Required and optional placeholders

Placeholders are required by default.

A template author can explicitly mark a field optional:

Plain text$notes OPTIONAL: Additional notes?

The document may display an incomplete generated result, but copying or exporting is disabled while any required placeholder remains unresolved.

Required-field errors appear near their placeholder and in the unresolved-issues panel.

Optional empty placeholders render as empty text. The renderer does not automatically rewrite nearby words or punctuation.

A template author must write text that remains appropriate when optional values are absent.

A required answer containing only whitespace is treated as unresolved.

A06. Template versions and document independence

Template versions are alternative writing structures, not revisions of the same document.

Consider:

Plain textVERSION: Input + processing + output TEXT: Takes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}. VERSION: Purpose + function TEXT: A {{$tool}} that {{$action}} to {{$benefit}}.

The user completes the first version.

They then choose Purpose + function.

The new version starts with empty answers. It does not inherit values from the previous document, even if the two versions happen to use the same placeholder names.

If modified, it becomes a separately saved document.

The sidebar might contain:

Plain textDocuments 2026-10-25 14:05 — Input + processing + output 2026-10-25 14:18 — Purpose + function

Switching back restores the first document's original answers.

If the user revisits a version already created through that document's version-selection flow, restore its associated document rather than silently create a duplicate.

Independently choosing New Document creates a new instance, even when using the same template version.

The implementation may retain a lightweight relationship between related documents to support version switching. This relationship must never cause answers to be shared.

A07. Temporary documents and working drafts

A newly opened, untouched template is temporary.

The user may:

  • Select a template.

  • Switch between styles.

  • Focus placeholders.

  • Read guidance.

  • Preview the resulting text.

None of these actions alone creates a persisted document.

Entering an answer creates a modified working state.

Persistence happens automatically, using the configured autosave behavior.

If the user enters text and then removes it, returning the document to its original untouched state, the application should avoid retaining an empty document.

Each document has only one current working draft.

There is no revision history, historical snapshot browser, or manual Save Draft Copy workflow.

The application must never create unlimited duplicate drafts from ordinary typing.

A08. Autosave

The default autosave interval is 60 seconds.

Users may change it in Settings.

Changes are reflected immediately in the interface, whether or not they have been persisted.

The application attempts to save outstanding changes when:

EventBehavior
Autosave interval elapsedSave dirty documents.
User changes documentsSave pending changes from the previous document.
User switches template versionsSave pending changes from the previous version's document.
Application becomes hiddenAttempt to flush pending changes.
Save failsPreserve in-memory content and display a recoverable error.

An application close or crash may interrupt asynchronous storage operations. The UI must not promise that every last keystroke is crash-proof.

The save-state indicator distinguishes Unsaved changes, Saving…, Saved, and Save failed.

See .specs/suggestions001-01-url-storage.md.

A09. Result and copying

The generated result appears below the guided editing area, within the same central document.

The result is read-only and updates immediately.

The application supports:

OperationBehavior
Copy textCopy the completed result as text/plain.
Copy rich textCopy semantic HTML and plain-text representations together.
Export textDownload .txt.
Export MarkdownDownload .md.
Export HTMLDownload .html with semantic markup.

Rich copying does not imply rich-text authoring. The user edits plain-text placeholder values.

The exported result must not contain placeholder controls, hint text, error decorations, background images, or other application UI.

If required placeholders are unresolved, copy and result-export actions are unavailable with a visible explanation.

Template-source downloading is separate and may proceed even when the template source contains errors.

A10. Template Authoring

Template Authoring is a separate mini-application within the same website.

It allows users to:

  1. Create a new template definition.

  2. Open an existing bundled definition.

  3. Edit source text, placeholders, questions, hints, examples, and versions.

  4. Receive live syntax diagnostics.

  5. Preview valid template behavior.

  6. Autosave the unfinished source in IndexedDB.

  7. Download the source as a .gtl file.

The exported file becomes part of the application's built-in template collection only when a developer adds or replaces it in the project and registers it in the template manifest.

Editing a built-in template in the browser does not directly change deployed project files.

Template-authoring drafts are independent from generated-document drafts.

Invalid template source may still be downloaded. Validation informs the author but never prevents source recovery.

See .specs/suggestions001-01-url-language.md.

A11. Settings

Settings appear on a separate application page or workspace.

They are generated from a declarative settings schema, not implemented individually as unrelated forms.

The initial categories are:

CategorySettings
AppearanceBackground, theme options, visual density where supported.
EditingPlaceholder guidance visibility and keyboard behavior help.
StorageStorage mode and autosave interval.
DiagnosticsConsole log verbosity and formatter.

Each setting includes an identifier, title, description, default, validation rule, and appropriate UI control.

Changes take effect immediately and propagate to other open application tabs where supported.

The settings implementation should follow the source patterns in the browser-extension reference package.

A12. Representative acceptance scenario

Scenario: Create and recover a project description

Given a new browser session with IndexedDB persistence enabled:

  1. The user selects New Document.

  2. They select Input + processing + output.

  3. No saved history item is created yet.

  4. They click $processing.

  5. The guidance panel displays its question, hint, and example.

  6. They enter checks it for syntax errors.

  7. The document becomes modified and eligible for persistence.

  8. They enter the remaining answers.

  9. Autosave persists the current working state.

  10. They close the application and reopen it.

  11. The document appears in the history sidebar with its answers restored.

  12. They select Purpose + function.

  13. That version starts with empty answers.

  14. They return to the original document.

  15. Its previous answers are still present.

  16. They copy its completed result.

Expected outcomes: no answers are shared between separate documents, no historical revision is created, no empty preview is persisted, and the copy operation contains only the generated text.

A13. Explicit exclusions

The first implementation must not include:

  • AI chat or conversation generation.

  • A general-purpose rich-text editor.

  • Free editing of fixed template prose in generated documents.

  • Historical document revisions.

  • Automatic answer sharing between documents.

  • Complex conditional scripting or procedural template execution.

  • Remote accounts, cloud synchronization, or server-side document storage.

  • Remote telemetry.

  • A marketplace for templates.

  • Dynamic runtime installation of application feature plugins.

The architecture should allow sensible later extensions, but these excluded features must not be implemented speculatively.

A14. Definition of complete

The application is ready for initial release when a user can create, edit, save, reopen, delete, copy, and export guided documents; author and download templates; configure settings; and recover gracefully from expected failures.

Each feature must have automated tests for its important behavior, meaningful errors, and technical documentation maintained during implementation.

The application must build using Bun and deploy as static files without a runtime npm dependency installation.

File 2: .specs/suggestions001-01-url-language.md

Guided Template Language — Syntax and Processing Specification

Created: 2026-10-25 Specification: suggestions001-01 Status: Proposed language contract Related: Main specification, architecture, UI/UX

L00. Purpose and scope

The Guided Template Language (GTL) describes reusable text with named placeholders and optional guidance.

It is a document definition format, not a programming language.

A template author should be able to express common writing structures without learning flow control, variables with computed expressions, functions, or a questionnaire DSL.

The parser needs to understand only:

  • Template and version names.

  • Literal text.

  • Named placeholders.

  • Questions, hints, and examples.

  • Required versus optional fields.

  • Single-line, multiline, and simple constrained-choice input.

The application—not the template—decides how to display editors, navigate between fields, save drafts, and export results.

L01. Basic template

Plain textTEMPLATE: Input + processing + output TEXT: Takes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}. $input: What does the user provide? HINT: Describe the original data or information. EXAMPLE: pasted JSON $processing: What happens to the input? HINT: Describe the main operation. EXAMPLE: checks it for syntax errors $result: What does the application produce? HINT: Describe the final output. EXAMPLE: a readable formatted version

The template contains literal text and three placeholders.

Each {{$name}} occurrence references a value provided by the user.

The corresponding $name: declaration describes that value.

If $processing is assigned "checks it for syntax errors", every {{$processing}} occurrence within the same document displays that answer.

Answers never propagate into other documents.

L02. File format and lexical conventions

Templates use UTF-8 text files with the .gtl extension.

ElementRule
Directive namesCase-sensitive uppercase keywords.
Placeholder references{{$name}}
Placeholder declarations$name: Question
Optional declarations$name OPTIONAL: Question
IdentifiersASCII letter followed by letters, digits, underscores, or hyphens.
Identifier caseCase-sensitive.
Line endingsCRLF and LF accepted; normalized internally to LF.
Text contentLiteral; preserve meaningful whitespace and line breaks.
IndentationUsed to distinguish block content from directives.
CommentsNot included in the initial syntax.

Examples of valid identifiers:

Plain text$input $feature-one $technology $project_type

The following refer to different placeholders:

Plain text{{$input}} {{$Input}}

Authors should prefer descriptive, stable identifiers.

Unknown directives are errors rather than silently ignored text.

L03. Literal text and line breaks

TEXT: introduces a block of literal text.

The parser removes the common structural indentation from the block. It does not wrap lines, alter punctuation, or automatically reformat sentences.

Example:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Email introduction TEXT: Hello {{$name}}, Thank you for contacting us about {{$topic}}. Regards, {{$sender}}

Rendered result:

Plain textHello Alex, Thank you for contacting us about the release. Regards, Morgan

Blank lines inside the text block are significant.

A leading or trailing blank line introduced solely by the block layout may be removed. Internal blank lines and relative indentation must be preserved.

Whitespace normalization must be documented and covered by parser tests.

The language does not automatically correct grammar. For example, it will not change "A application" to "An application".

L04. Required and optional placeholders

Every placeholder is required unless its declaration specifies OPTIONAL.

Plain text$tool: What kind of application is it? EXAMPLE: browser-based utility $notes OPTIONAL: Additional details? EXAMPLE: optimized for offline use

Rules:

Field stateRequiredOptional
No answerValidation errorValid
Empty stringValidation errorValid
Whitespace onlyValidation errorValid
Nonempty answerValidValid

An undeclared placeholder is treated as required and is given a generated question based on its identifier. Template Authoring may display a warning recommending an explicit declaration.

An optional unanswered placeholder substitutes an empty string.

The renderer must not automatically remove commas, spaces, conjunctions, or surrounding lines to compensate for absent optional answers.

For that reason, optional placeholders should normally occupy locations where an empty substitution remains acceptable.

L05. Guidance metadata

Supported guidance directives:

Plain textHINT: Describe the expected answer. EXAMPLE: An example answer.

These belong to the immediately preceding placeholder declaration.

A placeholder can omit either directive.

For the initial language, each placeholder has at most one question and one hint, but may have multiple examples by repeating EXAMPLE:.

Example:

Plain text$audience: Who will use this tool? HINT: Name the audience, not the application. EXAMPLE: developers EXAMPLE: technical writers EXAMPLE: project managers

Examples are guidance only. They do not automatically become submitted answers or default values.

The UI may provide Copy Example and Use Example actions.

L06. Multiline input

A placeholder may request a multiline input:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Blank note TEXT: {{$body}} $body: Write your note. INPUT: multiline

Without an explicit input type, the field is a single-line text input.

Multiline values preserve entered line breaks.

For example, $body may contain:

Plain textPrepare the project description. Check keyboard navigation. Review the background images.

The generated result contains the same three paragraphs.

The application's keyboard controller must preserve native multiline cursor movement.

L07. Constrained choices

Some placeholders should accept a selection rather than arbitrary text.

Keep this facility small. It must not become a separate conditional-choice language.

Proposed syntax:

Plain text$tone: Which tone should the message use? OPTION concise: Concise OPTION friendly: Friendly OPTION formal: Formal

A field with OPTION declarations displays a suitable choice control.

The stored answer is the option key (concise, friendly, or formal). Its interpolated text is the display label.

No choice is automatically selected merely because it appears first.

For example, selecting friendly inserts:

Plain textFriendly

There are no conditional branches depending on selected choices in the initial language.

A field cannot combine INPUT: multiline with constrained choices.

This small extension covers restricted-answer scenarios without bringing back CHOOSE, WHEN, or other procedural constructs from the abandoned DSL design.

L08. Template versions

A source file can define multiple alternatives:

Plain textTEMPLATE: Project description VERSION: Input + processing + output TEXT: Takes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}. VERSION: Purpose + function TEXT: A {{$tool}} that {{$action}} to {{$benefit}}. $input: What goes in? EXAMPLE: pasted JSON $processing: What happens to it? EXAMPLE: checks it for errors $result: What is produced? EXAMPLE: formatted JSON $tool: What kind of tool is it? EXAMPLE: browser-based utility $action: What does it do? EXAMPLE: formats JSON $benefit: Why is it useful? EXAMPLE: improve readability

The template selector displays the available versions.

Each chosen version creates or reopens an independent document, with isolated answers and saved state.

Placeholder metadata may be shared at the source-definition level, but values are never shared between documents.

A template must use either one top-level TEXT: block or one or more VERSION: blocks. Mixing both forms in the same file is invalid.

Version names must be unique within a template.

L09. Template examples

The first application should ship at least six useful templates or writing styles.

Purpose + function

Plain textTEMPLATE: Purpose + function TEXT: A {{$tool}} that {{$action}} to {{$benefit}}. $tool: What kind of project is it? EXAMPLE: browser-based tool $action: What does it do? EXAMPLE: formats and validates JSON $benefit: Why is it useful? EXAMPLE: make structured data easier to inspect

Problem + solution

Plain textTEMPLATE: Problem + solution TEXT: Helps {{$audience}} {{$problem}} by {{$solution}}. $audience: Who needs this? EXAMPLE: developers $problem: What problem are they solving? EXAMPLE: inspect messy JSON $solution: How does the tool help? EXAMPLE: validating its syntax and formatting the result

Input + processing + output

Plain textTEMPLATE: Input + processing + output TEXT: Takes {{$input}}, {{$processing}}, and produces {{$result}}. $input: What does the user provide? EXAMPLE: pasted JSON $processing: What happens to the input? EXAMPLE: checks it for syntax errors $result: What does the application produce? EXAMPLE: readable formatted JSON

Features + technology

Plain textTEMPLATE: Features + technology TEXT: A {{$tool}} built with {{$technology}}, featuring {{$feature-one}} and {{$feature-two}}. $tool: What kind of application is it? EXAMPLE: browser-based JSON utility $technology: What technology does it use? EXAMPLE: vanilla JavaScript $feature-one: What is its primary feature? EXAMPLE: syntax validation $feature-two: What is another feature? EXAMPLE: readable formatting

Structured project profile

Plain textTEMPLATE: Structured project profile TEXT: A {{$tool}} for {{$audience}}, providing {{$workflow}} through {{$detail}}. $tool: What is it? EXAMPLE: browser-based utility $audience: Who benefits? EXAMPLE: developers inspecting JSON data $workflow: What is the primary workflow? EXAMPLE: syntax validation and formatting $detail: What distinguishes it? EXAMPLE: a lightweight interface

Blank note

Plain textTEMPLATE: Blank note TEXT: {{$body}} $body: Write your note. INPUT: multiline

The project-description styles may be distributed as separate files or combined into one versioned template. Both are valid authoring approaches.

L10. Parsing and validation

The parser should produce a small structured representation containing:

Plain textTemplate name versions[] name text parts[] placeholders[] name question required input kind hint examples[] options[] diagnostics[]

The parser must not directly construct UI elements or access storage.

It returns structured data and diagnostics, including source locations.

Examples of diagnostics:

Source problemDiagnostic
Missing TEMPLATE:Template name required
TEXT: without contentText block is empty
Duplicate placeholder declarationsPlaceholder declared more than once
Duplicate version namesVersion name must be unique
Malformed {{$input}Unclosed placeholder reference
Unsupported directiveUnknown directive
Invalid option declarationExpected option key and label
Unsupported input typeSupported type is multiline
Declared but unused placeholderInformational warning

Diagnostics should include line, column when determinable, severity, code, and explanation.

Template Authoring runs validation during editing and displays errors nonintrusively.

Invalid source can be saved and downloaded, but it cannot be treated as a valid executable template.

L11. Rendering

Rendering is a pure operation over a parsed template version and a document's answers.

Conceptually:

Plain textrender(version, answers): result = empty output for each part in version.text: if part is literal text: append part unchanged if part is placeholder: if a valid answer exists: append its displayed value else if optional: append empty text else: record unresolved required field append a visible preview marker return: rendered text unresolved required fields canCopy = no unresolved required fields

The renderer must not modify the input template or answer map.

Do not pass generated HTML through raw string interpolation into innerHTML.

Plain-text generation must be separate from UI decoration.

A valid result can then be converted into semantic HTML for rich clipboard operations.

L12. Language exclusions

The initial language deliberately excludes:

Excluded featureReason
WHEN, ELSE, conditional expressionsNot needed for guided text replacement
Loops and iterationAdds scripting complexity
Functions and calculated variablesNot needed
Arbitrary JavaScript expressionsSecurity and predictability
Remote includes or importsStatic manifest is sufficient
Template-defined UI layoutApplication owns presentation
Filesystem writes or side effectsTemplates are data
Dynamic plugin executionOutside scope

Any future syntax extension requires a real authoring example that cannot be expressed reasonably with the existing language.

L13. Documentation and tests

The parser, validator, and renderer should each have clear source-level documentation.

The language reference is the authority on supported syntax. Parser implementation comments should explain mechanisms and invariants rather than duplicate this entire document.

Tests must cover all published examples, repeated placeholders, multiline values, optional fields, malformed references, duplicate declarations, version isolation, line-ending normalization, and incomplete-result validation.

A new syntax feature is incomplete until its examples, diagnostics, and tests are updated.

File 3: .specs/suggestions001-01-url-architecture.md

Guided Document Editor — Technical Architecture Specification

Created: 2026-10-25 Specification: suggestions001-01 Status: Proposed implementation architecture Reference package: gtl-ui-architecture-reference-kit.zip

H00. Architectural principles

Implement a static TypeScript application with feature-oriented modules and browser-native APIs.

The main principles are:

  1. Organize substantial functionality by domain feature, not exclusively by technical layer.

  2. Keep UI, domain behavior, and persistence responsibilities separate.

  3. Let features own their implementation, including optional UI, worker, and repository components.

  4. Keep the worker's entry point small and focused on routing.

  5. Use native Web Components and selectively encapsulate them with Shadow DOM.

  6. Commit runtime libraries as vendored source or distributions.

  7. Keep runtime behavior independent of npm-installed packages.

  8. Prefer explicit imports, interfaces, and simple event flows over a framework or complex plugin infrastructure.

The architecture should be understandable by following one feature folder at a time.

H01. Reference projects

Two existing projects provide implementation references.

SovietRDTL UI

Archive: sovet-rdtl-0.13.1-source(1).zip

Reference package location:

Plain textreference/sovet-rdtl/sovet-rdtl/

Useful files:

FileReference purpose
src/ui/components/sov-app.jsApplication orchestration
src/ui/components/sov-sidebar.jsVertical history and selection
src/ui/components/sov-message.jsDocument-like presentation
src/ui/components/sov-origin-panel.jsContextual information and focus restoration
src/ui/components/sov-backdrop.jsBackground image handling
src/ui/dom.jsEvent and DOM utilities
src/app/chat-service.jsState updates and subscriptions
styles.cssDark design system and responsive layout

These are behavioral and visual references.

The source application uses Custom Elements with light DOM. Shadow DOM is an architectural choice for this new project, not an existing feature of those components.

Do not copy its chat engine, conversational behavior, or auto-scroll-to-latest-message interaction.

Browser-extension architecture

Archive: browser-extension-src.zip

Reference package location:

Plain textreference/browser-extension-src/

Important files:

FileReference purpose
src/background/service_worker.tsWorker setup, database initialization, message dispatch
src/background/tab_trail_service.tsService/repository separation and memory-backed testing
src/content/runtime_messaging.tsTyped message envelopes
src/settings/settings_runtime.tsDeclarative settings schema
src/settings/settings.tsSettings UI behavior
src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/components/field.tsShadow DOM fields, labels, help and errors
src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/core/styles.tsShared component styles
src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/src/core/define.tsCustom Element registration
src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/tsconfig.jsonTypeScript compiler configuration
src/vendor/retroos-ui-v001/scripts/build.mtsBun build conventions

The original extension runs in a Chrome extension environment and uses a Manifest V3 background service worker.

Our application is a standard website.

Therefore, Chrome-specific APIs must be replaced with normal browser equivalents.

H02. Execution environments

Use two primary execution environments.

EnvironmentResponsibilities
Browser main threadWeb Components, user interaction, immediate document state, focus management, clipboard, settings UI
Dedicated Web WorkerIndexedDB access, background persistence, feature service operations, selected processing tasks

A future website Service Worker may provide offline asset caching, but it is not required for the initial data architecture.

The Dedicated Worker is not a permanent server. Each application instance can create its own worker, and worker state must not be treated as durable storage.

The editor must not wait for worker round trips before reflecting user input.

H03. Feature-oriented project structure

Recommended initial structure:

Plain textindex.html package.json tsconfig.json scripts/ build.ts generate-template-manifest.ts src/ app/ main.ts app-shell.ts app-state.ts features/ documents/ model.ts service.ts protocol.ts ui/ document-workspace.ts document-list.ts placeholder-editor.ts generated-result.ts worker/ handler.ts storage/ repository.ts dexie-repository.ts templates/ model.ts parser.ts validator.ts renderer.ts loader.ts manifest.ts ui/ template-selector.ts guidance-panel.ts worker/ handler.ts template-authoring/ model.ts service.ts ui/ source-editor.ts source-diagnostics.ts preview.ts storage/ authoring-repository.ts settings/ schema.ts defaults.ts service.ts ui/ settings-page.ts setting-field.ts export/ service.ts ui/ export-menu.ts platform/ messaging/ protocol.ts worker-client.ts worker-router.ts storage/ database.ts storage-coordinator.ts memory-storage.ts local-storage.ts diagnostics/ logger.ts console-formatters.ts clipboard/ clipboard.ts ui/ component-base.ts theme.ts styles.ts worker/ main.ts vendor/ dexie/ jszip/ markdown-it/ turndown/ types/ vendor.d.ts templates/ manifest.json project-descriptions/ notes/ tests/

This is a suggested starting structure, not a mandatory folder for every tiny function.

Features should be sufficiently substantial to justify a dedicated boundary. Small utilities belong in their owning feature or a narrowly scoped shared module.

Do not create a worker/ or storage/ folder within a feature unless that feature actually needs it.

H04. Feature responsibilities

FeatureOwnsDoes not own
DocumentsDocument identity, answers, temporary state, navigation, save requestsTemplate grammar
TemplatesDefinitions, parsing, validation, rendering, built-in loadingSaved document persistence
Template AuthoringEditable template source, diagnostics, preview, source downloadAutomatic deployment of built-in templates
SettingsSchema, effective values, persistence and live notificationsIndividual feature business logic
ExportPlain-text, HTML and Markdown serialization, browser downloadDocument mutation
Platform StorageIndexedDB initialization, adapter selection, shared transactionsDomain-specific rules
Platform MessagingRequest routing and correlationFeature operations
Platform DiagnosticsLogger, formatting, severity filteringUser document content

Document history is implemented as the document collection and sidebar navigation, not as a separate historical-revisions engine.

A dedicated History feature is unnecessary unless future behavior gives history responsibilities beyond listing and locating documents.

H05. Worker routing

The worker entry point should initialize shared services and register feature handlers.

It must not accumulate document-specific, template-specific, and settings-specific decision trees.

Conceptual registration:

TypeScript
const handlers = new Map([
  ["documents", documentHandler],
  ["templates", templateHandler]
]);

A request contains a scope and operation type.

Example:

TypeScript
{
  scope: "documents",
  type: "save",
  requestId: "request-123",
  payload: {
    document: documentRecord,
    expectedRevision: 4
  }
}

The router identifies the handler and delegates the request.

Each feature validates its incoming payload before using it.

Unknown scopes and operations produce structured errors.

The worker router must never infer success from a message being dispatched. Success requires an actual response from the feature handler.

Protocol

Adapt the conventions from:

Plain textsrc/content/runtime_messaging.ts

The new browser protocol includes:

TypeScript
type Request = {
  protocolVersion: number;
  scope: string;
  type: string;
  requestId: string;
  payload: unknown;
};

type Response = {
  protocolVersion: number;
  requestId: string;
  ok: boolean;
  result?: unknown;
  error?: {
    code: string;
    message: string;
    details?: unknown;
  };
};

Contracts should be strongly typed by operation at the client and handler boundaries, even though the transport envelope uses unknown.

Required behavior:

SituationBehavior
Valid requestDispatch to owning feature
Unknown scopeReturn UNKNOWN_SCOPE
Unknown operationReturn UNKNOWN_OPERATION
Invalid payloadReturn INVALID_REQUEST
Handler failureReturn structured error; do not crash router
Worker timeoutReject pending client request with actionable error
Worker restartsReinitialize services; reopen persistent storage
Late responseMatch using request ID; ignore responses for expired requests

Errors must be handled locally by the caller. The UI determines how to explain them to the user.

H06. Repository boundaries

Domain code depends on repository contracts, not directly on Dexie.

Example:

TypeScript
